madam president, the european arrest warrant (eaw) is a valuable cross-border tool in the fight against terrorism and serious crime. the speed of extradition now, compared to the cumbersome processes of the past, is greatly improved. however, many will remember a time when this house repeatedly expressed concern regarding its use for petty crimes, or the system errors which destroyed the lives of unlucky individuals. this parliament pushed therefore for actions and consequently its implementation has greatly been improved. yet we were recently reminded of the pitfalls when the spanish government used the eaw for its own political agenda. it reminded us that instruments can be used, abused and stretched if there isn’t careful monitoring and protection in place. rather than seeking to create new powers and widening the scope of the eaw, we must first build back trust between the member states and increase transparency and judicial independence. this is the only way we can help.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, i would of course like to thank the court of auditors for its work. its independence and rigour make it the best ally of the european citizens and therefore of our parliament, the european parliament. and this year, for the first time, we had a report dedicated to performance. this was a priority for my renew europe group. it is one thing to have accounts free of irregularities, or even errors, but it is also necessary that the funds are effectively invested in economically promising projects or policies with significant leverage effects for the development of our values. every euro spent must be spent efficiently. commissioner, there are some remarks which would merit more attention from the commission, given the new budgetary programming and the next generation eu with 750 billion. in particular, i remain concerned about the very low absorption capacity of many states. only 9 out of 27 states have a better absorption capacity than in 2012. the european average is barely 40%. how do we get our economy back on track with numbers that low? what is the point of providing funds if governments are not able to use the money? moreover, and not surprisingly, errors are mainly found in the areas of high-risk expenditure, such as rural development, innovation, cohesion. the solutions to reduce these errors are known: simplification of administrative rules, digitalisation of procedures, technical assistance and training for administrative staff and applicants. the union must also make progress in this area.

member of the commission. madam president, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. thank you for this good, engaged discussion. a few remarks, if i may. first of all, if you really look at the evolution of error rates over the last decade, you can definitely see a significant decline. when i left the position of regional commissioner in 2014, the error rate was around 5 to 6%, and i didn't think it was possible to go much lower than that. but in the meantime, that's the case. number one. number two: we should also look at the price in the true sense of the word. and don't get me wrong, i am for any form of control and effective control. but we must also see - and there is a study that goes back several years - that the discharge process alone requires 2000 full-time equivalents throughout europe. this means that, in plain language, 2 000 people are working all year round to relieve the burden of the european union in the member states at european level. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. i think we're on the right track. secondly, they have repeatedly complained about the high backlog of payments, which is approximately in the order of eur 300 million. i'm sorry about that too. there's no misunderstanding. but you also need to see what's causing it. the reason is essentially that a majority here in the house, together with the member states, believe that, particularly in the case of cohesion payments, there must be the possibility of the so-called n+3, i.e. the possibility of completion and payment up to three years after the approval of a project. i remember when i started ten years ago, it was n+2, and we still made it. and we actually had a higher absorption rate because of the pressure. that is, my suggestion is, as always, that you go back to n+2, ideally n+1, and then you definitely have a smaller number of outstanding liabilities. the third and final point is the staffing. i understand all of this. we have had the same discussion in the course of the mff. as you know, we have not received any significant allocations in terms of additional staff. but i also have to say, we all need to be creative here. because, yes, we have almost double the normal budget. but this is a temporary matter - at least from today's perspective. so when we're dealing with how we deal with this problem here - on the implementation side, but also on the control side - we also have to be clear that this is a temporary, a temporary matter and we can't build up a staff here that we may not need later on. so, what we need here is not just a certain flexibility, but also a certain creativity. once again, thank you for your dedicated contribution to maintaining the high level of control functions within the european union.
president of the court of auditors. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to thank you for your contributions and for the interesting discussion. i would also like to make a few comments on what has been said. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the fact is that in 2019, even in the high-risk areas, more spending took place than in previous years. i would therefore like to say that the development towards this audit opinion, if it is realistic, was a predictable development if one looks at the situation in these budgetary sectors in recent years. that's how i want you to understand it. i can only emphatically confirm what the commissioner has just said: if we look at the development of the last ten to twenty years, we have made not just one, but a whole series of huge steps forward. the fact that the results vary in one direction or another is perfectly normal, especially since we are also working with statistical methods. however, if we are to act on our own understanding, we must of course put the facts on the table and also say what is and what we have found. the consequences must then be drawn by the executive and the legislature, as far as they deem it necessary. that's not our job. on the subject of performance reporting, i would like to make a very basic point that performance and compliance are two sides of the same coin. mrs hohlmeier rightly pointed out - and others have also done so - that it is of no use at all if we have perfect compliance, for example, if the specific tender for the construction of a bridge or an airport terminal or something else has gone perfectly and there is no legal error in it, but then nobody or hardly anyone uses that airport terminal or the bridge. that means, of course, that the question of meaningfulness must also be examined. this is what, of course, needs to be checked in a performance review in terms of performance, performance capability. i also believe that this is of particular interest to members of parliament, to the executive, but also, of course, to the citizens, whether or not these funds have been spent in a meaningful way on certain objects and products, that is, whether or not they have been effective. i think that's very important. this is why the court has also considerably increased its performance work in the past. we shall be discussing this report this week at the court of auditors, and we shall be discussing how to continue this report. as i said, this was a pilot project and of course lessons must be learned from the pilot project or there may be some adjustments here or there depending on the course of the discussion this week, but i think that in principle - if i may express my personal opinion here - this was a step in the right direction and i certainly think that this makes sense in principle, although of course we have to talk about details and implementation in the individual use of funds. mr kuhs is, of course, quite right: financial control must be effective, that is not the issue. i also believe that we as the european court of auditors feel well taken care of in this house and have always found the appropriate support here. as i said, we will certainly be able to contact you with regard to the additional staff and expertise needed in the preparation of the next budgets, while being fully aware of what the commissioner has also mentioned here, namely that the change in budgetary structures is - yes, perhaps it is an understatement - seasonal, but it is still a matter of temporary expenditure. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that is logical and clear and does not need further discussion. but of course we must also be able to cope with the increased effort in a reasonable way. the issue of the reliability of the information we have has also been raised. so if you read this report, mr pieper, you will see that we have made a very clear distinction between where we can use data supplied to us by member states or by the commission and where we cannot. we only take data that we're sure is of the same quality as if it had been collected by us, and we test that very carefully and very sustainably. this has been one of the main focuses of our activities in the context of the annual report, particularly in recent years. this is partly why we have had a greater use of resources in the annual report than we might have originally hoped, because of course these special checks of the data material that has been made available to us have also caused additional effort. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information available to the public. the court will do all that is necessary to prepare for the digital age, which has already begun and which is sure to have an even greater significance. but i want to make it very clear that this is not something the court can do alone. we had a vision a while ago of ernst and young's way of doing audits on private companies: same database, full access to all data at any time, push of a button, complete overview. we can't do that. we cannot do that because, for example, in cohesion audits we have to deal with 170 different institutions, bodies, where we have to carry out audits, all of which have different databases, and that makes it impossible. but my dream would be - and this is probably only possible through a long-term change in the financial regulation, perhaps over ten years, because it also involves investment and costs, not only here but also in the member states in particular - to oblige the member states and the institutions in the member states which have to deal with the funds to provide compatible data in the long term through the financial regulation. i think that would be very desirable, and i think that would be an initiative for this election period, and that would also take us and our work very considerably forward and open up entirely new possibilities. i am not sure that i have covered all the issues, but i have already passed the time allotted to me. i would like to thank you again and wish you every success in the further discussions on the discharge reports.
in writing. for 2019, the european court of auditors presented for the first time, together with the report on the legality and regularity of the accounts, an assessment of the performance of eu spending programmes, going beyond the formal budgetary review, to examine the performance of the european union's policies and the achievement of their objectives, in an effective, efficient and cost-effective manner, the "3e". unfortunately, for 2019, the court of auditors has expressed a negative opinion on the use of eu funds: more than half of them were considered high risk, with an estimated error rate of 4.9%. the economic challenges we face and the health, social and economic crisis we are going through require the utmost attention to the fairness and good use of all eu spending programmes, starting with the recovery fund, which offers an unprecedented opportunity for european states to get the economy back on track. as vice-chair of the committee on budgetary control, i will work with the court of auditors and the european commission to ensure that eu funds are implemented in a timely and correct manner, in order to support member states and thus support citizens and businesses in their recovery from the current economic crisis.
deputising for the rapporteur. – madam president, thank you for giving me floor. unfortunately our rapporteur, mr morten løkkegaard, is not able to present his report – he is stuck in traffic. so therefore i will present the report on strengthening the single market: the future of free movement of services, on behalf of mr løkkegaard. this report is extremely relevant right now. the european economy is in a terrible crisis due to the coronavirus, which affects all countries. last year, the european economy contracted by 7.4%, and the unemployment is rising in all member states. we need to boost the economy and create jobs. one way to do this is to realise the huge potential in the single market for services. the european parliament research service estimated that the potential gain of completing the single market for services could be around eur 297 billion, or close to 2% of european eu gdp. the aim of the report is to realise this untapped potential – a potential that doesn’t require new laws or new recovery funds to be established. the three main proposals of the report are: firstly, we must make sure that unjustified barriers in member states are removed and that member states enforce the existing rules. secondly, we must provide legal clarity to businesses and consumers by supplying the necessary information. thirdly, we must provide transparent evaluation of member states’ performance. for example, the report suggests using scoreboards to rate member states’ implementation of the existing rules of the single market. mr løkkegaard has asked me to thank all the groups and the shadow rapporteurs who participated in reaching a compromise for this report. thank you, all of you, for your hard work and your valuable inputs to this proposal. this area has often been highly debated among the political groups; therefore it’s a victory to reach a consensus. the broad support for this report shows that parliament now has a majority behind several proposals to strengthen the single market on services. this is an important step in the realisation of the potential. we are facing an urgent need to create jobs and growth in europe, and strengthening the single market for services is one of the ways to do it. with a broad majority in parliament, it will be up to member states and the commission to follow the proposals in the report and to make use of the opportunity that lies at our feet. we hope that all of you will support the report. now let me add on my behalf, madam president. i would like to stress that the service sector accounts for around 70% of the european gdp and a similar proportion of its employment. a better-functioning single market for services is therefore a crucial necessity for a more competitive and innovative european economy. the economic fall-out from the coronavirus pandemic has only increased the urgency of unleashing the full potential of the service sector. we need greater clarity and transparency for businesses and consumers alike. we must remove unjustified barriers that deprive our citizens of jobs, our consumers of choices and our entrepreneurs of competitiveness. for too long, member states have hidden behind over-regulation of professions and administrative barriers to prevent one of the four freedoms. i was extremely disappointed with the decision to withdraw the services notification directive after the member states and the council blocked it. commissioner, i call on you to engage to find new solutions, new proposals, on how to ensure all four freedoms are fully implemented. you must have the courage to act. now it’s time to complete the single market for services: now or never.
member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to welcome this own-initiative report and thank the rapporteur and all the other colleagues concerned for the important work they have done. the commission would like to stress that we need a functioning and well-functioning single market for services now more than ever. that is the message that my colleague, commissioner breton, in charge of the internal market, would have liked to recall today and it is the same message that i want to share with you. i fully agree with the central message of this report on the need to deepen the single market for services to strengthen the economic recovery and growth of the european union. the covid-19 crisis has clearly shown us how fragile the smooth functioning of the single market can be. at the same time, she underlined how much the european economy depends on the single market and that when the single market fails, everyone pays the price: supply chains are disrupted, small and large businesses cannot source, deliver or sell related goods and services. the lesson is clear: the requirements of economies that rely more on services or manufacturing are the same: to be able to rely on the backbone of a fully operational and resilient single market. it is therefore time to put an end to the old-fashioned dichotomy between the so-called friends of industry and the friends of the single market. the crisis has hit a whole series of sectors in our single market and also in services. the latter is heavily dependent on the free movement of workers and service providers. they have faced closed borders or delays in crossing them. in order to restore and safeguard free movement, the commission has made considerable efforts to put in place rapid and effective solutions in this respect, as shown by the many guidelines and communications presented. it is clear that a more integrated, more competitive single market has never been more important for recovery and resilience. it is also an indispensable asset in mastering the dual transition to a greener and more digital economy. and, as the report points out, the services sector accounts for almost three quarters of the union's gdp and employment. services are one of the cornerstones of our major industrial ecosystems. services account for nine out of every ten new jobs. in addition, we are seeing an increasing servicing of products, and as the crisis has highlighted the essential nature of services for our industry and our economy, i can assure you that services will be at the heart of our work on ecosystems. but there is equally compelling evidence that the services market remains fragmented and characterised by weak intra-eu trade and investment. in this context, the own-initiative report rightly highlights the last obstacles to services that deprive citizens of jobs, consumers of choice and entrepreneurs of opportunities. it is our common responsibility to fight against unjustified restrictions on the way to a genuine single market for services. some key themes in the report, but first, as you have seen, we have decided to withdraw the proposals on notifications under the services directive and the electronic card from the commission's 2021 work programme. don't see it as a sign of abandoning our ambitions. on the contrary, while we regret that it has not been possible to pursue these initiatives with you, we will use all our resources to chart a new course. first, a few words about implementation and enforcement. very often, barriers to services are linked to insufficient or incorrect implementation of existing single market rules. you are right to put pressure on the commission and the member states. improving implementation can only be done in close cooperation between the european union and the national or even local level. our action plan on long-term compliance with the single market reflects this shared responsibility. in addition, the single market enforcement working group set up with member states plays a key role in addressing obstacles through better implementation and enforcement of the rules. secondly, i would like to stress the importance of prevention. it is often much easier and more effective to prevent barriers to the single market in services than to try to remove them afterwards. the importance of effective notification procedures and ex ante testing of the single market is undeniable when it comes to avoiding unjustified or disproportionate restrictions in the services sector. here, too, we are working on several fronts. on the one hand, we will focus on the strict application of the proportionality directive by the member states, and on the other hand, we will mobilise all our resources to improve the application of the notification procedure established under the services directive. thirdly, i would like to stress once again the importance of digitisation. this is a necessary precondition for the smooth functioning of the single market for services. the recently adopted digital services and digital markets bills, currently under consideration by the co-legislators, will modernise the eu regulatory framework and will be of key importance for the services sector of the single market. i am also pleased that the own-initiative report emphasises the role of the single digital gateway launched last december. it will gather information, provide businesses with much needed administrative simplification and eventually provide a one-stop shop. the one-stop shop and other assistance and problem-solving services will be fully integrated into the portal. the success of the portal will now depend largely on the political and operational support of the member states to put things into practice and we count on parliament to continue to advocate for this. finally, it is encouraging that the own-initiative report highlights the role of greater integration of services markets in achieving social and sustainability objectives. it goes without saying that services can and must contribute to increasing the sustainability and resilience of our industrial ecosystems while respecting the principles of the circular economy and the objectives of our green deal. it is also clear that the objectives of the single market and social and environmental policy can and must go hand in hand for genuinely sustainable development. in conclusion, much remains to be done and we must work together to strengthen the single market for services. your own-initiative report is a useful contribution to this work. in the spring, the commission will present a review of the industrial strategy of march 2020 to ensure that we take into account the lessons learned from the covid-19 crisis and the acceleration of transformation processes. the single market will be central to this. we will take stock of progress under the long-term action plan for enforcement. we will also present concrete ideas on how to deal with the crises in our single market and remove the remaining obstacles. this will form the basis for our successful and continuing work to ensure a genuine single market for services.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. – madam president, it’s too bad that the rapporteur couldn’t be here. i know traffic jams can be difficult and bad, but this text is worse. deze tekst is een aanval op werknemersrechten. het vrije verkeer van diensten in de europese unie vergemakkelijkt al te vaak sociale afbraak en dumping. zo kan een bedrijf uit één land diensten verrichten in een ander land, in de bouw of de transportsector, zonder werknemers dezelfde rechten te garanderen. ook wordt het gemeenten moeilijk gemaakt om een mcdonald's tegen te houden die te dicht bij een school staat of om airbnb te reguleren en zo wijken leefbaar te houden. vanuit de commissie werkgelegenheid hadden wij een advies opgesteld en dat was duidelijk: het vrije verkeer van diensten mag sociale rechten niet schaden. in dit verslag wordt ons advies volledig aan de kant geschoven. dat kan niet. het is een oproep tot minder controles op sociale dumping en minder reguleringsvrijheid voor gemeenten. iedere stem voor deze tekst is eigenlijk een stem tegen sociale rechten en tegen werknemersrechten. daarom hoop ik dat een meerderheid straks tegen deze tekst stemt.
on behalf of the id group. madam president, today marks the end of a year of european decision-making in the face of the pandemic. the european commission, relying on pro-european ministers or meps, thought it could take control of europe's health. the current stage of active vaccination is a caricature of this. because after the release of significant funds, let's remember the 30 million direct to the curevac laboratory, then the loan-purchase by public procurement groups of six vaccine manufacturing laboratories, after the most opaque negotiations and unknown negotiators, one can also wonder what the dark web will reveal to us after the hacking of the files at the ema, we are in the distribution of vaccines and we discover that there is a beginning of shortage and a breach of promise by the laboratories. all of this is unfortunate. my question is simple, madam commissioner, do you really want to bring this essential vaccine to all europeans or do you want to provide these same laboratories with a comfortable mattress of two million doses a year for several years or a wonderful subsidised testing ground for gene therapy techniques?
for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, anti-european demagogy sometimes finds its way into unexpected places. for example, markus söder, the christian democrat who could succeed angela merkel in 2021, recently claimed that it would be the overload of bureaucracy in brussels that would explain the low quantity of vaccines ordered and the length of price negotiations. how easy it is to blame the european institutions when something goes wrong at home! mr söder would of course like us to believe that, left to themselves, the member states would have been much more efficient in identifying the best vaccine candidates in advance and in negotiating quantities and prices. this is bullshit! the truth is that the european commission negotiated with the laboratories in close consultation with the member states and in a context of great uncertainty, because it is precisely by acting together that we best serve the general interest. what can be rightly reproached to the commission, however, is that it has given in to the laboratories by keeping the contracts with them secret. i say, this opacity is an insult to democracy. in any contract, the buyer must know what he is buying, on what terms and at what price. and make no mistake: in a democracy, the political decision-makers hold their legitimacy and power by delegation from the citizens, which means that when they buy vaccines, it is in the name of the latter. it is therefore unacceptable that essential terms of these contracts should be kept hidden not only from citizens but also from their representatives. beyond the question of prices, it is crucial to know, for example, who will be responsible in the event of adverse effects or problems caused by vaccines: public authorities or pharmaceutical laboratories? we don't know! where's the transparency? certainly not in this charade of giving a few members access to certain contracts, all the crucial provisions of which will have been purged anyway, without even having the right to take notes or to state publicly what they have read. the commission tells us that it was the laboratories who demanded transparency. of course they did! like all multinationals, they fear the plague of free and undistorted competition which, remember your first-year economics course, requires perfect information from all actors, buyers and sellers alike. and it is not a question here of revealing manufacturing secrets but of the terms and conditions of sale. we say that the commission should have and must now impose transparency and i would like to see which of these multinationals will refuse to hand over europeans on the pretext that transparency would harm their interests. this is all the more so since these vaccines have received considerable public support to bring them to life. ladies and gentlemen, it is time to remind these companies that in a democracy it is not they but the representatives of the people who make the rules.
on behalf of the band the left. madam president, madam commissioner, i was one of the first members to see this contract you have concluded with the pharmaceutical companies and frankly what a circus! phone confiscated, confidentiality agreement, all the vital information crossed out in black. basically, you let the company decide what mps could read. you privatized transparency. you had to do it. so it's mind-boggling, but so governments are able to impose a general lockdown on the entire population but you, in front of the pharmaceutical lobbies, you make the carpet. but the vaccine is not primarily thanks to them, it is public funds that have paid for research and development, that have paid for production capacity, that will pay, who knows, in case of hidden defect. frankly, we've already paid him three, four times for this vaccine. for pfizer, meanwhile, that represents, i think, 24 billion additional admissions over a few years. so you let the multinationals make the law, they decide how much they produce, they can even turn off the tap. did you hear that? in december, pfizer came to belgium, saying that we would have 300,000 doses less. meanwhile, in israel, the shipments were in full swing. perhaps because israel was paying twice as much as the european union. because you know that profit is paramount for them and the countries that can't pay, even though they are losing, right? you talk about covax, but europe has bought five times as much vaccine for its population, while south africa has one vaccine for every three people. south africa, which is facing one of the worst variants of the virus, should be reminded of this. yesterday, who, the world health organization, called it a catastrophic failure. and the bottom line is that to guarantee pfizer's profits, you refuse, you forbid india and south africa not only to vaccinate but also to produce the vaccine. with the patent system, you're now preventing them from producing vaccines for everyone. you're stopping the vaccine from circulating, but you're not stopping the virus from circulating. do you feel the boomerang effect coming with this south african variant in europe? we need to share the vaccine, we need to take it out of the hands of big pharma and we need to put it under public control. but you, you continue to make the carpet for the multinational pharmaceutical companies. today i read in the belgian press a call for a citizens' initiative to collect one million signatures, and our group is joining this citizens' initiative. in the face of the power of multinationals, it's time for people to rise up: no profits on the pandemic and certainly no segregation in vaccination.

mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the transeuropean transport network project is finally bringing us back to one of the union's first objectives: the free movement of goods and people. however, although transeuropean network projects have been considered highly successful, there are still obstacles to our ability to explore their full potential and demonstrate it in the future. firstly, uninterrupted crossborder rail transport on the transeuropean transport network and along the european rail freight corridors requires strong crossborder cooperation, which is of course mandatory even between infrastructure managers. throughout europe we have more than 21 different safety systems, and so the revision of this text we are discussing today calls on the commission to propose important binding measures for infrastructure managers. secondly, we must of course ensure that our transport infrastructure as a whole stands the test of time. we must align these projects with our environmental objectives and therefore, in the context of the transeuropean transport network, give priority to the full deployment of alternative fuels. and finally, our goal must be as strategic. today, our military mobility is limited because our trains cannot transport military equipment from the port of antwerp to the rest of europe, for example, so we need to increase these synergies for defence needs. i am convinced, ladies and gentlemen, that with this report we are making the necessary contribution to enabling the european commission to modernise all these elements.

dear chairman, the beautiful country of croatia was overthrown in the last war fought on european soil. the resistance of our citizens and their willingness to make a new beginning has given us the strength to rebuild our country. and then in the middle of croatia's presidency of the council of the european union, there was a pandemic. unfortunately, that was just the beginning. in march of that year, a devastating earthquake leveled the historic city of zagreb. just when we thought this unfortunate year would end with this human tragedy and natural disaster, a terrible earthquake of magnitude 6.4 on the richter scale struck the croatian region of banovina and completely destroyed the city of petrinja. the ground is still shaking, bringing a night of rage to the citizens of croatia. i would like to commend the swift response of the croatian government which, among other things, activated the european civil protection mechanism. this improved mechanism that i was working on once again highlighted the benefits that croatian citizens derive from membership of the european union. i will continue to promote the other essential aspect of european instruments, namely prevention and the redesign of new homes that offer protection to the population against possible future disasters. pilot projects for the safe reconstruction of earthquake-affected areas in croatia can be established through the 'next generation eu' budget and the recently launched 'bauhaus' initiative by commission president von der leyen. this resolution calls on the commission to speed up the process and to show flexibility in providing support to croatia in this difficult situation.

rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is with great pleasure that i present to you this report which addresses one of the most important means of gender discrimination in our day: the huge gap in women's participation in the digital economy. this is a social justice issue for women. digital inequality increases the wage gap, with negative consequences also in reforms, and one of the big problems today is the low income of retired women. the competitiveness of the european economy is also at stake, with the main barrier being the lack of skilled workers in new technologies, particularly digital technologies. a huge pool of talent, creativity, competence and capacity for innovation is being wasted. only 18% of information and communication technology professionals in europe are women and if nothing is done, the situation will only get worse as the percentages of students in this field are even weaker. in fact, of the estimated 1.3 million people studying in the ict field, only 17% are girls. the report analyses the data, identifies the potential causes of the current situation and provides solutions in the form of recommendations to the commission, the member states and society at large. as regards the causes, there are two major phenomena which contribute to this reality. on the one hand, for a variety of cultural reasons, girls tend to avoid this area as not a viable career path. on the other hand, many of those who actually follow this path eventually give up along the way, either as students or as professionals, in a phenomenon that has been dubbed the leaky pipeline effect. there's a lot of work to be done. so we propose a set of actions. first, by asking member states to set up entrepreneurship programmes and funding for projects in the ict sectors targeting women and, very importantly, to take gender diversity into account in the evaluation for funding actions supported by the multiannual financial framework and the european recovery plan. secondly, by encouraging companies and other institutions in the ict fields to create conditions to improve the career progression of their professionals. in the field of education, from the earliest levels of education, it is necessary to make girls aware of the importance of these areas, to define contents that are more attractive to them and to present success stories of women who have succeeded and who can serve as a reference in this professional area. we also propose measures in the cultural, media and audiovisual fields. some of these media unconsciously contribute to the perpetuation of certain stereotypes. it is important that they are encouraged to change the way they portray women's relationship with technology, both in the information and fiction sections. combating this digital divide is not the exclusive task of public institutions and bodies. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and to monitor the implementation of the new rules. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we'll have a single vote, no amendments or any split vote request.
member of the commission. – madam president, i thank the european parliament for putting gender equality on the agenda today. i also obviously extend my thanks to the rapporteurs and the shadows who have worked on finalising the three reports we are discussing today. every day we continue to discover more about the havoc which the covid—19 pandemic has played, and is playing, on the lives of people who are already in disadvantaged positions in our societies, and about how existing inequalities have been exacerbated. many women, as frontline workers, were at the deep end of this pandemic and thus impacted by it disproportionately. but this is not only at the place of work. while at home, confinement measures have kept many women in abusive and hostile environments. the situation of closed schools and childcare facilities meant more care work for many women. many lost their jobs and thus their financial independence. all this means that we must, more than ever before, step up our work towards equality. it is with this conviction in mind that the commission continues to work with one goal in mind: to do everything in our power not to leave anyone behind. this commission has already taken a number of actions in response to the social and economic consequences of the pandemic. in particular, the eu long—term budget 2021—2027, together with next generation eu, form the largest stimulus package ever financed, through the eu budget, of eur 1.8 trillion. this will promote gender equality through gender mainstreaming and specific targeted measures. in line with its commitment to the gender equality strategy and the interinstitutional agreement, the commission is working on the development of a methodology to track gender—related expenditure at programme level and in the new mff. this will bring significant progress on gender mainstreaming across all eu policies. gender mainstreaming is also at the core of the gender equality strategy, which i presented in march last year. the crisis we are living today has only amplified the importance of this strategy. it has confirmed the need to address core issues such as women’s economic empowerment, violence against women and the gender digital gap, which are all priorities of the gender equality strategy. the first major initiative of the strategy is the proposal to introduce binding pay transparency measures. we are in the final preparation phase and aim to present the proposal shortly. in essence, the purpose is to detect and prevent gender—based pay discrimination or unjustified pay differences and empower those being discriminated against to enforce the right to equal pay. the commission will move forward also with new initiatives on gender equality. by the end of 2021, we will present a legislative proposal to prevent and combat specific forms of gender—based violence. this year we are preparing an initiative to extend the list of eu crimes to include hate speech and hate crime, including on the basis of a person’s gender. these actions will be followed by a recommendation on the prevention of harmful practices against women and girls, such as female genital mutilation, to address the need for effective pre—emptive measures, acknowledge the importance of education and strengthen public services. based on work already started, we will establish a cooperation framework with internet platforms to tackle online violence against women and girls. we are preparing initiatives in the area of childcare and long—term care. we will propose the revision of the barcelona targets on childcare and propose a child guarantee on social inclusion of children from disadvantaged backgrounds, including through affordable and quality early childhood education and care. we will also adopt a comprehensive strategy on the rights of the child, which seeks to ensure equal rights, protection and support to all children regardless of background, origin, gender or residence status. we will publish a report on long—term care, which will also be adopted this year. the report will also analyse the gender dimension of long—term care, including the impact of the provision of informal care. it is essential that we follow up and keep monitoring the development and implementation of the strategy. international women’s day would be a good opportunity to take stock of what has been implemented one year from the adoption of the strategy. the need to address the digital gender gap is absolute. empowering women’s success in the digital economy opens the doors to growth. the under—representation of women in the digital industries persists, as we have heard. women are still not involved as much as they should be in our building of a digital future. this is a huge loss of untapped talent to our economies and societies. more women in digital jobs could create an annual eur 16 billion gdp boost in the eu. for that, we need to encourage girls to study science and technology so that more women choose a career in science, technology, engineering and mathematics, essential areas for developing our digital sphere. the commission has taken various steps to encourage more girls and women in the digital sector by supporting networks and challenging gender stereotypes. these culminated in the ministerial declaration of commitment on women in digital, where member states have now agreed to a number of actions to address the various facets of this problem. the commission will support these efforts under the new mff and next generation eu. our efforts to build a more sustainable economy and society through digital innovation depends to a large extent on women’s involvement. many women’s talents and skills remain untapped. this is a waste of human capital. more women get involved if we start at the root. we must break down stereotypes and bias and encourage girls to choose their parts without influencing them with our biases. we must also give the necessary space for women to thrive in the digital economy and support more women as entrepreneurs and leaders. i strongly believe that this is key to the transition to a resilient and competitive economy, so i look forward now to hearing your remarks and, of course, i remain available to reply to your questions. thank you again, thank you for being here, thank you for your report, and thank you for participating in this debate. author. mr president, two years ago this parliament adopted a resolution on the plight of political prisoners in vietnam. almost a year ago, this parliament voted on the eu-vietnam trade and investment treaties. i remember that, faced with sceptics like myself, the defenders of the agreement said that it would improve the human rights situation for the benefit of the whole population. although commitments have been made to reform labour law, the human rights issue in vietnam goes beyond the mere ratification of ilo conventions. since then, the repression has indeed accelerated. commercial policy and human rights are thus two separate issues. not for pham chi dung, president of the association of independent journalists, who was arrested a few days after speaking directly to us with a critical view of the trade agreement ratification process. he was sentenced to 15 years in prison for this, and together with nguyen tuong thuy and le huu minh tuan, who were sentenced to 11 years in prison, he is one of the three journalists for whom we are today calling by a large majority for their unconditional release. the european external action service delegation in hanoi continues to express concern at the conviction of hundreds of other peace activists, confirming a worrying reduction in the area of freedom of expression, assembly and association in the country. our resolution therefore calls for the release of journalists and activists, improved conditions of detention, particularly in the context of the current pandemic, the adoption of a moratorium on the death penalty and a reminder to vietnam of its commitments to its own people, the european union and the international community.

mr president, madam president, the european union should defend the abolition of intellectual property rights in vaccines available on the market, as is permitted by the world trade organisation agreement governing these matters. there are two good reasons for this. the first is that all the capabilities of the pharmaceutical industry in all countries of the world must be mobilized to accelerate the process of vaccination globally, because the health crisis and the resulting economic crisis is global. it would make no sense to vaccinate only a part of the planet. and the compulsory licences under article 31 of the agreement on intellectual property aspects are not enough. but there's a second reason. manon aubry talked about it, these vaccines were paid for mostly by public funds: eight trillion dollars out of the 14 trillion dollars that their overall development cost, and 2,000 by philanthropy. as ellen t. hoyn said, it was unwise of our governments to hand over this public money unconditionally. the suspension of the patent should have been a condition for receiving public funding. but now we can correct this grave error if the union accepts the lifting of the patent at the world trade organisation and does not oppose it when the countries of the south are now demanding it.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. i understand everyone who is frustrated, angry and tired because we still have to adhere to these strict measures. but as an expert in germany recently said, unfortunately, the virus is not tired. it's even been boosted by the mutations. and we are seeing this in portugal now: if you loosen up too quickly and too soon, it gets bad. and that's why we all have to follow the measures. and we need to do better at the border. the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. open borders are a noble goal, but we have a dangerous mutation of the virus here three even and that is why we need to put more pressure to protect europe as a whole, but also to look more closely at the regions, to have controls that the mutations do not spread too quickly. on the subject of vaccines, i want to dispel two myths. it is always said that europe has ordered too late. but first, the fact is that the responsibility for this is primarily with the most important vaccine from biontech, the pfizer company. pfizer has refused - and this has been written about many times by astute journalists - to accept the right of every european to sue if the company makes a mistake. and i would like to see someone say: i want europeans to have no right to sue a company that makes a serious mistake when you are harmed. i want someone to stand up and say, "i don't care about the law anymore". the commission has not done so, and it cannot be criticised for that, ladies and gentlemen. and then they say it was too little money. it is always the 2.7 billion from the esi that is mentioned. but if you add up the money that the member states are already spending to pay for vaccines, the esi comes to 22 billion. so not everything went perfectly, but a lot of things are twisted, and the basic decision was right. we must come together as europeans to overcome this crisis, and we should continue to uphold that, ladies and gentlemen!

madam president, what we hear today is that we as a chamber are sometimes somewhat inconsistent in our calls to platforms, and thus by this we risk becoming irrelevant. we urged the platforms to act during the rohingya case in 2016 when thousands of people were killed, but in 2021, when five people were killed in the capitol attacks, we asked the platforms why did you act and silence trump? so the problem, i think sometimes, is that we often call for different things. i accept that platforms should not have the right to easily silence a president, of course, but what are the rules that would make this impossible in the future? i believe our job is to create them, and we are indeed on the right track. but in order to create them, we need to make sure we have clear definitions and visions of how we actually treat platforms. do we consider them to be media? are they a type of providers similar to cable tv operators? or are they simply an environment in a place where everything is possible and can happen? i think we need a coherent definition of that, and only then could we perhaps be consistent in our calls and, most importantly, successful in our efforts to create a just and a good environment, good rules for platforms that most importantly safeguard our fundamental rights and values.
madam president, freedom of the press has been in the spotlight for a reason in recent years. you can imagine that years ago, before social media, newspapers and news programs would be all over the place, spreading false information, wrong information. maybe it was the advertisers who got the message, not the reporters, the reporters, the anchors. today, however, the individual has a variety of verified and unverified information channels to serve him in the creation of truth. i am very concerned about how easy it is to publish a complete lie and disinformation and not take any responsibility for it, claiming that it is about freedom of speech and thus discrediting the target of the attack. these and similar posts, attacks, insults are mostly used today by the populist right, which with its autocratic tendencies undermines democracy and flouts fundamental human rights. they even see the free media as their enemy rather than a fundamental aspect of a free society. one of the more effective tactics of disinformation has been the party appropriation of new media that serve exclusively to promote party ideology. unfortunately, this practice is being used by more and more politicians who smear and spread untruths about their political opponents, including in slovenia, the slovenian prime minister. of course, it is normal to have different views and beliefs. when constructive dialogue is replaced by lies and offensive or even hostile rhetoric, our democracy is truly in danger. that's when things go so far, there's a coup like the one that happened at the capitol. we are at the crossroads between freedom of expression and individual rights of expression, and i strongly welcome the action plan presented by the european commission.
mr president, social media today is what printing was in the middle ages: a tool for citizens to express their views and defend their interests. but as in the past, the ruling elite responds with censorship and repression to determine the truth under the guise of fighting so-called fake news. but a truth that needs to be protected by censorship is a lie. the aim is to criminalise resistance to unwanted migration and multicultural inter-population under the guise of combating so-called hate speech. but like a mother caring for her children, this resistance is not out of hatred for what is foreign, but out of love for what is one's own. a democracy cannot exist without freedom of speech. it's not a matter of left or right, or agreeing or disagreeing; that's a fundamental right that protects us all from tyranny. censorship on social media must therefore be punished. we must break the oligarchy of silicon valley to protect the fundamental rights and fundamental freedoms of our citizens and our democracy.

on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, the ecr group strongly condemns the violence in tigray. we support international calls for swift and unconditional access to the whole region for humanitarian aid, independent human rights monitors and the media. the vast majority of the six million inhabitants of tigray have been without adequate food, water and medicine for weeks. two million civilians are reported to have been driven from their homes. in the north, the harvest is said to have been burned. elsewhere farmers have abandoned their fields. it seems that the ethiopian government is deliberately withholding food in order to starve people. central and east tigray are on the brink of famine. recent promises of humanitarian access have been insufficient and uncertain. there are continued reports of violations of international humanitarian law, such as the deliberate shelling of civilian targets, extrajudicial executions and widespread looting. it’s also reported that sexual violence is a weapon of war in this conflict. the offensive by the ethiopian government army against tigray has rekindled the old conflict with sudan over the al-fashaqa border region and eritrean troops are also said to be active in tigray. it should not be forgotten that repression by the federal government is also taking place elsewhere in the country, such as oromia, ethiopia’s largest state. the european union, together with other international partners, must play their part to help end this conflict and reform the ethiopian state, something supported by its many diverse populations. the ethiopian prime minister should not only give his nobel prize back but also return his country to the principles of peace and democracy.

mr president, commissioner, today we are taking a big step. with eu4health we are supporting member states, greece, cyprus, and they in turn will have the opportunity to strengthen the resilience of national health systems and to integrate innovative treatments and practices. the €5.1 billion may be just the beginning, but it's a great beginning in the pillar of european health that didn't exist before the pandemic. the greeks say that no evil is pure good. through the pandemic, we managed in the health sector first of all to jointly order medical supplies, then to further strengthen the research that led to the vaccine, and of course to jointly order vaccines, a great achievement, parallel to the recovery fund. we must now move to the next stage: the prevention and early detection of the diseases that afflict europe, that afflict each and every one of us: cancer, heart disease, diabetes. let me specifically record my interest in invisible cancer, childhood cancer. the eu4health programme we are voting on today can fund the initiative by helping children with cancer, so that all children have access to early and optimal detection, diagnosis, treatment and care. it can support the exchange of best practices and standards of care for children with cancer and thus complement the actions implemented by the new european reference networks. i would like to say that in my country, greece, prime minister kyriakos mitsotakis has already announced a major step: the registration of childhood cancer cases in a special register. together with eu4health, we have the opportunity at this time to support all the children who are fighting so that no one is left behind. all the kids are winners.
madam president, we in the s&d group share the ambition and we welcome this historically strong and comprehensive health strategy. given the current health crisis and the enormous challenge that our member states and we as a union are facing, with the adoption of the eu4health programme, which is ten times bigger than the previous health programmes, europe is sending a clear signal that we are doing everything in our power to increase the protection of the health of the citizens. ultimately, this is about finding solutions that bring the most value into the lives of our citizens. eu4health is an important step in the direction to ensure that people in europe can benefit from high quality, accessible, safe, equal and affordable healthcare. it is crucial that we do not forget the deeper underlying structures and factors that impact public health. we know that there are strong social, economic and lifestyle patterns that have clear impact on the health of citizens. this also includes the current health inequalities and unequal access to healthcare that persist in europe. the s&d group fully stands behind the importance of promoting access to sexual and reproductive healthcare. this is a human right and we will not allow anyone to try to rob this from the people of europe, to rob freedom from europe. therefore, the s&d group also welcomes the 20% of the resources under the programme that has been reserved for health promotion and disease prevention measures. this is also closely linked to the important work that the union must do in supporting and strengthening the national healthcare system by improving their resilience and their resource efficiency. we also welcome that the member states can perform stress testing on their national healthcare systems as a means to see what can be possibly further improved as well as the facilitation of best practice. this agreement will also support the development of medical products that is less harmful for the environment.
madam president, commissioner, €5.1 billion for a europe of health that is taking off, so to speak, is the extraordinary result our negotiators have achieved. i would therefore like to thank our rapporteur, mr christian buşoi, and our commissioner, mrs kyriakides, on behalf of us all. the time has come when health was relegated to the second division of european policies. the covid-19 pandemic has tragically shed light on the shortcomings of our healthcare systems. eu4health is committed to change the game. i'll try not to repeat everything that's already been said. 20% of the budget is now devoted to disease prevention and health promotion, with particular attention to health: tobacco, alcohol dependence, without falling into prohibition. we are also making progress in building up european stocks of essential crisis products, everything that we have actually lacked: medicines, equipment, devices. a strong demand, a repeated demand of our parliament from the beginning, 12.5% of the budget, or 637 million euros, will be dedicated to them. a crisis, madam commissioner, which has shown, as we have said and said again, our dependence on china, on india. europe must take back control of the production tool to overcome this shortage of medicines, which, however, largely predates the events of this year. in 2008, europe created the innovative medicines initiative (imi), one of the most ambitious private-public partnerships in the world for innovative medicines. it must now simply do the same for old medicines, often considered not to be profitable enough by certain pharmaceutical companies, i will not name names, but the waiting patients will recognize themselves. a final word to conclude, with reference to the demographic ageing that has begun in europe, the over 80s represent 6% of the population, europe clearly needs to rely on telemedicine if online health is to reach our isolated and connected elders. acting for our seniors is also the europe we want.
mr president, madam commissioner, what is conceivable is clearly stated. and whatever one may think, article 168 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union on public health is clear. it defines the necessary health cooperation between european states, with union action complementing, supporting and improving the complementarity of national policies for disease prevention. this action also includes the fight against major diseases such as epidemics, by promoting research into their causes, transmission, prevention and the necessary information, as well as the monitoring of serious crossborder threats by disseminating alerts and recommendations to be implemented. on the basis of this article, which we considered to be more than sufficient, many existing texts, which were also sufficient, should have enabled the european union to deal with the covid pandemic. but alas, 15 months after the crisis began, it is clear that nothing has worked in time. no ad hoc agency, not even the drug agency, with irresponsible authorizations of treatments like remdesivir or cdce on the side, is on the alert with three weeks delay, nor the european parliament in the control and the requirement of rapid results of studies funded for more than a year, nor especially the european commission in the negotiation of vaccines. nothing protected the europeans. the text we are voting on today is not working. this new version of the union's action in the field of health is going in all directions, in a total confusion of objectives and actions, but certainly by further reducing the independence of the states in their prerogatives. and this is the case with all the binding health regulations which are increasing and strengthening in the present emergency. the fact that the current crisis is being exploited by decision-makers who are completely overwhelmed by the burden of the existing mechanisms - which would be largely sufficient if they had been properly activated - is extremely worrying as regards the added value of the european union to the states.
president. - we are voting today on a european health programme. that is important and it contains an important principle: health in all policies. health everywhere, in all policies. i'm glad that's the case, but i hope we'll take it seriously. because it is difficult to talk about the importance of health and then to undermine or weaken the health of people with other policies. the current european policy, let us be clear about this, is undermining health in many ways. let me give you an example. save the health care system from collapsing. yeah, that's what caused health care workers to run out of gums before the pandemic even started, or people to delay doctor visits. or we can think of the european policy of flexibility in the labour market. they make our lives a rat race, that we have to work harder and harder, more and more flexible, until we're too sick to enjoy our retirement.
mr president, syria is a cautionary tale. it shows that the cost of inaction can be higher than the cost of action. it also shows that when america and her allies leave a vacuum, then it’s filled by malign actors – putin, erdogan, the islamic state. now, syria is both a no-man’s land and the site of a proxy war. europe has been unable to act against atrocities committed in our neighbourhood. we have been confronted by the limits of our famous soft power when dealing with autocrats. but we have also seen the consequences of our incapacity from the migration crisis to the radicalisation of so-called foreign fighters using isil propaganda. but even today, syria cannot look to the future until a political solution is found. here we have to accept that the un-led peace processes have failed. it’s now time for new thinking after a decade of failure.
madam president, mr executive vice-president, madam president-in-office of the council, this parliament is debating - and i hope it will adopt today - two important reports, which are key at this time of uncertainty, and even doubt, about the economic development of the union as a whole. and i would first of all like to thank my colleagues lina gálvez in the committee on employment and social affairs and joachim schuster in the committee on economic and monetary affairs for the excellent work they have done in reaching a broad consensus which will allow this house to have a clear voice in the debate that the commission and the council are having on the implementation of the european semester and also on budgetary rules. and i would also like to thank, of course, my colleague markus ferber for his effort to reach last minute compromise amendments to support the report on the european semester. it comes at a crucial moment when the union has, in a way, broken with old conceptions. for the first time in history, the union is going to issue community debt, common debt, to finance recovery. for the first time too, the commission has proposed not to apply the budgetary rules during these times of crisis. we welcome the maintenance of this rule for at least the next year and until the recovery is firm, and we believe that we should take advantage of these months to thoroughly revise the budgetary rules, because no one believes any more that we can go back to the old rules and we need to provide the union as a whole with a countercyclical budgetary policy that is sustainable.
president, europe is currently undergoing a full vaccination campaign against the coronavirus. and as prime minister rutte said this week: we are on our way to the finish line, to a more normal life where everyone has more breathing space, the breathing space we crave so much so that our economy can recover as well. we are talking today about the european semester. this is an important cycle of coordination of economic and budgetary policies within the european union. and it must remain so, especially in these difficult economic times. the question is: how can we restore the european economy as quickly as possible while keeping spending under control? we must find a good balance here. that means that we must think about how and in what timeframe and that we can tighten up the budgetary rules again. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european union has shown solidarity with countries that have been hit hard by the coronavirus crisis over the past year, and i think it is right. if the economy recovers sufficiently, we also need to return to normal fiscal rules, simply to make countries more resilient. we need to vaccinate people, rebuild the economy. that's how we get to the finish line, that's how we get to a more normal life.
madam president, commissioner, our fellow europeans have just experienced a year of health, social and economic crises. it is clear that we cannot run the european semester and apply the rules of stability as we did before. this position was defended and adopted by a very large majority in the opinion of the committee on budgets on the european semester. this european semester is of course essential to the convergence of economic policies necessary for the functioning of the internal market but since its inception many challenges have emerged. that is why we in the committee on budgets are calling for better consideration of social issues in connection with the recovery of employment, environmental priority, greater fiscal coordination, poverty reduction and gender equality. in this respect, i would like to say with my colleague sylvie brunet that the priority is to significantly reduce the gaps in employment between women and men and to improve the situation of young people who are neither in education nor in employment. it is also a priority to combat poverty and social exclusion and to address their root causes. we must also pay attention to the working conditions of some of the workers who are under severe strain and who have been indispensable to us during the crisis. this rebound, this new impetus, will be supported by a multiannual budget and an ambitious european recovery plan, offering member states funding opportunities for the environmental and social dimension of the european union.
madam president, if we want to regain the growth we lost long before the health crisis, and if you want this growth to be sustainable, liberate and protect, then both conditions must be met. we need to unlock our economy and allow our businesses to reach their full potential without crushing them with taxation and overregulation. the recent decisions on the european budget have shown a real leap forward: systematic budget increases, the introduction of own resources, not to lighten the contribution of the states, but to further increase the tax burden. in addition, your imposition of ever more demanding environmental constraints and standards, with a green pact for europe with unrealizable objectives, obviously weighs heavily on our companies, our industries and our farmers and producers. if you were to impose these standards on our competitors, you would have to agree to protect our economy, to get rid of the globalised free trade which creates unfair competition and holds back our growth. it is not a question of overtaxing all incoming products, but simply of setting up customs barriers when necessary, as most countries in the world know how to do, to finally defend our local products. the current portuguese presidency of the european union, and some in this house, are still calling for the swift conclusion of the trade agreement with the mercosur countries. i hope, of course, for the sake of our farmers that it will be buried once and for all. the only effect of insisting on being the best pupils in the ecology class will be to render us impotent and weak at the mercy of the aggressive strategies of countries which have neither our complexes nor our powers and to whom you have opened up our market.
madam president, during this legislature this house has not yet managed to pass a single resolution regarding the economic aspect of the european semester. the reason is simple. the rules binding the semester are not accepted by all as economically and socially effective. regions, economic agents and political factions experience the process as an arbitrary system of governance. in the meantime, many believe that the draconian approach to semester rules has been the cause of increased poverty and dwindling public spending in crucial areas like health and education. the root of the problem has been a ‘one size fits all’ methodology supposedly designed to promote a level playing field. it suits major eu regions and economic models to the disadvantage of others. even now, assistance programmes and allowed exceptions are favouring regions which economically are most resilient to crisis. so, for instance, the handicaps carried by insular peripheral regions of the eu are still being underestimated. we need a socially charged reform of the semester process, based on a tailor-made approach that reflects the separate needs of the different regions and communities of the union.
madam president, we have long been aware that, despite many strategies and initiatives, the gender pay gap persists. in the european union, the figure is 14 percent. this is followed by pension inequality. women are thus disadvantaged not only during their working lives, but later during their retirement, the retirement period, they become vulnerable to poverty. of course, the causes of this situation are complex, ranging from a higher representation of women in lower-paid sectors to a greater amount of time spent on unpaid domestic work or caring duties. for these reasons, women often accept lower-paid and part-time jobs. despite systematically raising awareness of this phenomenon for years, we have not yet taken measures to ensure that care for children or dependent relatives is not reflected in a reduction in pension entitlements due to the omission of years in a career. investing in childrearing or caring for a family member is a value in itself, but it also has its own quantifiable value for our economy and the sustainability of the social system. it is therefore necessary to consider how to assess care periods more fairly and convert them into fairer pensions. i would like to thank my colleagues for supporting my amendment in this sense and to call on the member states to follow this recommendation when adjusting their pension reforms.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we are witnessing a simultaneous development in our european societies of very different perceptions of this crisis. those who are or have been ill, those who have sick relatives, friends, and acquaintances are particularly aware of the health crisis and know how dangerous this disease is. those who are worried about their jobs or have actually lost their jobs have real, serious, existential worries. and then there are many who take the necessary measures, follow the necessary measures, but know: yes, it is not all easy in everyday life. we're focused on dealing with the health crisis. vaccinations and all things related to them are the priority. but then immediately the labour market is on our labour agenda. that is why it is so important that in the debate on the european semester we talk about how to preserve each individual job and how to create new jobs. the suspension of the aid scheme for the duration of the health crisis was important and is also a success for austria and other member states, so that direct, immediate, rapid and full-scale aid can be provided as long as this health crisis continues. and for that, we need training, continuing training, opportunities for young people and more entrepreneurial freedom, more opportunities for workers, to get europe out of the crisis.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, europe in the time of covid has changed its watchwords and decided to put at the centre of its political agenda pillars such as growth and reform and the combination of growth and reform, finally. it's a new paradigm, it's a paradigm that takes into account the fact that discipline is sometimes necessary, but it only benefits some, while growth benefits everyone. we cannot but consider that demanding reforms from individual countries cannot be done by a european union which at times seems to be entrenched behind the illusion of its own infallibility. the reforms must be implemented by all, otherwise it would be difficult to understand why we normally do not reach agreement on this half-yearly report, which we discuss every first half-year, even here in parliament. what's the reason? the reason is that too often what we call 'coordination of the member states' turns into a pretext for 'conditionality': that's not good for growth. the long run, to discomfort keynes, is not the one where we're all dead, the long run is the one where we're raising our children and our grandchildren. well, nextgenerationeu is the novelty that puts our passion for the future of citizens at the center, let's embrace this novelty in the european political paradigm.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, there is no doubt that the pandemic, the lockdown and the disruption of supply chains have exacerbated inequalities. the labour market looks like unemployment. the wage, pension and digital gaps reach unacceptable levels, as is the situation of access to education, especially for the most vulnerable groups. digitalisation has also accelerated and there has been and is a profound restructuring. member states and the european union are trying to fight the pandemic, rebuild the economy, accelerate recovery and prepare our societies for this digital and economic transition, but the response in this transition has to be a just response, a response that leaves no one behind, that leaves no territory behind, but also that does not compromise the heritage of future generations. we are in a period of disillusionment with the european project and democracy. there is also a disaffection towards established political parties and, unfortunately, some citizens are more open to authoritarian interpretations of democracy. we must give a response that must be transparent: european funds, europe's response, has been historic, but now it is the member states' turn: that these funds go where they have to go, where they are needed most, with transparency and common sense, never for ideological projects. it is time for political leadership with a european vision; it is time for a more social europe, but also more transparent and with common sense.

executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank the european parliament for holding this timely debate. in circumstances such as these, it’s perhaps useful to recall that the european semester came into existence a decade ago and in the wake of the last crisis. one lesson that we learned at that time is that economic policy coordination and surveillance can only be effective if it takes an integrated approach and covers all relevant aspects, and takes into account how they interact. over the years, the european semester has evolved and adapted according to changing circumstances. it has assumed its central role for coordinating economic and employment policies. it has also inspired domestic policy debates. its focus has expanded to include social and employment objectives, and it pays increasing attention to investment challenges. the european semester now integrates the united nations sustainable development goals, adding environmental and climate aspects into this analysis. it has repeatedly proved its value by its flexibility alone. it remains our main tool for coordinating economic, fiscal and employment policies. in the wake of the covid-19 crisis, we will need a functioning european semester more than ever to coordinate these policies across the european union. it will be vital for an inclusive and lasting recovery, especially from social and employment perspectives, and a recovery that is as uniform as we can make it across the member states. by the same token, it is important to include all parties in this process, particularly within member states. this means continuous, closed engagement with social partners, civil society, local and regional authorities, as well as with national parliaments, and of course with the european parliament. i look forward to continued engagement and to update you on the european semester implementation.
