madam president! ladies and gentlemen! the covid-19 pandemic is forcing restrictions that are causing dramatic negative changes in the labor market. i am therefore pleased that the european commission has presented a concrete action plan to implement the european pillar of social rights. our priority now should be to curb these negative trends in order to avoid a massive increase in unemployment and social precariousness. but on the other hand, we also need to look to the future and work to adapt the labour market to the challenges of both the green and digital transformation. therefore, economic recovery must go hand in hand with the implementation of the pillar. otherwise, inequality will only increase. unfortunately, once again, we see that the people most affected by the crisis are workers in precarious employment and young people. youth unemployment is becoming alarmingly high and once again we must focus our efforts on avoiding catastrophic social consequences for an entire generation. i believe that the instrument for recovery and resilience has a major role to play in this regard. member states should design their implementation plan to complement the pillar and to support both job maintenance and job creation. the ambitious goals we set for the future must not be achieved at the expense of any social or professional group, and especially not at the expense of those who have been affected by the current crisis.
madam president, finally, the social action plan presents a landmark shift in our approach to crisis recovery and for the european integration itself. we are finally focusing not on markets, banks and big corporations, which were labelled ‘too big to fail’, but on every european citizen and their quality of life and well-being. it was about time, because thanks to the long years of progressive efforts, we now all share the vision that each person is too small to fail, to be failed by their governments, by their union. this is a big change, which moves us from policies as quantity to policies as quality. you know the striking figures about unemployment. but it is not enough to simply preserve or create new jobs. we need to ensure fair opportunities for all: quality jobs for all, good working conditions for all european workers, life-long learning and training for all european workers. this is a big shift. this is why the proposed eu targets for 78% unemployment, yearly training for 60% of adults, and reducing the number of people in risk of poverty by 15 million people is really important, and it’s a step in the right direction. i am happy that socialist commissions were leading in this process, but i would also like to call on the member states after the social summit in porto not only to commit to these targets, but also to focus on the social dimension of the national recovery plans.
the summit in porto is a unique opportunity for all of us to strengthen the social europe that our people so badly need. indeed, as many as nine in ten eu citizens said that their expectations of a social europe were very high, which shows that it is our core element of the european union. social europe is not an abstract structure - it is a europe of fair and decent working conditions, a decent wage that does not allow poverty, quality and accessible health care, equality between women and men and, finally, the well-being and secure future of our children. the commission's roadmap for the implementation of the european pillar of social rights is essential to give a new and powerful impetus to the building of a strong social europe.

promote access to early childhood education and care, and further promote the work—life balance. better jobs mean striving for better working conditions. we have already proposed ambitious legislation on adequate minimum wages. two weeks ago, the commission launched a consultation of eu social partners on decent working conditions and social protection for people working through platforms. a major priority must concern skills and equality. if we are talking about effective labour market policies, we cannot emphasise enough the importance of skills for jobs. at least 60% of adults should participate in training every year by 2030. skills empower people to adapt to change and skills are an ever more important source of our global competitiveness. the different actions and our european skills agenda are crucial to this end, and we will only successfully manage the green transition and the digital change transformation if we invest a lot in skills – and not just in the skills of those who are already skilled, but also in those who have lower skills, giving them better opportunities on the labour market. we must advance gender equality and equal opportunities for all, with a particular focus on youth. last week’s proposal for a directive on binding pay transparency and enforcement measures and a new disability rights strategy are crucial. (the speaker was interrupted by the european anthem). (laughter and applause) that’s great. that’s really great. thank you very much. lastly, i want to emphasise the fight against rising inequality and poverty. this stands at the heart of the action plan. we want the number of people at risk of poverty or social exclusion to be reduced by at least, i insist, at least, 15 million. the fight against poverty requires stronger safety nets for those in need and robust social protection systems. later this month, we will table the long—anticipated proposal for a european child guarantee, which was born here in this parliament. we will also launch a european platform on combating homelessness, and next year we will come forward with a council recommendation on minimum income. i agree social investments are key and, particularly in this recovery, we have to build a more resilient society and therefore we have to mobilise european funds and support social enterprises by proposing a social economy action plan. dear president, honourable members, we can only achieve our ambitions for a green and digital transition by covering also the social dimension. social progress has to be embedded in our recovery strategy and to become a driving principle of our post—covid european agenda. that is what’s at stake in porto – an economy that works for all people. that’s what we are aiming at.
acting president of the council. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you all for the very timely and very rich debate we have had here, which makes it clear that strengthening the european social dimension is fundamental. it has been said here that there is no competitiveness and cohesion without inclusion. it has been said here that the road is long and requires courage and action. now we have a plan of action. this plan, together with the financial instruments at our disposal, and the possibility of having to draw up our own national recovery and resilience plans, will allow us to build a renewed commitment to social europe in which we expect all key actors to participate. the plan, in our view, combines ambition with realism and takes account of the diversity of social models in the member states. it is a good plan because, firstly, it sets concrete targets for the union by 2030 in terms of employment rates, including employment for women and youth. the focus is on strengthening skills, in particular in adult education, reducing poverty and social exclusion, and reducing child poverty. secondly, it proposes concrete measures to achieve the objectives, many of them through legislative instruments. thirdly, the objectives will be monitored through a social indicators panel, the social scoreboard, which is part of the european semester and which should have new indicators. finally, it is clear that the implementation of the action plan is the task of all: the european institutions, the member states, the social partners, business and trade union associations and civil society, in accordance with their respective competences and the diversity that marks our europe. we will work to ensure that the porto summit is an opportunity to make a renewed commitment from all those involved in the european pillar of social rights. the summit will focus on how to strengthen europe's social dimension to meet the challenges of climate change and the digital transition, and send a clear signal that the union is promoting policies that put people and their well-being at the centre of its concerns. we're counting on your input, your presence, i hope, in the port. this will be an important step, a further step on the road to building a social europe, a fair and inclusive europe, as the european citizens are asking us to do. we have to live up to their expectations.
in writing. this action plan defies your petty intentions, but does not hide a limited ambition: it assumes that 78 million people will remain at risk of poverty. as regards employment, it is symptomatic that, even in rhetoric, the objective of full employment has been definitively removed from eu strategies. the eu implicitly assumes unemployment as intrinsic to the economic and social model it advocates. this plan is largely an attempt to disguise policies which undermine social and labour rights with a social label. social progress is not compatible with the neoliberal policies promoted by the eu. as we have argued, what is needed is a genuine pact for social progress and employment which, together with the repeal of the stability pact and its successors, aims, among other things, at full employment, the raising of wages, the defence of collective bargaining, the eradication of precariousness, the reduction and regulation of working hours without loss of pay, the defence and strengthening of public services, in particular access to the right to health, education, housing, social security, public networks for the care of children, the elderly and disabled people. that way you make sure no one gets left behind!
in writing. 3.5 years after gothenburg and in the midst of the corona crisis, it is not a day too soon that the commission finally came up with an action plan for the social pillar last week. this action plan is a step in the right direction towards a more social europe, with good initiatives such as adequate minimum wages or the european social security passport. on the other hand, the plan lacks the ambition to use this momentum to finally tackle poverty and focus on quality jobs. the target of lifting 15 million people out of poverty by 2030 is far too low-hanging fruit. in the coming years, when we will really feel the economic impact of the corona crisis, even more people are at risk of losing their jobs and falling into poverty. furthermore, an increased level of employment is of course important, but far more than quantity we must focus on the quality of these jobs, as more than 20 million workers in europe are still at risk of poverty. finally, the real work is just beginning. this is also a joint struggle with the member states, regional and local authorities, the social partners and civil society, so that this action plan does not remain a dead letter.

madam president, africa is certainly a continent with one of the poorest populations in the world, but it is also undeniably the richest continent in the world in terms of natural resources: gold, diamonds, uranium, phosphorus, coltan, gas, cocoa, cotton, to name just a few. without these raw materials, most of the world's industries would be at a standstill today. for example, 80% of the electricity produced in france comes from the exploitation of uranium in niger. since colonization and after independence, the main concern of the colonizing countries has been to empty these countries to guarantee the welfare of the western populations and to fill the pockets of business. foreign companies do not bring much to the local population, they take advantage of the abundant, cheap labour force, without social protection. they also benefit from low taxation and, in addition, from no environmental liability. true, they often face failing, often corrupt governments, more concerned with their own personal enrichment than with the welfare of their fellow citizens. global warming caused by our production models is adding to this disaster. so yes, we need a new eu-africa strategy. but this strategy must impose social and environmental responsibility on companies. this strategy must combat corruption. this strategy must change our production model and it must include much more civil society, women and young people.
madam president, nine miles is the distance that separates africa from europe. everything that happens in africa happens within nine miles. that's why it's never been truer: helping africa is helping ourselves. to achieve the goals of climate change, equal rights, fighting poverty -- we're not going to get there unless we're together, unless we're on board. so this strategy is a great opportunity to make this partnership different, a partnership between equals; it has been said, far from paternalism, of the donor-recipient relationship. because, indeed, africa is the continent that has today the largest pockets of poverty, that has enormous problems of instability, of violence, but it is also the continent that has doubled its gross domestic product in 12 years; it is the continent that is becoming the largest trading area in the world since the creation of the wto; it is the continent that has gone from having no telephone to being connected with mobile phone; it is the continent with which we must go hand in hand in a partnership between equals. thank you, chrysoula, for your work; thank you, commissioner, for your commitment.
madam president, this report is a testament to the double narrative of the lrem on migration, as it openly advocates new legal immigration routes under the false pretext of stopping illegal arrivals. but a solution seems obvious. with €25 billion in development aid to africa in 2018, europeans must demand in return that recipient countries accept the repatriation of their nationals. indeed, according to the commission's own admission, of the six countries identified as having particularly low execution rates, five are in africa. in addition, the money remitted by immigrants to their countries of origin now represents more than three times the amount of official development assistance, and unctad recently estimated illicit capital flight at 3.7% of africa's gdp, or $88.6 billion per year, showing that vast untapped sources of finance can be tapped. the european union is also committed to the development of a common foreign and security policy, which is based on the principles of the united nations charter and the international covenant on civil and political rights.
madam president, commissioner, is the european vaccine policy racist? i wondered for a long time when i saw this map which actually shows that this year, europeans could be vaccinated while africans will have to wait several more years. people will die needlessly because a vaccine exists and could be produced in africa. but when the african union asked europe to produce the vaccine at the world trade organization, the european union replied: no, the vaccine will remain in the hands of western multinational pharmaceutical companies. the former colonial powers who deny africa the right to produce the vaccine: the symbol is really strong. the european union therefore prefers to save the profits of multinationals rather than saving people's lives. thomas sankara said that at some point we had to choose between champagne for a few and drinking water for all... but today we have to choose between profits for a few multinationals and the right to vaccination for all. let's make the right choice.
madam president, madam commissioner, madam minister, ladies and gentlemen, the istanbul convention was signed in may 2011. turkey was the first country to sign it and that is why it is named after its most emblematic city. since then, 34 countries have signed and ratified it, and 45 have signed it. the european union has always wanted to be at the forefront of the fight against violence against women. it is the first legally binding international instrument to put an end to this social scourge and these glaring figures which we unfortunately see every day in the world. the defence of human rights is an intrinsic value of the european union. there is no european union without democracy and human rights. unfortunately, turkey left the istanbul convention last saturday - the night between friday and saturday - in a deliberate misinterpretation and with a complete disregard for the rule of law. it is a slap in the face to human rights and tramples on the struggle for women's rights. it also means more victims and less democracy in a country where more than 400 women are killed every day. it sends a dangerous signal to the world. we have to think about it and get back to the convention. in 2019, 440 pairs of high-heeled shoes were hung on the facade of an istanbul building. the work by turkish artist vahit tuna was made in memory of the more than 440 women murdered by their partners in recent years. how many more pairs of shoes does it take to convince the government that these human lives are at stake?
on 20 march, we learned that turkey was withdrawing from the istanbul convention 2011. the withdrawal is a significant political event, as the istanbul convention is the main european instrument for combating violence against women. it is an imperfect text whose preamble gives way to an exacerbated feminism that sets women against men, but it has the immense merit of trying to protect women against all kinds of violence, including domestic violence, rape, forced marriage and female genital mutilation. that is why i have always defended it personally. by withdrawing from the convention, turkey is therefore turning its back on the equality of men and women, which is an essential value of european civilisation. on a symbolic level, this withdrawal is a declaration of war addressed to us by erdoğan, who is clearly aligning himself with the most retrograde islamism. we must learn the lessons and abandon the process of turkey's accession to the european union once and for all. it's no use complaining, we have to act.
madam president, every year, just as in the european union, hundreds of women are killed by their partners, ex-partners and close relatives in turkey. thousands are battered, raped and beaten. after deciding to withdraw from the istanbul convention, you might argue that erdoğan does not care about this brutal violence against women, but actually it is worse. autocrats like erdoğan and his lookalikes in the european union are afraid of free and independent women. they are afraid of societies where women can freely decide about their own bodies, their money and their lives. they are afraid of the strength of women and lgbti movements and that is why they hate the istanbul convention. that is why they hate us to be protected from discrimination and violence, and that is why they attack our freedom and independence. we cannot let these autocrats win, not in turkey nor in the european union. we express our solidarity with all the feminists and queers who are standing up to this in turkey right now. but most of all we need to have the full accession, ratification and implementation of the istanbul convention all over europe.
president, the istanbul convention is the first legally binding treaty that obliges member states to prevent and combat domestic violence. and just as it turns out that violence against women has increased in recent months with alarming figures due to the lockdown, with no less than one in five women facing domestic violence in recent months, just at that moment turkey decides to withdraw from the treaty! the president of the commission has always defended the ratification of the istanbul convention as one of its top priorities. it's time to turn words into deeds. because some people want to turn back the clock. i therefore urge the commission to send a clear signal to turkey, but also to set a good example by encouraging the remaining member states to finally ratify the convention and to stop blocking european ratification. how long are we going to wait to do what needs to be done?
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the regulation to include the protection of personal data. in a post-covid world with the rapid development of artificial intelligence and the increased threat of cyberattacks, a robust and anti-phishing legal framework for data that allows our economy to flourish is vital. the gdpr has the potential to unlock the eu's greatest asset: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products. but we still have to address the persistent inconsistencies, the non-compliance and the gaps. this is not the time to throw away all the rules and start over. but it is time to make the rules work as the legislator intended. we need to look at how we can make the gdpr go hand in hand with changing technology. how can we help businesses grow in a digital age? how can we provide more guidance and support to smes? and finally, how can we better protect the privacy and data of our citizens? that's what it's about!

president, vice-president, we support the agreement on export controls on dual-use goods. we thank the rapporteur and we also express our special appreciation to mr bernd lange, who has played a very decisive role in this. the new criteria in this recast regulation ensure greater harmonisation of standards and procedures, better coordination and information flow between member states and increased transparency. the new regulation also allows for an autonomous human rights monitoring of exports of cyber-surveillance technology. we cannot continue to tolerate human rights violations and the oppression of populations through the use of european high-tech products. in this connection i am told that in this parliament there are cameras from a chinese company which is supposed to be supplying surveillance technology for the famous chinese prison camps. if that is true, it is extremely cynical and extremely inconsistent. mr vice-president, you have the task of ensuring a balanced implementation of the new regulation by strengthening the eu's competitiveness, avoiding unnecessary red tape for our businesses, safeguarding our security interests, upholding high ethical standards and promoting human rights.
mr president, the reform of the dual-use regulation is an important step forward in promoting human rights in the value-based eu trade policy. dual-use equipment is a concern, as emerging and disruptive civilian technologies are more and more frequently used by authoritarian states to suppress human rights both internally and externally. china’s military-civil fusion is very good example of this. there is now an opportunity with a more coherent and responsible eu policy also to step up on the international cooperation to promote human rights. we need coordinated action to deprive authoritarian leaders and states of technology so that they cannot be used for malign purposes. the next step might be that we need to start working more on import controls to also protect our own citizens.

the next item on the agenda is the joint debate on the recommendation for the second reading of the dan nica, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy, on the establishment of horizon europe, setting out the rules for participation in it and its dissemination (07064/2/2020 - c9-0111/2021 - 2018/0224(cod) (a9-0122/2021), the report by christian ehler, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy, the specific programme implementing horizon europe, on the framework programme for research and innovation (com (2018) 0368 - c0425/2018 - 2018/0225 (cns)), on the value of innovation and innovation in the european energy and technology area, and the report by maria graça (com (2019) 0329) - 2019/01 (cod) on the european commission's 2019-2020 programme for innovation and innovation, on the european commission's 2019-2020 programme for innovation and innovation, on the european commission's 2019-2020 programme for innovation and innovation, and on the european commission's 2019-2020 programme for energy and climate change, on the c022021-2020 (com (2019) 0321)).
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we are celebrating today nothing less than the adoption of the largest and most ambitious research programme in european history. it may even be the largest civilian research program in the world. almost a hundred billion euro budget the largest amount we have ever spent on digital innovation in europe. we will spend 35% of the budget on fighting climate change, we have completely new instruments, we have opened up new audiences, we have completely refocused on innovation. the european innovation council is an instrument which links innovation, the new generation of start-ups, with the traditional research and financial instruments. we've opened up a whole new world of possibilities. the importance of the creative industries, of artists, but also of the link between the digital sphere and creativity for innovation on climate change cannot be overstated. that we are already in the forefront of initiatives like president ursula von der leyen's new bauhaus, that we are turning climate change, from a dystopia, into a utopia, that climate change is an opportunity, that we are reflecting the green deal, the economic opportunities that are associated with tackling climate change. but still, and this is the way it is in politics these days, the glass is half full and it's half empty. we promised ourselves ten years ago - the heads of state and government solemnly declared it - that we would spend 3% of the gross national product in the european union on research and innovation, and this programme will not achieve that goal either. this is not an abstract goal; we simply have to look at the competitive situation in the world: the united states, which is highly knowledgeable about climate change, spends more than 3% of its gdp on research, china almost 5%, japan well over 3%, israel almost 5%, korea 4.6%. even the oecd average is higher than the average for research expenditure in the european union. we have to be clear that this has an impact on the programme. the programme is highly attractive; it is a successful programme for the european single market, for european competitiveness, but we must also see and explain that those who want to participate in the programme are faced with the reality that we can only cater for 9% of applications in the ict sector. in biotechnology, it is only 8%, in materials science and manufacturing, it is only 7%. we have a huge need for innovation in europe, and we are facing nothing less than transformative changes in our society. we want to reduce co2 by 50% by 2030. this means that in a decade we will have to develop new technologies that do not exist today. we must develop them, bring them to industrial maturity and introduce them into the european single market. i think it is becoming clear that on the one hand this programme exceeds europe's ambitions in research and development, but it is also becoming clear that we are facing such great challenges that a discussion such as we have had over the last three years, over three presidencies - the romanian, the german and the portuguese - and with two commissioners - i may mention carlos moedas in particular, but also mariya gabriel - and a parliament, cannot continue in this way. why not? - why not? the council had lost all ambition; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. we have really said goodbye to the ideological differences in this parliament, we have fought for research and innovation in europe. without this parliament we would have fallen far short of our ambitions, and i thank the many colleagues, but i also thank my co-rapporteur dan nica for this kind of cooperation. i think we have saved europe's ambitions in this parliament. that is a reason to be proud, but it is also a reason to look back thoughtfully. what's next? we have new challenges, which are old challenges. for the first time in two decades, we have to deal with the question of scientific freedom in europe again - the question of scientific freedom being called into question in the member states. we have to deal with the european research area. we have been discussing it for 20 years, but we have not really made progress. we are proposing to the commission and the council that they should continue this discussion, that they should also take it to the civil societies of europe, and that they should promote this project. without a common european research policy, we are falling back on the privilege of the few who can spend a lot and on the problems of the many, so to speak, who must all work together to meet the challenges of climate change, digitalisation and greater competitiveness. so today is a day to be proud, to be proud of this parliament, to thank an ambitious commissioner, but it is also a day to look to the future and say: this is not enough. we're going to have to do transformational work for the next ten years. it will be all about innovation and technology, and that is why we will also be thinking in this parliamentary term about how to find more funds and develop more ambition.

fund for the social sciences and the humanities, and this contrary to the initial proposal tabled by the european commission. we will also have a better distribution of the funds over all european countries. we have a better promotion of gender balance. funds for health research are increased and then of course, there is the part on the exclusion of direct defence research from the programme. is goed. maar heel vaak zijn het in die programma's en partnerschappen uiteindelijk multinationale bedrijven die de doorslag geven over wat er al dan niet gefinancierd wordt, met soms dramatische gevolgen. u herinnert zich waarschijnlijk de terughoudendheid – om niet te spreken van een veto – toen de europese commissie in 2017-2018 via het initiatief inzake innovatieve geneesmiddelen meer wou investeren in epidemiologisch onderzoek tegen mogelijke pandemieën. dat onderzoek was nuttig voor de maatschappij. dat was een goed voorstel, maar niet winstgevend genoeg. dus wou de industrie niet. en in het kader van een publiek-privaat partnerschap mocht zij meebeslissen over wat er met belastinggeld gebeurde. had de overheid toen doorgezet, dan hadden we misschien de schade van de pandemie kunnen beperken. maar we laten tegenwoordig privébedrijven beslissen over wat er met publieke fondsen gebeurt. om een concreet voorbeeld te nemen: farmaceutische bedrijven zijn vandaag de dag gefinancialiseerd. dat betekent dat ze zich in de eerste plaats richten op kortetermijnrendement voor aandeelhouders en niet op wat voor de volksgezondheid ook op langere termijn nuttig is. we weten het: het systeem negeert al te vaak ziekten die niet winstgevend zijn of ziekten die het meest voorkomen in ontwikkelingslanden. wereldwijd gaat slechts 10 % van de onderzoeksmiddelen naar gezondheidsbehoeften in ontwikkelingslanden, waar nochtans 90 % van alle voorkoombare ziekten zich voordoen. de prioriteiten voor onderzoek en ontwikkeling moeten veel meer bepaald worden door behoeften inzake volksgezondheid – om bij ons voorbeeld van de gezondheid te blijven – en niet door privébelangen of winstbejag. het systeem is ook niet efficiënt. in de jaren 50 kregen we voor 1 miljard dollar grofweg 40 nieuwe geneesmiddelen. vandaag is dat, geloof ik, voor een vergelijkbaar bedrag minder dan één geneesmiddel. dat is een enorme daling in innovatie en productiviteit, maar ondertussen cashen de aandeelhouders van big pharma wel. want al te vaak worden de resultaten van publiek onderzoek geprivatiseerd en dat is een tweede probleem. zelfs als we met publiek geld dingen sponsoren, dan wordt het product uiteindelijk een monopolie van bedrijven. die worden daar dan alleen eigenaar van, verkopen het ons in ruil duur terug en laten niet meer toe dat er daarna collectief verder gewerkt wordt om die kennis uit te diepen en samen vooruit te gaan als maatschappij. kijk naar die covid-19-vaccins. die betalen we ondertussen drie tot vier keer en dan zijn ze nog niet van ons. volgens mij moet er tegenover publiek geld ook publieke controle staan. gelukkig is er nu een europees burgerinitiatief “no profit on pandemic” dat die volgende stappen wil zetten in ons europese onderzoeksbeleid om volksgezondheid altijd voorrang te geven op privaat winstbejag.
president, madam president of the commission, madam minister, mr vice-president, mr michel, ladies and gentlemen, brexit remains a historic event - hopefully without any follow-up - which i still deeply regret. it is a story with only losers and, almost five years after the referendum and after a difficult process of negotiations - brilliantly led by michel barnier - this agreement puts an end to the great legal uncertainty for our citizens, for our companies and also for our prosperity. fortunately, a no-deal scenario is avoided in this way. we have made agreements in this agreement which form the basis of our future relationship and which must therefore be respected. i'm not going to be a part of this. but that's not the end of the story. the conflicts of the past few weeks prove that this deal is not a done deal. in the interests of citizens and businesses on both sides of the channel, we must continue to seek pragmatic solutions that preserve the integrity of our single european market, but also protect, preserve the good friday agreement and build on a future good relationship, a good partnership with our british friends. in the short term, i encourage the european commission and the british government to work together now to speed up the agreed customs controls and to maintain those controls in northern ireland as efficiently as possible. and i am pleased, madam president, with the commitment you have just expressed on this matter. not only can smoother customs clearance, but hopefully also agreements on phytosanitary controls, help to pull the trigger from the northern ireland powder keg and de-escalate the situation, but general freight transport will also benefit. and with that in mind, i recommend that the commission adopt the following recommendations: - first, a more thoroughgoing digitalisation of customs controls with shared data; - second, the use of a system of trusted economic operators, which means that companies that export a lot and meet certain safety standards are only subject to random checks; - pre-clearance, so that we can create green lanes; and - finally, the best possible tools to support our smes. but the departure of our british friends is not merely an economic loss. under the motto 'better a good neighbour than a distant friend', it is in our mutual interest to continue and even strengthen cooperation. indeed, michel barnier, there is more that unites us than divides us, not least in areas such as security, migration, border management, transport, research and development, foreign policy, the north sea, education. let us also work with these devolved nations who are the requesting party to continue to implement erasmus+. let us also learn from this exit. i am opposed to any form of gigantism. more europe is not always the solution to every problem, but the search for a better european union is. the eu must remain a unique, strong partnership between member states and länder that retain their identity, that decide what they do and do not do together. the eu must never become a superstate over the heads of the european peoples. this is contrary to the basic principles of subsidiarity and unity in diversity. our motto should be: adding value to cooperation, looking at shared challenges of member states, based on economies of scale, based on added value, by focusing on deepening the internal market, more trade agreements, more research and development and better protection of our borders. let us now look to the future, but not without first expressing our sincere thanks to david mcallister, to the rapporteurs, to vice-president šefčovič for his approach and good dialogue with parliament, but above all to michel barnier, who has carried out a very difficult task in an inimitable way. we are in favour of this and the accompanying resolution will be adopted.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, president of the court of auditors, ladies and gentlemen, we are closing the discharge procedure for 2019 this week. we started this process last september and i really want to thank the vast majority of european bodies for their collaboration and work - the commission, parliament, the agencies, the development funds, the joint undertakings and the other institutions. as you will have seen from the beginning of this sitting, my renew europe group was the rapporteur for the other institutions and i dealt specifically with the european ombudsman and the european economic and social committee. we did not give the discharge to the economic and social committee last year because of serious malfunctions. given the improvements in the fight against harassment, the new code of conduct, the solutions for victims of harassment, we have decided, for 2019, to grant him discharge. but the message is clear: the committee on budgetary control supports the new president of the committee, christa schweng, in her actions, her process of renewal, but we will be vigilant as to how the situation develops. as regards the services of the european ombudsman, i would like to renew our congratulations to the ombudsman and to his director, emily o'reilly, for the work they have done. i hope that in the coming months the ombudsman will be able to claim greater media visibility, as this is an essential link to advance transparency and ethics, and thus to maintain citizens' trust in the european institutions. i would like to thank the court of auditors for its work, its independence and its rigour. we have here a powerful tool and i can only advise the members of our assembly to consult regularly the work, the special reports of the court of auditors. i have often said that the court of auditors is the best ally of the european citizens and therefore of the european parliament. the discharge procedure is neither a trivial gesture nor a formality. it is a major political act that allows the management of funds, transparency, but also to sanction abuses, ineffective policies and misuse of public money. this good management is essential to maintain the credibility and legitimacy of our political project with the european taxpayers. this year, out of the 52 european bodies examined, we are not giving discharge to the council, nor to the european council - we have just spoken about this and this has been the case since 2009 - nor to the frontex agency, and we have just developed the reasons that led us to take this decision. as for frontex, we're postponing the release; it's a meeting clause we give the agency in six months. let me be clear: this is not a question of the agency's missions, let alone its existence. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the budget and the implementation of the budgetary discipline and the budgetary discipline of the union. this year, for the first time, we have had a dedicated performance report, which was one of our long-standing requests. it is one thing to have accounts free from errors and irregularities, but it is also necessary that money be invested effectively in economically viable projects, policies with significant leverage effects on growth and employment. every euro spent must be spent efficiently. our vigilance will need to be further increased in the coming years, given the implementation of the new multiannual budgetary programming and the next generation eu, with 750 billion, and given the many expenses incurred in the context of the covid-19 crisis. in this context, i welcome the fact that in recent years olaf has been strengthened and the european public prosecutor's office created. this is a first step, but we still need to make progress in tracing fraud and mismanagement. i am also concerned about the very low absorption capacity of the member states. only 9 out of 27 states have a better absorption capacity compared to 2012, the european average being just 40%. how do we get our economy back on track with numbers this low? what is the point of providing such a large amount of money if the governments are not able to use it? ladies and gentlemen, many projects are under way and must be completed quickly. data mining, with programmes such as arachne or ceeds, better transparency of funds, digitalisation of procedures, simplification of administrative rules, better training and technical assistance for administrations, but also for applicants, especially the smallest smes. the union must make progress in each of these areas. the coming years will be crucial for the european project. the management of the health crisis and its economic impacts, respect for the rule of law, implementation of the green deal, digital transition, research and innovation, the emergence of a europe of health are all topics on which we are expected to work and for which we will need to provide sufficient budgetary resources to implement these policies that are essential for our growth and our future.
on behalf of the band the left. madam president, the debate on budgetary discharges does not excite the masses and yet it allows us to affirm a political orientation that will preside over the proper functioning of our institutions. in this context, a number of us have tabled amendments to improve the functioning of our institutions. firstly, we note that many communications and working documents are available in english only, that many meetings are held without the possibility of interpretation and that this situation has worsened with the health crisis. so i know that some of you are very attached to the treaty, i could quote all the articles, as well as the basic law which imposes multilingualism and really, i insist on this because european parliamentarians must be able to express themselves in their mother tongue. this brings richness and diversity to our debates, but above all it guarantees equal quality in legislative work, and therefore more resources are needed for interpreters. i congratulate them; they are doing a great job, but there must be more of them if our legislative work is to be of quality. second point: this may seem marginal to you, but for me it is essential. our institutions consume goods and services which european companies are capable of producing. but all too often we use foreign companies in our public procurement and frankly, furnishing our offices or using all kinds of chinese-made equipment, when european companies are ready to do so, is a pity. we can make an effort and that is why i think we need to change our public procurement procedures and include in the funding programmes a minimum threshold of materials or services purchased from european companies and suppliers. it may seem superficial to you, but to me, it's essential. europe is also built by concrete actions.
i have to say, madam president, we have been happy to work on the discharge report and, in view of the many advances in transparency, we will also be in favour. however, we are facing major challenges. we have, in this period, some 290 billion uncalled for because of the lack of projects, because the member states are too slow, because the regions have problems with our legal requirements. 290 billion unused at the same time we are allocating 750 billion euros of fresh money for the reconstruction of corona. of course, solidarity is appropriate in the face of the pandemic, but this money should be invested quickly in the future of the regions. - how fast? the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. we are therefore obliged to the taxpayers and we will be very careful about recovery, because rapid crisis relief must not be at the expense of a serious approach to money. we have a great responsibility here - the commission and the court of auditors alike. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council.
madam president, citizens need to be able to rely on the fact that the money spent by eu institutions and agencies is money well spent and it is spent in a correct and careful way. this is crucial for the trust that citizens have in our work and it is why these discharge procedures are therefore so important. i would like to thank all my colleagues for the good cooperation on these reports on the other institutions. there are always politically sensitive topics involved and i’m very glad to see that we managed to find common ground on so many different issues. we spoke with one voice in condemning the harassment by a member of the european economic and social committee and the lack of support, rehabilitation and compensation for the victims, and we kept pushing for the committee to improve the rules of procedure and the code of conduct. furthermore, we agreed on recommendations and critical notes concerning important topics like geographical and gender balance, ethics and integrity, equal opportunities for people with disabilities, and fair financial compensation for trainees. as in previous years, we will not grant discharge to the council due to a continuous lack of cooperation on information exchange, which is quite frankly becoming ridiculous. we will vote on these reports this week, but our work will not end there. as discharge authority, we have a continuous duty to protect the eu budget and to tackle any misconduct within our institutions and agencies, and we will continue to do so.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, our role as european parliamentarians is to exercise democratic control over the european executive and the policies that are being pursued on behalf of our fellow european citizens. and when that executive goes astray, it's our responsibility to tell him that's unacceptable. yes, it was unacceptable that the european union was paying 20 million euros to the eritrean authorities for a road renovation project using the edf; it was unacceptable to repair roads for a dictatorship that institutionalized forced labor and oppressed its people. i am pleased, ladies and gentlemen, that the commission listened to us last year and took action to stop the second tranche of €60 million from being paid to this shameful project and to ensure that it was redistributed to eritrean refugees wherever they are. yes, it is unacceptable that the european union should stand by and watch these tragedies, the sinking of migrant boats off the coast of libya. look, just a few days ago, european and libyan authorities were informed that there were three boats of migrants in distress, and no one went to rescue them. at least 130 people were left unrescued and neither frontex, nor our national european authorities, nor the libyan coastguard - which was very close by - wanted to intervene. this incident is in addition to the many incompetencies and refusal of the libyan coast guard to conduct effective search and rescue operations. this european aid to the libyan coastguards is a disgrace to europe, especially since there is no human rights guarantee or conditionality required. we, the green group, refuse to give the european commission the 2021 discharge for the edf and ask it to stop all european funding to the libyan coastguard and to submit to parliament an honest and thorough assessment of the use of european aid.

madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs for their good cooperation on the joint undertaking files. joint undertakings are public-private partnerships that play a key role in implementing specific aspects of european policy by engaging strategic industry partners across borders and business sectors, consolidating european research and creating a powerful framework for academic and industrial research. these days, we have seen more than ever that joint ventures are of great importance when we speak about health, clean energy and economic recovery. as an example, the innovative medicines initiative is supporting projects that are making valuable contributions to global efforts to tackle the covid-19 pandemic. obviously, this result could not be achieved without an intervention on the european union scale. however, the joint undertakings should improve and review their communication strategy to ensure that the relevant stakeholders are aware of their mission activities and achievements.

president of the european court of auditors. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would like to begin by thanking you for the great support and the positive words which have been heard in the debate on the court of auditors today. i can promise that i will take this to luxembourg and pass it on to my colleagues. mrs hohlmeier raised a very crucial point in her last contribution, when she was talking about how we can be more effective and how we can be better. it has pointed out that there are up to 300 different systems in the member states - i can only confirm that. we have, of course, the problem that we have with our tests, again and again, that they are confronted with completely different systems, and these different systems are of course not compatible with a possible digital test. i would perhaps ask this house, the legislature, the commission and the council to consider - and to consider very seriously - whether a change in the financial regulation, so to speak, with a long-term perspective, could at least ensure that all those who handle european money in europe provide compatible data which can then also be used in a simple, digitised procedure. we're talking about digitization everywhere, including in the yard. we are constantly finding that we are facing considerable obstacles and problems from the very beginning, simply because of the different facts, the different systems, and that this must be fundamentally changed at some point. i do not believe that we can harmonize all of this, but i do think that it must at least be compatible. i think that is imperative and that is something that can be prescribed. i should like to say a few words about the situation of our resources, because of course today too, in the discharge declaration, as in the conference of committee chairmen, we are hearing a great many wishes in the council, just as that we should do everything anytime, please! member of the commission. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i listened with great interest to the debate and, above all, to the various requests for resources from various quarters. i could also agree with that, because the commission is called upon to implement in the end all that was agreed in the multiannual financial framework. but i think we are all equally called upon - even in the light of the limited resources that we ultimately have at our disposal - to identify potential where we can make further savings on the one hand, shifts that will ultimately enable us to actually meet our tasks. i would like to point out that there are significant untapped opportunities in the field of artificial intelligence, which we must all identify and ultimately apply. i would like to add my voice to what monika hohlmeier and president lehne have said. for example, if we are faced with the fact that there are 300 programmes in shared resource management alone, which ultimately prevent successful interoperability, we must do everything together to enforce this interoperability - i say this very deliberately - because if there is interoperability, then there is automatically a dramatic reduction in programmes, and then there are also additional possibilities to rely more on artificial intelligence, so to speak, in the processing of information and data, etc. and to use people, so to speak, where it is about interpretation, the analysis of the corresponding data, and not that we have to waste resources - in the true sense of the word - to physically get to data. i believe that we have common tasks to tackle here, and i would also like to say that we have indeed been able to achieve successes together in the negotiations on the multiannual financial framework, particularly as regards the possibility of having direct access to the final beneficiaries at european level. i regret, however, that this joint decision could not be implemented in the sectoral negotiations, and we are back to where we were before. so, in view of the limited, or rather limited, but limited financial possibilities, we all have to think about where efficiency gains are possible, which we must then ultimately use in our respective institutions. i have, in any case, instructed the commission to look into this in detail for our sector. it is a complex exercise, but i think we would be well advised to approach it in an institutional way, because ultimately we all benefit from it and the first step should be to ensure interoperability within our institutions.
acting president of the council. madam president, thank you very much for this very useful debate. i listened very carefully to the speeches of the rapporteurs and also to those of the commissioner and the president of the court of auditors. this exercise is relevant to assess how eu funds have been managed and the added value they bring to the functioning of the institutions, the european economy and, above all, the well-being of citizens. the evaluation of budget implementation is becoming increasingly important. this will be particularly relevant for the year 2020 in view of the unprecedented crisis we are facing and the additional effort that is being required in this context to verify the legality and the compliance of the implementation of the union budget. as regards the possibility of resuming negotiations with the european parliament with a view to resolving the dispute over discharge in respect of the section of the budget relating to the european council and the council, i would like to thank mr pascal durand for his frank and positive intervention, as well as mr bas eickhout and other members of parliament, and to tell them that we are ready to reach an understanding which will allow for pragmatic cooperation with the european parliament during the discharge process, while preserving the positions of principle of both institutions. we are well aware of the background to this process. the council submitted a proposal for a memorandum of understanding in 2011 and the european parliament reacted to it in 2017. the council has twice revised its initial draft in order to move closer to a compromise. however, the latest version of parliament, from may 2020, was considered a step back from previous positions of the european parliament. that said, we are ready to continue to discuss this matter in a constructive manner, considering that there are better chances of reaching an agreement if we return to the texts exchanged between our two institutions in 2018 and 2019. we agree on one thing: we must show that the union is at the service of its citizens and we must also make it work better and better. that is our common goal and i therefore thank you once again for your hard work to achieve it. thank you again.
in writing. the closure procedure is the european union's main financial control mechanism, which begins with the presentation of the annual report of the european court of auditors and ends with the adoption by the plenary of the discharge reports. it is a positive result that the use of the agricultural budget has never been so regular, according to the court's opinion. however, it is regrettable that, despite the improvement in the results of recent years, the report gave a negative opinion on the legality and regularity of union expenditure. the regularity of the implementation of the union budget shows a general deterioration towards the end of the seven-year programming period, due to the acceleration of payments and the completion of major investments. it is important that the european parliament and the committee on budgetary control exercise their financial control function as effectively as possible and do their utmost to ensure that union funds are used in a lawful and orderly manner.
deputising for the rapporteur. – madam president, for me it’s a great honour to be here and indeed i’m not vilija blinkevičiūtė, who is the rapporteur of this file, but she is ill at the moment, so she asked me to deliver her speech. so i’m going to do this as best i can. so please bear with me and pretend that i’m vilija. i think here today we are here to discuss the european globalisation adjustment fund for displaced workers (egf) and, for health reasons, i’m not able to be here with you today, but my colleague ms jongerius will present my position as parliament’s rapporteur. to start, i would like to express my gratitude and appreciation to everyone who was involved in the egf negotiations. it was thanks to your efforts and your dedication that we were able to achieve an excellent result. so a big thanks goes to all my colleagues, all the shadow rapporteurs, of course, also maria arena, who was a rapporteur during the previous mandate, commissioner schmit, and the colleague from the commission, the croatian, the german presidency and the current council team and, of course, to all the staff. i’m convinced that the outcome of the negotiations and the agreement that we found with the council will further strengthen the egf and its very important instruments to help dismissed workers. this will be crucial in the difficult times to come, with the immense social and economic effects of the covid-19 pandemic. many people have lost their jobs already and it’s our job to help them to find new employment. the new egf is far better equipped to respond to these challenges. let me briefly remind you of the most significant achievements. firstly, the egf interventions will no longer be exclusively linked to globalisation or an economic or financial crisis, but will cover any restructuring events in the future of a company, a sector or a region. this change will make the egf far more accessible than before. we have managed to introduce a threshold of dismissed workers from currently 500 to 200, which allows more dismissed workers to benefit from the egf matches, therefore increasing their chances to return to employment again. we expect that both the increased scope and the lower threshold will result in an increase of applications, and the egf has an annual ceiling of eur 186 million to provide for support. much to my regret, the member states were not willing to agree on a higher ceiling as demanded by parliament and the commission, not even in the light of the current crisis. we were, however, successful in securing a childcare allowance, among other special time-limited measures to support dismissed workers. i think it’s of extreme importance, as it will have also a practical importance in daily life for people with children who are already struggling at the moment. they can rest assured that they have the means to provide for their family while participating in reskilling or any other vocational training or education activities supported by the egf. we also increased the amount of financial support to eur 20 000 for dismissed workers who wish to start their own businesses, and we hope it will provide a good incentive for entrepreneurship. last but not least, the new name of the egf is from now on the european globalisation fund for displaced workers. it has been changed to underline the true mission of this fund, namely to support the workers who lost their jobs and to avoid any misunderstandings of the past that the fund supports companies that lay off workers. to wrap up, i really am convinced and proud that for the next seven years we have reinforced the egf, which is much better equipped to support people who have lost their jobs and to return them to sustainable employment. just as a last sentence on behalf of myself, on behalf of our whole political group, the s&d group, i really want to thank vilija for all her hard work on this important file. she did a great job and she managed to get support for even more workers throughout europe and to help them to return to sustainable employment in these times of need, and i think this is very much appreciated, so thank you for that, vilija.
member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank the european parliament, in particular the rapporteur ms blinkevičiūtė, for all the efforts to conclude the negotiations at the end of last year. the commission supports the overall agreement that has been reached in the trilogue. since 2007 the european globalisation fund (egf) has received 169 applications. some eur 636 million have been mobilised to help about 155 workers in 21 member states. the average reintegration rates of the 2014—2020 period were around 60%, which is a great achievement considering that the egf is assisting the most vulnerable groups of displaced workers. and now, more than ever, it is essential that the egf provides continued support to workers who have lost their jobs in the current challenging economic and social situation. at the end of 2020 we received six applications, among them an application from estonia aimed at 5 000 people who lost their jobs in the tourism industry. they fall under the previous regulation. not later than today, the commission proposes eur 1.1 million to support 500 foundry workers in germany, and there’s another proposal by the commission of eur 3.7 million to support nearly 1 500 dismissed airport workers in belgium, and we all know that for the time being airports and aviation are in a very critical situation. all this shows that the need for a fund like the egf that demonstrates european solidarity with displaced workers who fall victim to the economic downturn; structural changes are absolutely necessary. this is even more important in the current juncture where we need to mitigate the impact of the covid-19 crisis. we did well to broaden the scope of the fund. the new egf will enable more workers to benefit from it in a simpler way. it will no longer be conditional on the cause of restructuring which has led to their dismissal. the threshold has been reduced to at least 200 workers made redundant, currently 500. on a proposal from parliament we also included a provision in case the fund’s resources are exhausted at year end in order to avoid any funding gaps. the revamped egf will thus be more flexible, allowing the union to react to economic challenges the future might bring. this is of paramount importance in a fast-changing world undergoing major technological transitions. the egf complements the support offered by the european social fund which can be programmed in advance and offers support in an anticipatory way. both the egf and the esf contribute to the implementation of the european pillar of social rights. we need to use all tools available to preserve and strengthen social cohesion, especially in times of crisis. with the objective of building a strong social europe we have put forward the european social pillar action plan setting out ambitious goals and concrete measures which should also guide the recovery strategy. there is only one point where the commission would have liked to go a bit further. the commission considers that the text on the single data mining tool is not sufficient to enhance the protections of the union budget against fraud and irregularities. we already made this point at the trilogue negotiations. we have issued a statement in this regard in our communication on the council position. the commission is very much looking forward to continue the excellent cooperation with the european parliament and the council, and to ensure that the european globalisation adjustment fund for displaced workers expresses the solidarity of the european union with people dismissed due to major restructuring events, contributing to enhancing social cohesion and ultimately to the resilience of our economy and society.

member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, first of all let me welcome your very good report on digital taxation because, of course, we are in a crisis that makes the taxation of digital businesses and global minimum taxation even higher priorities – both in order to secure much needed tax revenues for the recovery and to ensure that everyone contributes their fair share. this is even more relevant for large digital companies, who are among the real winners in this crisis – higher profits, larger market share – while most traditional businesses have been severely affected. so we will redouble our efforts to reach an agreement at global level – both on the reallocation of taxing rights and on minimum effective taxation – by mid—2021, because a global solution is of course the best way to address these challenges. in this domain, we welcome the recent decision of the us to withdraw their ‘safe harbour’ proposal. we know that the us are also proposing a few new ideas to simplify and widen the scope of pillar one to capture the largest and most profitable multinationals worldwide. an agreement on pillar one along these lines, covering the global share of the taxable base of six or seven digital companies, should not prevent the eu and its member states from adopting a digital levy, since this has a different scope and is needed to restore a level playing field in the single market and contribute to financing the recovery and the resilience of the eu economy. regarding the project for the other pillar – global minimum effective taxation – the recent announcement by the us government on a possible us tax reform will also bring a new dynamic. the new administration is in favour of a minimum effective tax rate that is higher than the one previously discussed at the oecd. so, undoubtedly, work still needs to be done to bridge differences, but recent us announcements and the ongoing work on the oecd blueprints create a good chance for a political agreement at g20 level in the coming months and we must maintain momentum. from the european commission’s point of view, the two pillars under discussion are, and should remain, a package. their objectives are complementary and we need a solution on both of them to provide a comprehensive answer to the tax challenges of modern economies. at the same time, the commission services are working on a proposal for the digital levy, which will also serve as the basis for a new eu own resource. this stems, as you know, from the clear mandate from the european council last year, which is also enshrined in the interinstitutional agreement on the multiannual financial framework (mff) of last december. we will be ready to table this proposal in mid-2021, as agreed, with the aim to make it operational from 2023 onwards. let me reassure you that finding a global solution remains the key priority for the commission. this is why we will work to develop a design that does not interfere with the oecd process. there are ways to do this so that we meet eu objectives and actively support the oecd. moreover, we will ensure that the design of such a digital levy is not discriminatory and thus fully in line with the eu international obligation under world trade organization (wto) agreements. the digital levy should not fuel trade tension in any way. the commission will also ensure that the design of the digital levy is compatible with the eu’s key policy objective of supporting and accelerating the digital transition. in conclusion, this terrible and extraordinary crisis could pave the way for new progress on global and digital taxation. i’m sure that we will work together to seize this opportunity.

president. - ladies and gentlemen, nobody likes to pay taxes, but they are necessary because they finance, among other things, the education of our children, the care of our sick, the pensions of our elderly. but taxes must be fair. that means they have to be balanced and proportionate. everyone has to do their part. and that's not the case now. a single flemish worker, for example, pays more than half of his salary in taxes. this is in stark contrast to the digital companies that pay barely 9.5% in taxes. through all sorts of tax structures, these digital and multinational companies are deliberately evading their contribution. they want to do business everywhere, but nowhere to pay taxes. this is socially irresponsible, undermines solidarity and means unfair competition with small and mediumsized enterprises. the coronavirus crisis has exacerbated this injustice. families and local businesses are having a very hard time. big tech, however, reported $38 billion in profits last quarter alone, and they're still trying to keep taxes down. the pleasures are for a few super-rich shareholders, the burdens for the community. that's a parasitic model. we therefore support the idea that digital companies should contribute and pay taxes in the countries where they operate, and not where they happen to be headquartered. what we do not support, however, is that the european union
