the debate on the recommendation for second reading of the committee on industry, research and energy on the council position at first reading with a view to the adoption of a regulation of the european parliament and of the council establishing the european defence fund and repealing regulation (eu) 2018/1092 (06748/1/2020 c9 0112/2021 - 2018/0254 (cod)) (rapporteur: zdzisław krasnodębski (a9-0120/2021) is closed.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i am very pleased that after almost three years of work over two terms, after five trialogue meetings with the participation of three council presidencies, after countless meetings of parliament's co-rapporteurs, whose composition has also evolved considerably over time, we are now faced with the opportunity to conclude the legislative procedure at second reading. in this way, i hope we will create a european defence fund for the years 2021-2027. however, i would like to start by recalling what the fund is and what it is not as proposed by the european commission in may 2018, because various myths have arisen in this regard positive and negative. the edf is not the first step towards a european army, and the money from its budget will not be used by the member states to buy arms together. the dream of creating the first european legion or any other permanent military formation within the union must be postponed. maybe someday we'll make that dream come true. the almost €8 billion that eventually came into the fund is indeed earmarked for the union's defence policy, but only for research and development projects carried out jointly by all the member states. it should also be noted that the edf will not subsidize bilateral cooperation between countries, since the minimum number of participants in a project financed is three entities from three member states. moreover, the fund aims not only to support the r&d projects of the european defence industry, but also to bring new players into the cooperation network and supply chain, including those who have not been active in the industry before. another important point: small and medium-sized enterprises and mid-caps will be able to participate in the fund on preferential terms. another very important point to be emphasised is that the aim is to guarantee equal conditions of participation for operators from all member states. the fund will enable member states to save between eur 25 billion and eur 100 billion a year. this is obviously a considerable sum, considering how much the member states have spent so far on their nato alliances. in addition, it is of course an opportunity to develop an industrial base and access advanced technologies through cooperation with foreign partners. for countries with less developed defence industries, this is one of the biggest advantages of the fund. next, it is important to realize where the dangers are. the first danger is that the member states will be able to buy the products that will be developed under this fund, otherwise this fund will be nothing but a piece of art for a piece of art. it is important to emphasise here that the european commission will play a significant role through the newly created directorate-general for defence and space industries (dg defis), because it is important what programmes the commission will choose. we as the european parliament will be watching the implementation of the edf and in the next term of office, if this fund does not fulfil its tasks and the expectations that were attached to it, especially in terms of fair and transparent distribution of the fund's resources, we will demand its review. some of you are hesitant to support our deal with the council for ideological reasons. well, i would like to tell them that the big success and the major change compared to the partial predecessor of the edf european defence industrial development programme (edidp) was the elimination by the parliament of ethical controls on the funded projects, including much more restrictive rules on technologies that will not be able to receive funding from the fund. i remember how long we have been discussing this and i appreciate all the more the concessions made by the council in this respect. i would also like to stress that i am convinced that the ongoing rivalry between china and the united states, as well as the volatile situation on our southern and eastern borders (which we had the opportunity to discuss yesterday with the high representative), mean that the union should attach increasing importance to common defence and, consequently, increase spending in this area. but we must always remember that nato and strong transatlantic ties remain the foundation of european security, and that is not going to change anytime soon, but we should be doing much more as europeans to defend ourselves. in conclusion, i would like to stress that the fund's sole purpose is to support joint european projects in the research and development phase. in the vote in the itre committee, the outcome of our negotiations was supported by a large majority of members. in order to preserve the agreement with the council, i ask you to reject all the amendments tabled in plenary and to vote for the text as we have negotiated it.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr rapporteur, i am very pleased to be able to speak in this house about defence, and in particular, of course, about the european defence fund. first of all, i would like to thank mr krasnodębski for his work as rapporteur for the itre committee and of course also mr cristian buşoi, the chairman of the committee, as well as all the fictitious rapporteurs and presidencies who have accompanied you. it is a long-term task, of course, which we are discussing together in this house this morning, and i would really like to thank all those who played an important role, particularly during the previous mandate, in bringing the ambition of the european defence fund to the baptismal font, in particular president juncker. the european defence fund is a turning point in our european integration. we know that in europe we have different defence cultures, and we also have different attitudes towards defence. however, by listening, respecting all sensitivities and sharing a common understanding of global issues, we were able to go beyond this and reach agreement on a budgetary programme for industrial cooperation in the field of defence. i am not afraid to say that this is a truly historic step. the european defence fund is a key milestone in the realisation of a 'europe of defence'; it marks a new stage in our strategic interdependence and our capacity to build bridges for a genuine european ambition. without compromising our historic alliances, europe can no longer be forced to make choices made by others, either because of too much dependence or because of the absence of an acceptable alternative. this is the case in health, we have seen it clearly and covid-19 has reminded us of some of our vulnerabilities that we were able to quickly compensate for, very fortunately; the same is true in terms of technology, with for example semiconductors or the cloud. here too, we will compensate for our fragility. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'commercial vehicle'. and so it is with questions of defence capability. this is especially true in view of the stresses of today's world. europe must be stronger, more resilient and more strategically autonomous. it should be able to gradually supplement its soft power arsenal with more and more hard power attributes. it must be able to take its own security gradually into its own hands and be a player in global security. the european defence fund is thus an essential contribution to greater european sovereignty. it is a vital lever in the defence of our citizens, but also of our interests and values. it is also the basis for our strengthening as a credible partner, particularly vis-à-vis our allies, for example in nato, because the european defence fund, which we have built together, has several key elements and i would like, if you will allow me, to highlight a few of them this morning. first of all, the fund was designed to be a strictly european fund, which should benefit all member states. this is obviously the key to its success and, you can trust me, i will be particularly careful. it also organises european industrial cooperation by imposing criteria such as the number of countries associated with each project financed and also by supporting the integration of small and medium-sized enterprises for each of these projects. it provides real incentives for the opening up of supply chains in order to build genuine european defence industrial chains. it is fully consistent with other defence initiatives such as pesco and eacd. with a budget of €7 billion, it will focus on structuring capacities and technologies in line with priorities defined by the member states. although it is restricted to europeans, it also allows cooperation with non-european entities, subject to certain conditions. finally, it organises the development of disruptive technologies, since 8% of the budget will be devoted to disruptive innovations through open calls for proposals to attract new players, including start-ups. this is therefore a real ambition that we are already pursuing through many of the fund's flagship programmes - the european defence industrial development programme and the preparatory action on defence research. we are already financing 34 high impact and structural projects in the technological and military fields and are finalising the evaluation of the last call for proposals, but the figures for the edip are clear: more than 200 entities in 25 member states benefit from the fund, of which 40% are smes and nine of the 16 projects financed are also pesco projects; the preparatory action on defence research is funded by the european union through 200 entities from 22 member states plus norway. these pioneering programmes therefore show that we have already built a functioning market and will of course continue to do so. ladies and gentlemen, i would like to conclude by saying that with the european defence fund we are clearly changing the paradigm of defence cooperation at european level. the stakes are high for europe's place on the world stage and, of course, you can count on my full commitment to achieve this ambition.
i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community, but that we should be concerned about the future of the european union. the creation of a common fund to support collaboration and cooperation between different companies and different countries in a field such as defence, which is so characterized by national specificities and is also anchored in differences between states, is a very important step towards political integration and an integration which, i hope, will also contribute to the much-desired common defence policy. and it is in this perspective that i believe we should value this measure. in doing so, we encountered two particular challenges, namely translating the projects that were previously experimental into a real fund: the first challenge, as the commissioner said, was to find a balance between the different countries, between the different sizes of industrial operators in the field, in other words, between large and small operators. an agreement that was broad-based but at the same time efficient, capable of producing results. the second challenge is ethical. i'm sorry you called it "ideology" this morning. ethics is not ideology. ethics is ethics, and i think that's very important in this business. the fund, as all european industrial policies, puts competitiveness at the centre, it has been said, a competitiveness which must lead to the creation of new start-ups, but at the same time also to the development of smes, which we do not want to be marginalised in this development process, which is carried out mainly by the large operators. it is also important for achieving the strategic autonomy that has been called for, and it is no accident that we have worked so hard to ensure that the presence and participation were by consortia that include at least three countries and at least three industrial entities and refer to enlargement, not to a restriction to a few samples or a few countries that are already very strong in the field. this concept of enlargement contributes, as the commissioner said, to europe's strategic autonomy but also to the creation of a conception of our industrial and innovation policies as continental policies which are beneficial for all countries. finally, it is very important to say what the fund will do and what it will not do, because it is very clear - and it must be said loud and clear - that it will not be able to finance weapons production, let alone actions for the development of lethal autonomous weapons without the possibility of human control. and i think these are very important aspects. my group considers them very important. i was not involved in the negotiations from the beginning - i took over from mr martin - but i think these are key points, because we have worked on these aspects and we have made progress in the negotiations on these points. but perhaps we could have done more, commissioner. i say to my colleagues and i say to you: we could also have made progress which would not have hindered the effectiveness and value of this design, of this innovation, but would have gathered a consensus, perhaps even a wider consensus, in parliament. there are, in fact, amendments tabled which make demands which we will accept in the implementing part but we cannot accept them now. it was a tough negotiation. we must support the agreement reached and it would be useless to reopen it: but those themes remain. i therefore ask you, commissioner, and i commit my group to constant attention and monitoring, so that the operation is a success, so that the results we are aiming at are achieved in the common european interest, but also so that these extremely important aspects - and we see this in this period of great conflicts - are always scrupulously observed in the implementation phase.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr zdzisław krasnodębski, for the long work we have done together over two parliaments. today, by voting for the european defence fund, parliament is opening a new page in the construction of europe, a page which all europeans have had in mind since the 1950s and which takes us back to the genesis of the project that binds us together. what clearer expression of our desire to build a geopolitical union than cooperation in such a highly sovereign field. of course, the text does not live up to our budgetary expectations and hopes. at the end of the previous term, i stood in this house to celebrate the fact that the sum of 13 billion euros has now fallen to less than 8 billion, and we must be satisfied with that. but more than the envelope or the technical subtleties of the text, it is the symbol of a europe that innovates, cooperates and protects what we are creating. it is also a sign of maturity in our relationship with the world. in the last 15 years europe has been through various crises and there is a fear that the coming years will force us to draw on natural resources that we had hoped we would never use again. when that day comes, we can rejoice that we have been given the means to stand up for ourselves against our enemies and our allies, that is, for our sovereignty. saint-exupéry said that the future is always only the present to be put in order: we do not have to foresee it, but to allow it. my dear colleagues, by laying the foundation stone for a defence europe, we can put this maxim into practice.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the virus. they celebrated five years of continuous increases in defense spending. for example, did you know that france and germany alone today spend more on defense than russia combined? can you imagine that? we in europe are not spending too little on defence, we are spending too much on defence. and when it comes to defending ourselves, that's one thing, but to go to war abroad ourselves - we've seen it from libya to afghanistan - that only creates more chaos. meanwhile, our health care system is totally underfunded, our hospitals are under pressure, our health care staff is totally understaffed. and that's the result of political choices, choices to cut back on public services, choices to give those billions to big military corporations instead of investing in health care. these choices cost lives, in europe and far beyond. and what are you doing now, in the middle of a pandemic, in the middle of a health crisis? you are setting up a new european defence fund worth billions: the subsidy slush for the arms multinationals. we don't need another arms race. no, we need peace. put that money into public services, public services, re-industrialization. please invest in health, not war.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. – madam president, as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco), we in the committee think that there are many good points in the final report. for example, the inclusion of passages on the freedom to provide services across the union, the freedom of workers to move around and the digitalisation of data exchanges between member states. i would also like to thank the committee on employment and social affairs for its cooperation in trying to incorporate as much as possible from our imco opinion. i know there have been some technical difficulties. however, personally, i would also like to take this moment to underline the importance of a free and functioning internal market. there are elements in this report that suggest that a free and functioning internal market is incompatible with fair working conditions and this is, in my opinion, simply not true. personally, i therefore cannot endorse the full report as it stands now.
member of the commission. – madam president, let me start by thanking the rapporteur, mr kanev, and the committee on employment and social affairs (empl) for this important report on labour mobility in the european union. today’s discussions are under the best auspices. during the social summit in porto, a new commitment was made to reinforce the social dimension of the eu. the leaders of the eu institutions, heads of state and government, and the european social partners and civil society organisations recognise that the european pillar of social rights action plan is our way forward and endorse the new eu—level 2030 targets on employment, skills and poverty reduction. labour mobility is a crucial element in the action plan and is at the heart of a well-functioning single market. i agree that mobility, the freedom of services, is a fundamental freedom of the european union; but it has to be accompanied by fair working conditions. it has to be fair, and it has to be just for everyone. we have also seen that the freedom of mobility is not an acquis which cannot be called into question, as it has been called into question by the covid—19 crisis, which really brings new challenges. this crisis has highlighted the importance of ensuring the free movement of workers, in particular essential workers. it has also unveiled the harsh reality of strained working and living conditions, especially of seasonal workers. the commission has been working to ensure that the measures to mitigate the health emergency are non-discriminatory and proportionate. in march 2020, we issued guidance to safeguard the free movement of critical workers. in july, we presented guidelines on seasonal workers in order to stress the rights of seasonal workers, including information and social security, and to ensure decent working and living conditions by addressing occupational safety and health, accommodation and transport. the posting of workers is another important element of free movement, as you have also highlighted in your reports. the revised posted workers directive ensures a level playing field between local companies and those providing their services across borders. that is why the posted workers directive is so essential in the context of the internal market. it guarantees a core set of equal rights for posted workers and local workers, for instance as regards minimum rates of pay, conditions of accommodation, and hiring through temporary work agencies. we are monitoring closely how this directive has been transposed into national legislation. the modernisation of the social security coordination rules will also contribute to facilitating free movement and protecting the rights of mobile citizens. in this context, i also hope that, with your support, the co—legislators will find an agreement on the revised rules proposed by the previous commission. looking ahead, in 2022 we will examine the working conditions of posted workers in subcontracting chains and others working across borders, including third—country nationals. this is a request the parliament has put forward in resolutions and that i am keen to follow up. we need to protect and enhance mobile workers’ rights, while ensuring fair mobility based on a level playing field and the responsiveness of labour markets. a key novelty is the establishment of the european labour authority (ela). still in its build-up phase, results are already tangible with better access to information and inspections. ela is launching an action plan on seasonal workers within an awareness—raising campaign informing seasonal workers about their rights. labour mobility is one way of matching labour market needs and skills. the eures network, an online platform connecting public employment services, employers and job seekers, is at the centre of the european labour market. at the same time, we also have to make sure people can develop the skills that are needed in their regions. this is a precondition for a fair and resilient recovery and successful green and digital transitions. the european skills agenda sets out concrete actions to empower individuals to learn, including the upcoming initiatives on individual learning accounts. with the pact for skills, we are engaging public and private organisations to join forces to upskill and reskill people in europe, and this paves the way to reaching the target of at least 60% of adults participating in training every year by 2030. i welcome your resolution and this debate. we are striving to achieve the same goal: ensuring that the rights of mobile workers are protected and eu rules are enforced, and that a fair mobility is safe.
the european parliament has also called for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the european union's social policy is not disrupted by the coronavirus. at the beginning of the pandemic, internal borders were suddenly closed and workers were left to fend for themselves. europe was rocked by harrowing stories of workers caught in the crosshairs of the social security system, between their country of work and their country of origin. workers were denied access to their workplaces and others were isolated abroad without access to social security. infected workers were expelled from their countries of work and found to be uninsured for health care in their home countries. this is unacceptable. let the current coronavirus crisis be the ultimate warning for the strong social protection of all workers in europe. europe needs social security coordination without rancor. it is therefore incomprehensible that the negotiations are stalled. the free movement of workers must no longer be a pretext for social dumping. these abuses must be stopped. our employees deserve better and this report is a good start.

the european parliament has already expressed its views on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. files at the italian or french border posts are long gone. employees who cross the border to work in another member state also benefit from these benefits. the same applies to my region, flanders, where there are more and more workers from other member states. they work in key sectors such as healthcare, agriculture and logistics. the current pandemic has made it even clearer to us how important these people are. without them we would not have a full health service and the flemish asparagus would be rotting in the fields. unfortunately, there are also downsides. the lack of people in our labour market cannot be solved by taking the brightest minds from other member states. this could undermine the social cohesion of the union. we need to solve the disparities in prosperity between regions by creating jobs where people have no prospects today. unfortunately, the fact that workers move to another member state is often abused to circumvent fundamental labour rules. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. as soon as a worker crosses the border, the fundamental rules of work are put on the line. it's got to be different. in europe, all workers have the right to good working conditions, a decent wage and full social protection. europe must therefore be much more active in ensuring that the rules are respected. only then can we achieve a social europe with fair jobs for all.

in writing. before the second world war, relations between estonia and turkey were special. at a time when turkey had only 20 missions abroad, one of them was in tallinn. turkey never recognized the forcible incorporation of estonia, latvia and lithuania into the soviet union. turkey is an important partner of estonia in nato. turkish planes have secured our airspace. turkish society is european. turkey is a country of strategic importance in political, economic and foreign policy terms. the european union has cooperated effectively with turkey in the migration crisis. turkey, like any sovereign state, pursues its own interests and aims, but must of course respect fundamental human rights and democratic values. the eu wants good relations with turkey. unfortunately, these relations are now at an all-time low. the merger process is in place. to improve relations, both parties must use respectful language. the opposite approach is only beneficial to those who seek to divide turkey and the eu.
in writing. – i have supported the ep report on turkey since it provides critical yet fair overview and vision for the future. this is the strongest ep position on our relations on turkey ever adopted by the european parliament. the accession negotiations between the eu and turkey are in serious danger as long as turkey refuses to carry out reforms and undermines the rule of law, human rights and media freedom. unfortunately, currently, it is the case; therefore, the accession talks with turkey must be formally suspended. this should allow for the eu and turkey to review the current relations and to explore possible new models for future relations. also the question of visa liberalisation for turkish citizens and the modernisation of the customs union must be linked to democratic progress, human rights and fundamental freedoms in turkey. meanwhile we should still keep in mind that turkey is a member of nato and there is a lot of room for successful cooperation.
in writing. i do not understand the continuation of the current accession negotiations with turkey led by the erdoğan government. turkey no longer meets the conditions of democracy, the rule of law and convergence towards european standards that are implied by its status as a candidate country for eu membership. worse still, turkey has been moving further and further away from this in recent years. the time is not now to commit ourselves to offering turkey some advantage such as the modernisation of the customs union. today's vote on a possible suspension of the accession negotiations is only a first step. the commission and the high representative are to recommend this suspension as early as the june european council if no significant progress is made. finally, if the situation does not change by the end of 2021, i believe that the accession negotiations will have to be definitively stopped and wider sanctions, including in the context of the customs union, will have to be developed. we can't let mr. erdoğan play games with us anymore!

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank you for this debate. in the meantime, the extraordinary meeting of foreign ministers called by the high representative and vice-president of the commission, josep borrell, has come to an end. he has also just communicated, through the press conference, the broad majority view expressed by the member states at that meeting. let me try to sum up the debate in my view on four essential points. the first is a very strong condemnation of the escalation of violence that has been witnessed in recent days in israel and the gaza strip, the lament for the countless civilian casualties, including countless children on both sides of the conflict, and the consequent call for an immediate ceasefire, an immediate cessation of hostilities so that the political process can prevail over violence and military confrontation. the second point is an unequivocal condemnation, without any ambiguity, of the launching of rockets by the terrorist organization, in our entirely legitimate classification, the launching of rockets, i said, by the terrorist organization hamas on civilian populations residing in israel, and the consideration, the support for israel's right to defend itself, to defend its territory, to defend its populations, a right to defend itself exercised in a proportionate manner and in compliance with international humanitarian law. that's how the right of defence should be exercised. we therefore call on israel, in exercising its right to self-defence, to act in a proportionate manner and in accordance with international humanitarian law. the third point is the relative point that we should not forget the reasons for the social and urban violence that broke out earlier in east jerusalem. this conflict must not be ignored and we must also be firm in condemning the forced displacement of palestinian families living in one of the neighbourhoods of east jerusalem and we must also be firm in our defence of the status quo with regard to the holy sites, with regard to al-haram al-sharif, or the temple mount, and in defence of the freedom of worship of all the religions represented there. finally, a fourth point, which means a call for a return to the political process, for the relaunching of the peace process, because only the peace process can guarantee a lasting and stable solution for the region, based on two independent states living peacefully side by side, and the defence of the european union's commitment to this political process, a european union in which anti-semitism cannot have any kind of place.

president, commissioner, the end of the pandemic tunnel is gradually approaching and our vaccination campaigns are beginning to bear fruit. the prospect of a post-corona world is coming closer, and i am therefore very pleased that we can vote today on the reinforced solidarity corps, which is of symbolic importance. our young people have shown a great deal of solidarity over the past year. they are generally less vulnerable to the virus, but they have sacrificed a lot to protect their most vulnerable fellow human beings. the crisis has shown more than ever the importance of solidarity, not only between countries but also between citizens. the success of the current vaccination campaigns is due in part to the many volunteers who have worked on them. the solidarity corps is therefore much more than a european fund. it offers 300 000 young people across europe the opportunity to volunteer in another country, learning another language, learning a new culture and showing solidarity. after many frustrating months of social isolation, they finally have the prospect of spreading their wings. european solidarity is the foundation on which the european union is built and on which we must build in the coming years with initiatives of this kind.
for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, more than ever we are unequal in the face of death. at present, hundreds of millions of people are still denied a vaccine that could save their lives, simply because they were born in the wrong place. the european union is of course not responsible for managing the health crisis outside its borders. while it can be proud to export 50% of its vaccine production, it would be shocking and undignified to see it continue to block internationally the suspension of patents on covid-19 vaccines, a measure that would allow for rapidly increasing production. if it were to put the right to profit above humanitarian law, in order to preserve the profit margins of big pharma rather than human life, the european union would be denying its values, starting with the protection and respect of human dignity. the pharmaceutical industry does not need our help, however, because it is in a very bad financial state. so pfizer, johnson & johnson and astrazeneca spent $26 billion on dividend payments and share buybacks last year alone. it is time for europe to see vaccines as a global commons, the quantity of which is available and the prices of which cannot be left to the discretion of three or four pharmaceutical groups. i have already heard some people say that the removal of patents on vaccines would be useless because poor countries would not be able to produce the doses. first of all, let me remind you that some european laboratories went from zero capacity to producing several million doses per week in just a few months. and then, all over the world, pharmaceutical companies exist and announce the ability to produce vaccines, but are denied a license to produce by the big labs. others go so far as to claim that patent suspension would threaten the private sector's ability to innovate in the future. this is a relief when you know that public research and public funding are largely behind the most effective covid-19 vaccines. once again, the profits would be privatised, while a substantial part of the costs was borne by the community. the suspension of patents is only part of the solution. it must be accompanied by a transfer of technology and know-how. if big pharma refuses to share their knowledge on a voluntary basis, the european commission will have to make a decision to force them to do so. continuing to deny other countries access to the tools to fight covid-19 would not only be a moral failure, it would also be a serious policy mistake. the longer we delay immunizing people in developing countries, the greater the risk of new variants emerging against which current vaccines would be ineffective. so our leaders must choose between winning the support of shareholders or the support of citizens, between short-term profit and global public health. it is in these choices of society that the real nature of the european project will be revealed.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). competitive research enables us to develop well-functioning and effective vaccines at a rapid pace, and to develop vaccines that hold great promise for the future. intellectual property is the fruit of years of intensive research and costly effort and creates tens of thousands of jobs, many of them in research and development. there is no evidence that the abolition of patents will lead to faster, higher production and a higher vaccination rate. the production of vaccines is a highly complex matter. the production and quality control alone would take so much time that no more lighting could be brought in 2021. the solution lies in dramatically increasing our production. this year's production alone, of 10 to 11 billion doses, would be sufficient in this regard, but optimists expect even surpluses in 2022. in the west, more than ten doses per adult have been ordered. nevertheless, the eu is the only one that exports vaccines. the large quantities of production will have to be exported to developing countries at cost or free of charge. of course, these must be licensed and the existing rules on compulsory licensing may be invoked where appropriate. however, this does not mean that the vaccines have been administered. i therefore call on the council and the commission to make arrangements with the western countries as soon as possible, not only to export surplus vaccines to developing countries as soon as possible, but also to provide large-scale human and logistical support for the administration of the vaccines in countries which do not have the necessary resources themselves.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i want to say without any further ado: release the patents! it's not just the five star movement that i represent that is asking for it, but the whole world: president biden, the pope, the who, but most of all, the majority of european citizens. she says it 's complicated . of course it is! but complexity is addressed, not avoided, especially when lives are at stake. only 9 percent of the world's population has been vaccinated, 1 percent in poor countries. every day we delay is a gift to the virus, an opportunity to create variants that can turn back the clock to march 2020 and start us all over again. because one thing must be clear: if the world doesn't get out, neither will we. and so you just pretend to protect innovation, when really you're just protecting shareholders and dividends. big pharma has received tens of billions of dollars from the public for research, and we've paid for these vaccines twice: first to develop them, then to buy them before we even knew if they worked. zero risk and guaranteed profit for big pharma, while we've suffered secret contracts, delays and cuts. so i say vaccines are a public good: they belong to the human race, they belong to all of us. and if we want to get out of this nightmare, we have to vaccinate the whole world, force manufacturers to stop making money off the pandemic. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. does he mean that he cannot force some multinational corporation not to speculate on life? we 're releasing the patents on the vaccine and the machinery to produce it immediately: let's release them now!
mr president, two types of vaccines have been developed against covid: one, traditional, and the other, based on a new technology that could change the treatment with which many diseases are fought today, including some cancers, known as messenger rna, and which is european. in the second case, the patent would also expropriate a long research work and the key to all future applications. the companies that have developed messenger rna technology in europe are small and have been in the industry for less than 20 years. if they are now forced to share their work with chinese, indian or russian pharmaceutical companies, for example, not only will their work be ruined, but also the european leadership in one of the few fields in which we still maintain it. and the countries that need them the most will still not manufacture vaccines. they're getting shot, not patents. the shortage of vaccines is not caused by patents, but by insufficient production. we are facing an international humanitarian emergency. we are therefore encouraging agreements between companies to increase the number of vaccines they produce, but when it comes to something as strategic as public health, we are protecting our researchers from the ambitions of russia or china, which, by the way, will never share their advances. let's make vaccines for everyone, but let's not risk not knowing who makes ours.

mr president, there are nearly ten thousand deaths a day because there are not enough vaccines. when the european left warned of this a year ago, we stood alone and were looked down upon by the european commission. however, thanks to our campaign and pressure from below, this issue is now at the top of the agenda of the european parliament. we urgently need more vaccines, but production is limited by patents. these patents give one or a few companies the exclusive right to produce vaccines, creating a monopoly. the companies then decide how much or how little they want to produce: billions in profits for them, vaccine shortages for us. it is said that patents are not the problem and that the abolition of patents will hinder innovation. however, modern mrna vaccines have been developed at universities, and pharmaceutical companies have received billions in government subsidies. innovation is a collective success. pharmaceutical companies are raking in billions in patent profits while people are losing their lives because there are not enough vaccines. take those patents away.

the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the tereos sugar factory in northern france has caused a major ecological disaster by breaking a dam in the decantation basin, causing enormous pollution of the river eixample. for fauna and flora, it will take at least three years for a return to normal. however, establishing liability and obtaining compensation, if any, will take much longer. another company, clarebout in wallonia, which offers the world famous belgian frozen, industrial, pesticide-laden fries, has a poor environmental record. these are two examples in my own region of picardy wallonia, among many others, which have dramatic consequences for nature, biodiversity and the lives of the inhabitants and workers concerned. our report aims to take environmental crime more into account, to strengthen legal instruments to combat such crimes, including criminal liability, and to ensure access to justice and reparation for ecosystems and human victims of crimes that have become almost as lucrative as drug trafficking, and therefore cannot enjoy any impunity. in this report, we call in particular for the 2004 and 2008 directives on environmental liability and environmental crime to be revised, for ecocide to be included in european law, i.e. the recognition of crimes against ecosystems, for the mandate of the european public prosecutor's office to be extended to environmental crimes, and for victims to have better access to justice, in line with the modernisation of the aarhus regulation.

vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i will not attempt to refute the thesis that the high representative of the european union for foreign affairs and security policy is engaged in supporting dictatorships around the world, and in particular the cuban one. it's such a ridiculous thing that it's not worth answering. it is another thing that the official position of the european union and its member states, not just mine, is to be against the us blockade of cuba. no, i am not saying this because i am a dangerous castroist, it is because this is the official position of the european union, which is defended every year in the united nations security council. see if any of you guys find out at once. and that being against the extraterritorial sanctions of the united states is also our official position. we are against extraterritorial sanctions: those applied in cuba and those applied elsewhere. because we consider that they are harmful to the interests, in particular, of our companies. so please, please, please, put your feet on the ground. we are against the blockade and we say this every year in the un security council, and we have said, both actively and passively, that we do not agree with the extraterritorial sanctions of the united states. does that make me a supporter of a dictatorship? why in the world does that make me a supporter of a dictatorship? that cuba is not a democratic, liberal, multi-party system, as we have here, i guess you don't find out today, right? do you want me to list all the countries in the world that are not countries where we practice a liberal, multiparty, competitive democracy, like we do in europe? you want me to make you a list? why is it that for some strange reason, only you guys talk about cuba? why don't they just pick any one of those countries at random and raise it too? i have a feeling there's a certain concentration of your concerns. does this mean i'm not worried? of course i worry. as much as i'm concerned about the situation in other countries that are not liberal multiparty democracies like we are. now, there are things i can't admit. because they're very specific things. like the accusation that the money from european cooperation with cuba is going to fund organizations of the cuban regime. look, the amount of aid we are lending to civil society organizations in cuba is, at the moment, 8.4 million euros. of this 8.4 million euros, 7.6 million are managed by civil society organizations of the european union, which, by definition, i say is not part of the cuban government, and 0.8 million by cuban civil society organizations. here are the data. and these organizations have been carefully selected by our delegation there, taking into account that they are organizations whose members serve the general interest and that they have a democratic functioning and play a verified role of mediators between the authorities and the citizens. so this accusation that we take european taxpayers' money and just send it by registered mail to the cuban government is completely unfounded. we may disagree on many things, but facts are facts. and the fact is that i do not sincerely believe that anyone can say that under the previous system of political relations between the european union and cuba things were better than under this one. frankly, i do not think that by returning to the system of incommunicado, which was adopted by the european union at the request of another spanish government, we would be better off and more able to influence, contact cuba, its civil society and its government. and so, no, i don't think we have to abandon the current system. we can perfect it and we can apply it better. everything is improvable. but to return to the old system, frankly, i do not think it would improve the situation in cuba at all, or our ability to influence it. and so we will continue to develop the implementation of this agreement, which has - you are right, you are right about that - been in place for almost four years, which was approved in november almost four years ago. but to leave it and go back to what we had before, honestly, i don't think it would do anything, that it would improve anyone's situation. of our relationship with cuba and its people, certainly not.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on thursday, 10 june 2021. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. the right-wing forces in the european parliament have put this resolution on cuba on the agenda. to discuss human rights on the caribbean island, they say. - you did ? this same right which refused in all languages to condemn israel, or even to approve a resolution on the matter. i don't believe in that. so what's really bothering them in cuba? could it be cuba's achievements in education, housing and health? this small island, under us blockade, has even developed its own covid vaccine and sent solidarity brigades to europe to help italian hospitals! it has highlighted the failure of the neoliberal model, so dear to the traditional parties. if the european right really cared about the human rights of cubans, it would deal with the root cause of the violation of their rights: the illegal blockade imposed by the united states, repeatedly condemned by the un, which is suffocating the cuban economy. rather than blindly following the us, this parliament would do much better to respect and develop the agreement on cooperation and political dialogue between cuba and the eu. the people on both sides of the atlantic would benefit.
mr president, the european social fund plus must be used to promote employment opportunities that create prosperity in the member states and to help solve this very critical situation in which so many families find themselves. however, we must regret that this fund, which comes from european taxpayers, is used for purposes not always provided for in the treaties and therefore without a legal basis. this is the case of the imposition of gender ideology, not only because of the obsession to integrate this perspective at a cross-sectional level in all legislation, but also because it wants to intrude on the education of our children. moreover, it allocates more than 25% of the funds to immigrants, without distinguishing between legal and illegal immigration. the same applies to items intended for ambiguous concepts such as international organisations, philanthropic actors, third states or civil society. they define who they are, what their goals are and why they do it. it is clear that this lack of definition can conceal arbitrary allocations which are not always geared to the common good. the social fund must be able to achieve the objectives for which it was designed: to promote employment and to ensure a good standard of living for european citizens. you should see to it.
mr president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop this. the coronavirus pandemic has brought us into a very different reality. it was also a challenge to complete the negotiations efficiently and quickly. the first thing to consider was how to adapt the fund to the new socioeconomic reality. i think the negotiators have done a very good job. first of all, i am pleased that investment in the young generation has been identified as one of the top priorities and that a minimum of 12.5% has been set aside for this purpose in countries where the proportion of people not in education or training is unfortunately higher than the eu average. the previous crisis in 2008 showed that young people are particularly vulnerable to economic shocks. at the same time, i am pleased that other important priorities have not been abandoned, namely, the child guarantee, in which i have great hope, and social innovation, which will be our capital for building a society more resilient to future crises.
mr president, commissioner, today is an important day for our social europe. now that the coronavirus numbers are finally heading in the right direction, we need to shift our focus to how to approach the post-pandemic era. it is clear that the most vulnerable have been hit hardest by this crisis. to respond to this we need a strong european social fund and today we are voting on a strengthened esf which will focus on the most vulnerable: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of life of the people of europe. the budget for social inclusion is increased by 25 percent and 5 percent of the total budget is allocated to combating child poverty. the esf will also play a key role in the implementation of the european pillar action plan. the esf is the fund that is closest to the people, that makes europe more tangible and that has a direct impact on europeans. if we want to make europe an attractive lady again, the only way to do that is to make europe more social.
the european parliament has also called for the eu to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action against terrorism. good things have happened in a short period of time with the biden administration. i'm thinking of the truce in the tariff war. i hope that an agreement will also be reached, commissioner. but i am also thinking of the agreement in principle within the g7 on a global minimum corporate tax, an agreement which i welcome, but the modalities will be extremely important. let us use this period to develop a new dynamic as equals and as equals. first of all, by restoring multilateralism with the rescue and reform of the world trade organization. secondly, our economies, which are collectively the largest in the world, must use this to set the global standard. but there is also work to be done bilaterally. firstly, the establishment of the council for trade and technology for joint agreements. secondly, to develop a china strategy with the us. and thirdly, to participate in the summit of democratic countries. i hope that this parliament will establish institutional cooperation with the congress.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. social europe means family, job creation and innovation in hungarian. hungary has established a work-based society based on full employment and family allowances of 5% of gdp. we believe in giving people decent work and decent livelihoods. so the hungarian government raised employment to a record high and brought unemployment down from 12 percent to nearly 3 percent, while doubling the minimum wage. we've cut personal income tax to 15 percent, corporate tax to 9 percent. we have put the tax system at the service of family policy and childbearing, introduced family tax relief, exempted people under 25 from personal income tax next year, and exempted mothers with four children. so this is the hungarian model. strong social policies, ambitious tax cuts, support for families, which can create full employment.
president, commissioner, minister, first of all i would like to thank the portuguese presidency for the attention it has paid to the social field. the porto declaration is an important step in the right direction, but i am not ambitious enough in terms of poverty reduction. at present, the number of european children living in poverty is estimated at 19 million. the objective of lifting 15 million europeans out of poverty by 2030, including 5 million children, was much more ambitious in my view. if we want the porto summit to go down in history as a milestone towards a more social europe, we must now turn this social commitment into concrete action. it is therefore crucial that this concrete roadmap focuses on two things: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the objectives of the european semester through the new and existing criteria, the social indicators. most social objectives are national or regional in scope, and therefore the porto commitment is a fight that we must all fight together - europe, the member states, regional and local authorities, but also the social partners, and in cooperation with civil society.

mr president, we have a problem, a big problem especially affecting publicly—exposed women, among them women in politics. that problem is hate and power structures, and is perpetuated online. far too many women are threatened, every second, online – mostly by men and mostly simply because they are women. the reason lies in the fundamental question of power and felt superiority. some men think that women should not be allowed to have a voice in the public discourse, and too often their hate and threats actually silence women. this has to stop. we owe it to jo cox. we need better rules against online harassment and we need more effective reactions by the companies providing these services. we need this now because we have a very urgent problem which, in the end, is attacking our democracies. president, commissioner, one in three women in the eu is facing some form of violence and the lockdown measures during covid have only made this situation worse. unfortunately, we're also seeing this alarming trend online. cyber violence, online bullying and hateful behaviour towards women and girls are on the rise. a study by the parliament estimates the cost of cyber violence against women at more than €50 billion a year in the eu. not only does this cost a lot of money, it also reduces the participation of women in public debate, which weakens our democracy. moreover, the silencing effect is being used by some as a deliberate tactic against female politicians, journalists and activists. the barrel is really full. we must make the fight against gender-based violence in all its forms a real policy priority. that is why not only must all member states finally ratify the istanbul convention, why we must not only add gender-based violence to the list of serious crimes in article 83 of the convention, but also take strong action against this form of gender-based online violence. now is the time.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen of the house. the european union must take the lead in protecting animals, but not at the expense of people. we cannot make regulations that will destroy our farmers and make people's daily food more expensive. we all remember what happened in 2012 when we mandated the so-called enhanced cages. then a series of family farms and businesses went bankrupt, and there was a very severe increase in food prices. just one example, the price of eggs went up 33% in one year. of course, a complete ban on cages would have even more serious consequences. and how reasonable is it to put our own farmers at such a competitive disadvantage, since the same strict rules would not apply to foreign farmers. wto rules do not allow that. animal welfare is important to all of us, but we can only achieve this by making rules that do not endanger the livelihoods of european farmers and the food supply of the people who live here.
madam president, i am very pleased that the european parliament is echoing the european citizens' initiative 'for a new era without cages'. our vote, ladies and gentlemen, is the answer to their request. the answer is quite simple: we must ban cage-breeding throughout europe as soon as possible. and on this symbolic day, we could almost sing in unison the mythical french song "open, open the cage to the birds" by pierre perret, songwriter-performer. but to open cages quickly and efficiently, we need serious scientific impact studies as soon as possible to make this transition as smooth as possible. farmers and livestock farmers will also need to be supported and accompanied by, inter alia, incentive measures. the phasing out of cage use should be based on a species-based approach, which takes into account the characteristics of the individual animals. finally, all products of animal origin imported into the union should be produced under the same rules to avoid unfair competition to the detriment of our european farmers and in the interests of animal welfare, which implies a change in the wto rules.
madam president, this clearly shows us that our animals are sentient beings. they feel pain, they feel joy, they show sadness and they show happiness. acknowledging this, our society clearly needs to move forward into the 21st century by treating animals in a friendly way, granting them adequate space and living conditions. we have to reject claims that cages can be of use for animal welfare. let me take the example of sows that was mentioned by colleagues. if we grant sows with piglets enough space in our stables, there is no need at all to cage sows. ending industrial animal fattening also reduces the risks for us humans of newly occurring zoonosis and the spread of multi—resistant bacteria. so it’s not just about the animals, it’s also about human health. let’s end the cage age.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are all, like our european farmers, defenders of animal welfare and we all agree that we must move towards a constant improvement of welfare on our farms. so while i welcome the adoption of this resolution, i would like to admit that it also raises some concerns. we ask our farmers to ensure our food self-sufficiency, to provide us with healthy and quality food and to produce in a more sustainable and animal welfare-friendly way. they are already fully committed to this and have already made a great deal of progress in their practices. however, we must be careful not to impose too many constraints too quickly. but some in this house do not hear that voice and even propose to ban practices specific to certain of our sectors, which carry traditions representative of our regions. i am referring here to the gavage, which enables our french foie gras sector to perpetuate a gastronomic tradition specific to our regions. this proposal, which has been tabled in a plenary amendment, is outside the scope of the resolution we are voting on today. unfortunately, it is representative of the agribashing that our farmers are suffering. so i hope we can reject it completely.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the citizens' initiative 'end the cage age' has brought together 1.4 million people in the union. there is therefore strong public support for the abolition of animal cages in agriculture. the decency of our society is determined by how well we treat the most defenseless. article 13 of the treaty describes animals as sentient beings. no animal should be subjected to torture under the care of a human. that is why the abolition of caging in farming is so important and must be done quickly. i ask the commission to speed up the review of eu animal welfare legislation and to incorporate parliament's demands in the context of the review of the directive on the protection of animals used for animal production. the cruelty to animals in the eu must end, and the abolition of caged animals is a step towards recovering our humanity too. large-scale industrial farming is a thing of the past and must be scrapped in favour of small-scale, organic and animal-friendly farming.
madam president, i would first like to thank all the poultry farmers in europe who did not wait for this resolution to take action to give priority to animal welfare. i am thinking in particular of rural poultry farmers, all those who in recent years have given this freedom to all poultry and also contributed to the building of animal welfare in europe. however, there are still poultry in some countries that have to live on surfaces that are amazing to say the least. i'll show you the area on the screen. some birds live for a few dozen days, no more, on this area that i'm showing on the screen. this is clearly not acceptable. we must be able to make this transition quickly, with this objective by 2027, not only for all farmers and producers in europe, by supporting farmers and producers in their investments to transform this and bring the necessary, indispensable animal welfare, but also - and i very much insist - to ask the commission, beyond this support for the agricultural world, to have the same standards at our borders, thus prohibiting imports, which today represent almost 500 000 tonnes per year of poultry from brazil, thailand, etc., which do not respect animal welfare at all. so we must act in two ways: support farmers and herders and ban imports that do not respect animal welfare. this is a good cause for the european union as a whole.
president, commissioner, madam secretary of state, the european union must pursue a comprehensive migration policy to protect its citizens, to maintain schengen and the free movement of persons. we must not allow ourselves to be blackmailed by countries which want to use mass migration flows to put pressure on us. we appreciate that turkey is hosting millions of syrian refugees. but, ladies and gentlemen, we cannot ignore the fact that turkey has moved far away from the secular state of kemal atatürk, also from the rule of law, and is oppressing the kurdish minority in an unprecedented way. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
madam president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop. i would like to congratulate those who have worked in the committee on regional development, because we know very well that it has not been easy to defend cohesion policy over the years. initial attempts to limit cohesion policy could have ended much worse. the result is good, but i would also like to draw attention to another thing: namely, that a very large number of representatives of local and regional authorities, businesses and ngos are now waiting, after the legislative basis has been established, for a clear decision: what will be financed and implemented by the national reconstruction plan, by the reconstruction fund and what will be implemented by cohesion policy. we all have before us - and i think it is also thanks to the commissioner and the whole of parliament - the need for a really clear and understandable explanation of what the reconstruction fund will be used for and what the structural funds will be used for. it's an emergency, and everyone's waiting for it.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. cohesion policy is one of the most important european policies, because not only do we spend more than eur 300 billion, but through this policy we are implementing the principle of solidarity, which is very important in europe. with this policy, we are bringing development closer between different countries, between different regions. i was mayor of slovenia for 13 years. we have built many water pipes, sewage systems, built broadband connections on farms that are 800 meters high in the mountains, restored many cultural monuments. and those are the goals for the next few years. and now that this work in the european parliament, the european commission, in the european union is done, it is up to the countries to prepare excellent operational programmes, to combine them with recovery plans and to continue to welcome the signs that say that this was built with european funds and thus strengthen the european identity and support for the european union. congratulations to all who participated and we participated in these reports.
