member of the commission. – madam president, today’s debate clearly demonstrates the value of cohesion policy and the hope it brings for european citizens and for the regions. thank you for the kind words that a lot of you addressed to the commission. i will share them with the teams. in fact, now it is time to move from words to actions, finalising the preparation of programmes on the ground. here, i would like to underline that we should never forget the main purpose of cohesion policy. it is to promote convergence and correct internal disparities. for this, we need to look at the territories, address them and develop their development challenges, including rural areas, areas in demographic decline or with natural handicaps, peripheral regions, and outermost regions. i firmly believe that the green transition and the digital transition both bring new opportunities in new and in established industries, from sustainable tourism to sustainable transport, and from renewable energy networks to batteries. the digital and green transformation and teleworking also offer a golden opportunity to rebalance economic activity across european territories and make new regions competitive in new and innovative industries. but all these opportunities require planning and then investment, and this investment has got to be done in coherence across all the different instruments that we have in front of us. the recovery and resilience plans, the coronavirus response investment initiative, the just transition fund, react—eu and the 2021—2027 multiannual financial framework. they require that we apply a principle of ‘no harm to cohesion’ to all our policies and that, in the opposite sense, we benefit and we stimulate all the possible synergies so that in fact we do no harm to cohesion, meaning that nothing should aggravate the regional divide or hamper regional convergence. so our work together has just begun. it starts today with the adoption of these regulations. let’s keep monitoring and developing a new, more sustainable and more cohesive europe for all our citizens. thank you once again.
the joint debate is closed. i would like to inform you that with regard to these recommendations for second reading, no proposals to reject the council positions and no amendments have been tabled under rules 67 and 68, so the council positions are therefore deemed approved, and consequently the proposed acts are deemed to have been adopted. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. i welcome the political agreement on the regulation on the european regional development fund/cohesion fund (erdf/cfsp) and the fact that the new programme has now been given the green light by the european parliament after the council. the erdf aims to strengthen economic and social cohesion in the european union by reducing disparities between regions. the erdf focuses its support on a number of high priority areas such as innovation and research, digitalisation, support for small and medium-sized enterprises and the low-carbon economy. i am pleased that the cohesion fund can also support energy and transport projects in the field of environmental protection, provided that they have clear environmental benefits in terms of energy efficiency, the use of renewable energy, the development of rail transport, the strengthening of public transport, etc. hungary will have some eur 20 billion (fg 7020 billion) available under cohesion policy for the period 2021-2027. hungary issued a statement on the incorrect use of the term 'gender' when adopting the legislative package on cohesion policy. hungary is convinced that these legislative documents are not adequate to define the content of gender. hungary shall interpret the term 'gender' as a reference to the term 'biological sex' in accordance with the relevant articles of the treaty on the functioning of the european union and the charter of fundamental rights of the european union.
in writing. cohesion policy is at the heart of european solidarity, aiming to reduce the territorial, economic and social disparities between member states so that no region of the european union is left behind. the european union has had to face new challenges. however, nothing should diminish the ambition of the eu's fundamental goal of cohesion: neither the repercussions of the uk's exit from the eu, nor the consequences of the climate situation or the covid-19 pandemic. the cohesion fund is one of the most significant areas of european union policies, accounting for about one third of the budget. romania will receive €28.22 billion from the cohesion funds (7.52%) from the eu's long-term budget. in this way, romania has a historic chance to implement investment programmes through sustainable growth and job creation. cohesion policy will encompass the efforts of member states to recover, to remedy the consequences of the crisis and to emerge stronger from this ordeal. in a post-pandemic world, cohesion policy funds will in particular support investments in innovation and entrepreneurship, in the digital and green transition and in transport networks.
in writing. the adoption of the cohesion policy package, comprising the common provisions regulation, interreg, the erdf and the cohesion fund, is absolutely essential to reduce the economic, social and territorial disparities still existing in the eu. this new package, which has the largest budget allocated in the financial budget for the period 2021-2027, should place greater emphasis on social programmes and sustainable urban development, as well as on climate action, thus aligning with the sustainability objectives of the european green deal. under the new common provisions regulation, both interreg and the erdf will now have to channel at least 30% of their resources to climate action, the circular economy and investments in sustainable growth and job creation. it is now important that the member states are prepared to start using this new budget as quickly and efficiently as possible.
in writing. we have come to the conclusion of these long negotiations on the cohesion policy for the 2021-2027 programming period. we all know that this policy reflects the european union's solidarity with developing regions or regions in economic difficulty. it is now up to the member states to present operational programmes to implement the five strategic objectives we support. in this context, i am glad that this european solidarity applies in particular to the province of luxembourg in belgium, my region. as a less developed area, it will receive €54 million from the erdf. in this respect, i would like the commission to take account of the situation on the ground. it must be able to demonstrate some flexibility in the allocation of funds between strategic objectives, as the difficulties of one region are not those of another. for example, in the belgian province of luxembourg, strategic objectives 4 (a more social europe) and 5 (a europe closer to the citizen) deserve the most attention. the reason for this request for flexibility will of course be provided by the operational programmes drawn up by wallonia. thank you.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. we are on the paris course, as the european commission's analysis shows quite clearly, at minus 55 per cent. let's compare ourselves to the united states of america. if they reach their goal by 2030, they will not be where we are today. some talk, some do! who's in charge in europe? one of them is forestry. the good news is that 10 percent of europe's emissions are already being captured by europe's forests. in addition to being a place of recreation and biodiversity, our forest is also one of our most active climate protectors. i am therefore particularly pleased that this climate protection law recognises for the first time that natural co2 reductions in agriculture and forestry are also genuine climate protection measures. i think this is a long overdue step in the assessment of the climate performance of agriculture and forestry. and now we finally listen to the science. ladies and gentlemen, i am convinced that the climate law in its present form will only encourage farmers and foresters to do more to grow humus and to make agriculture more sustainable. this will allow us to increase our sink yields and achieve the goal of climate neutrality. but it does require a reasonable price tag. that is why i am in favour of a carbon market for agriculture. this will enable us to store even more co2, promote sustainable agriculture and forestry and maintain attractive rural areas.
madam president, mr vice-president, the european union is the first region in the world to have a climate law because it is right, because it is time and because not to act now would be more than a mistake, a real political mistake. the 2030 target is clear: 55% reduction in co2 emissions and carbon neutrality by 2050 at the latest. with this commitment, reiterated again by the commission, to propose no legislation henceforth that is not strictly aligned with the objectives of the paris agreement. the target is even better, with 57% less by 2030 if we increase land-use carbon sinks, knowing, it should be remembered, that the main natural carbon sinks are our oceans and they concentrate 50 times more than the atmosphere. i come to what i wanted to say this morning, here, it is essential, therefore, that europe does not forget the other battle for the planet: that of plastic pollution that is suffocating our oceans. booming plastics, cheap, durable, little or no recycling, that's the other threat to the climate. i conclude by saying that action is urgent. here too, europe must have a law for the protection of the oceans, a binding one, with the world as the field of action.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the world is upside down and in this upside down world it snows in summer and it is hot in winter. in an upside-down world we will leave our homes because we have the desert all around us and in an upside-down world the appeals of scientists go unheeded and it is the courts that have to remind politicians of their responsibilities to future generations. but in this upside-down world , we still have a choice . choose what answer to give to our grandchildren when they ask us where we were when we destroyed the planet we borrowed for them. this european climate law is not enough: we are heading for a temperature increase of 2-3 degrees celsius, and the next 10 years are crucial, and europe is not doing enough. the truth is that we have no choice and for that reason, in my heart of hearts, i cannot vote for this european climate law and with the greens/efa group i will vote against it.
president, the european union wants to cut greenhouse gas emissions by half with a climate law. after all, we must be "climate neutral" by 2050. so we're going to be hurting ourselves economically, and in large parts of the world, there's no effort. - no, it's the opposite. for example, china burns 3.8 billion cubic tons of coal a year and builds hundreds of coal-fired power plants in its own country. and that's also happening in indonesia, zimbabwe and so on. so while the climate-neutral nuclear power stations in belgium are being closed down, coal is being bravely continued. the purple-green government in our country even opts for gas-fired power stations, which do emit greenhouse gases. but anyway, belgium is the land of surrealism. so let's look at europe, which is responsible for less than ten percent of emissions. the impact on the global climate is therefore negligible. what a climate hysteria!
madam president, commissioner, minister, we are finally here. and that's good. europe is moving forward. it sets ambitious reduction targets by 2030, by 2050, through this climate law, which is kind of the cornerstone of the green deal. we're going to have to go very fast, much faster than before, seven times faster than before. we know that the main thing now and in a few weeks' time will be the proposals that the commission can make to achieve these objectives. what are the means we are using in all the policies deployed both at the level of the commission, but also by the individual member states, by all the actors of society to achieve these necessary but ambitious objectives together? i recognize that there will be work to be done, and i hope that will be done, particularly on trade policy. it is unthinkable that tomorrow we will continue to negotiate trade agreements at european level without taking account of the real impact of greenhouse gases. at the very least, the analysis, and beyond the analysis, the ambition is also to actually impose these elements in the reduction of greenhouse gases. i would like to conclude by saying that i am very surprised, since i have heard some members of the groups, particularly the greens, say that this is not ambitious enough, that we are not going far enough. we are sometimes world champions in europe for criticizing ourselves, whereas ambition is the strongest on a world level. at the same time, these same greens in their respective countries are endorsing the same ambitions. we'll look for schizophrenia at another time.
madam president, today can be described as a great historic green day for the european union, because 71 years after the historic schuman declaration and 54 years after the founding of the european union, it is a great historic milestone that, through the adoption of the climate law, lays the foundations for the green transition. today, in fact, is the beginning of the great green revolution of the european union, which is becoming a leader and a pioneering green power, because it first set this big and enormously ambitious goal to make our own continent climate neutral by 2050. the european union was the first of all the powers on the planet to set a target of reducing that incredible 55 percent of carbon emissions by 2030, and the european union was the first to make the big green deal with hundreds of billions to go to a just green transition. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected, and that the environment is protected in a way that is not harmful to the environment. i come from a small country, cyprus, which i believe can become the first green country in the european union, can become the first green island because it has the potential, because of the strong comparative advantages it has in terms of climatic conditions, to develop renewable energy sources and because there is the will and determination of the citizens and the government to turn cyprus into a model green island.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i just have a few remarks to make. first of all the big question: is at least 55% by 2030 enough to get us where we need to be? the answer of the commission is yes it is. we’ve done the numbers. we’ve analysed everything and we believe this will set us on the right trajectory. i believe our analysis is stronger today than it was a year ago because of the change in the international climate. many countries have joined our efforts. it was a lonely place a year and a half ago with our green deal, but now the us administration is back on track. i spoke to the chinese envoy, mr xie, this morning. china is upping the ante. we have a climate pact with japan. i will be travelling soon to south korea to discuss with them the possibilities to enhance etc., etc., etc. so i say without any reservation at least 55% by 2030 sets us on a course to climate neutrality by 2050 and we lead the way for the rest of the world, and i’m glad to say that the rest of the world more and more is following. so i would please ask you to have that confidence that it is enough. now i respect those who say that it isn’t enough, that we should be doing more. i respect that extra ambition, but i don’t understand how that can lead to a negative vote on the climate law. i do understand those who have denied and continue to deny and in my view completely abandon the citizens they represent by creating the illusion that there is no problem or that we can just bury our heads in the sand and then it will go away. i know those groups in this parliament and i understand that they vote against. aber ich habe überhaupt kein verständnis für ein negatives votum von den grünen. ich verstehe es einfach nicht! wir haben so gut zusammengearbeitet. wir haben auch so viel zusammen geändert in dieser ganzen prozedur. das scheint mir fast wie ein verantwortlichkeitsurlaub, den sie nehmen. denn sie wissen: auch wenn sie nein sagen – wahrscheinlich wird das parlament überwiegend ja sagen. und stellen sie sich mal vor, wir hätten ein anderes votum heute, wo es entscheidend ist, ob die grünen ja oder nein sagen. stellen sie sich mal vor, sie sagen nein und es gibt kein klimagesetz. was sind denn die folgen? sind sie dazu bereit, diese folgen zu tragen? ich sage das mal in ganz deutlicher sprache, weil wir noch so viel zu tun haben in den nächsten jahren. und ich hoffe auf eine konstruktive haltung aus der grünen-fraktion. wir brauchen sie, wir brauchen sie an bord! und ich will alles dafür tun als kommissar, dass sie auch an bord bleiben können. madam president, i ask for your indulgence just for one minute. whatever we do on climate with the climate law – which i think is a historic moment – whatever we do, we’ll fail if we don’t adhere to the fundamental values this european union was built upon. equality is a fundamental value. respect for diversity is a fundamental value. being gay is not a choice: it’s who you are. being a bigot is a choice that you should not make. being gay is not contagious: hatred is contagious. being gay is not dangerous to families: teaching children to hate is dangerous to families. it is profoundly non-european. we should stand up for our values. i say this in the place where all europeans are represented regardless of their religion, regardless of their race, regardless of their nationality, regardless of their gender, regardless of their sexual orientation or identity. if we have no equality we will not be saved by a climate law. if we have equality there’s nothing that can stop us.
acting president of the council. madam president, madam vice-president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, i have listened with great interest to this debate, which shows our common commitment to combating climate change. this is the right way. this is the commitment that will make us stronger and that will lead us to climate neutrality, a path to sustainability, to innovation, but also a path that will allow us to create more jobs, new businesses, without forgetting the social challenges of this transition that has to be just. this is the way for the planet and for the people. the european climate law, the law of laws, as the vice-president said, will be the cornerstone of the green deal with an impact on all european union policies. the law, together with the regulation on the governance of the energy union, will ensure that the european union has effective tools at its disposal to keep us on track and achieve the 2030 and 2040 and 2050 targets. the ambitious proposal put forward by the commission has been made even more ambitious by the many amendments which this parliament has introduced in the final text and which the council has accepted. we now have the political will, we have the ambitious and pragmatic basic legislation, we also have the financial instruments to help us achieve results. with the european climate law we are also sending a strong signal to the world, something we have already done at recent international summits. i hope that the ambition of those who feel that more needs to be done will help the union on its way and encourage other countries to follow our example, because climate change remains a global challenge that requires action on a global scale. it's all been said. now is the time to act and to act in the future, together for our common future.
just after the pre-publication of an alarming new un climate report, here is a completely inadequate climate law. scientists say that greenhouse gas emissions must be reduced by 65% by 2030, but the european union wants only a 55% reduction. worse, that 55% is accounting fraud. in reality, it is only -52.8%. again, this objective is only set at european level, not at national level. in other words, a country that wants to do less can always get out of it by saying: let someone else do more. that's how you organize a vicious circle. even the elimination of subsidies for polluting fuels is too much to ask. saying you want to save the climate, but not stopping the subsidizing of fossil fuels. you don't think it is possible. the european union naturally rejects any break with the free market, such as the notorious ets carbon market, which still distributes free emission allowances. a climate drama, a lot of profit for big business. the fund which was supposed to guarantee a socially just transition was amputated. we're already seeing attempts to get employees to pay for the transition. this climate bill has no future. we need a social climate revolution with ambitious climate goals, massive public investment and radical social goals.
madam president, commissioner, madam representative of the council, ladies and gentlemen, the european public prosecutor's office finally started its work less than a month ago, which, i agree with you, commissioner, is a historic turning point in the fight against corruption and crossborder fraud. it is now up to us to ensure that its independence is strictly respected. however, the irregularities and the partisan intervention of the slovenian government, led by the ultra-conservative janša, in the procedure for appointing the deputy prosecutor in slovenia show that the government is clearly exceeding its powers. the unprecedented scale of the social and economic needs of european citizens following the pandemic and the scale of the resources allocated under the post-covid recovery plan require more vigilance than ever before in the face of the risks of fraud. the appointment of the deputy prosecutor should therefore be made in accordance with the procedures and the separation of powers of slovenia, which is a party to enhanced cooperation. but that's not all. after hungary and poland, which also decided not to join the european public prosecutor's office, we are witnessing an extremely worrying change in slovenia: attacks on the judiciary, the press, civil society and the opposition are repeated. the slovenian media is being bought by viktor orbán's allies. this development, as slovenia prepares to take over the presidency of the european union in a week's time, requires us to recall with one voice that respect for the rule of law in the european union is not negotiable. to prove this, the european commission must take a step forward: sanction schemes that do not respect the rule of law to prevent others, such as slovenia, from continuing to follow suit.
president, commissioner, the protection of children must not slip through the cracks of eu legislation. i am therefore very pleased that an agreement has been reached which guarantees both the safety of our children and our privacy. the protection of our children must be a priority not only for the eu but also for the rest of society. online child abuse is one of the many challenges that the eu must address urgently. according to a report by the council of europe, in 2020, a us monitoring system reported 10.4 million unique images and 3.7 million unique videos of child sexual abuse. this is happening in europe too, which means that the eu cannot stand by and watch. i am pleased that technology companies can continue to voluntarily monitor online child abuse, including grooming, provided they provide additional safeguards to protect our privacy. this, i believe, strikes a good balance between protecting children and protecting our privacy and data. however, we must remain vigilant and monitor the situation so that we can make improvements if necessary.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the funding for small fishing vessels up to 12 metres in length, the drawing up of a plan for the development of small-scale coastal fishing and 25% of the funds for the protection of biodiversity: these were some of the main requests parliament had approved with the aim of using european public funds to help those who really need it - the small-scale coastal fishing segment - and thus promote the protection and restoration of biodiversity in european seas. unfortunately, we are faced with a text, once again, watered down by the council, which, as usual in an opaque way, serves the interests of the large fleet to the detriment of small fishermen and marine biodiversity. public money cannot be used to support profitable businesses that risk contributing further to the depletion of our seas and must instead be made available to small fishermen, who are the pillars of the sustainable management of marine resources. i therefore call on my colleagues to vote against this text: parliament must send a clear signal of its commitment to the defence of small-scale coastal fishing and the protection of biodiversity.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you very much for this rich debate, which has shown support for our new fund. to conclude, let me underline the following two key principles. first, in the same way as all the other sectoral funds under the multiannual framework for 2021-2027, the emfaf will contribute to the european green deal. it will support the protection of biodiversity and the full transition to low-impact and sustainable fishing and aquaculture in line with our biodiversity strategy and the common fisheries policy. and second, public support through the emfaf should deliver maximum collective added value. it should support market failures and suboptimal investment situations, but not duplicate or replace private financing. instead, emfaf should facilitate and complement private investment, especially where the private sector is profitable, like the maritime economy in general is. in this context, i see a clear need to use financial instruments in addition to traditional direct grants. indeed, loans and guarantees can maximise private investments with minimum public support, in particular for energy-efficiency technologies, selective fishing gear and production facilities in the aquaculture and processing sectors. i am looking forward to the final and formal adoption of our fund by the co-legislators. our attention will then shift to the timely adoption of the national programmes which member states are currently preparing. as i said before, it is of the utmost importance that these national programmes deliver maximum added value, in particular as regards their contribution to the green and digital transitions and to stronger resilience of the fisheries and aquaculture sectors. the action supported in this respect must address strategic needs and deliver clear results. with a fund as small as the emfaf, it is particularly important that every euro invested generates much more in terms of collective benefits and common good. let’s make the best use of it to address our key challenges and make it a success.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, thank you for today’s opportunity to address a key demographic trend that is impacting our entire continent, namely ageing, a trend that will significantly impact the european union in the coming years and decades. it is vital that we prepare ourselves now. many thanks to the rapporteur beata szydło for the comprehensive report. the issues you address all echo the work of the european commission, including health and long-term care, measures to encourage active and healthy ageing and social inclusion. many of the competences linked to ageing are in the hands of the member states. nevertheless, the commission has an important role in supporting the relevant measures at national, regional and local levels. the von der leyen commission is the first one to dedicate one portfolio to demography. our work is based on the report on the impact of demographic change of june 2020. it is a valuable tool to help us to understand demographic change. we see how life expectancy at birth has increased by about 10 years for both men and women. one of the most prominent features of ageing is that the sheer number of older people will increase. today, 20% of the population is over 65. by 2070 it will be 30%. meanwhile, the share of people above 80 is expected to more than double, reaching 13% in the same period. the demography report lays the ground for the green paper on ageing. i want to emphasise the important message that ageing brings both challenges and opportunities. ageing is not just a matter for older people. everyone is impacted directly by the prospect of living a longer life, including the young. we must balance our policymaking for everyone across the entire life cycle, making sure we leave no one behind. adopted in january, the green paper was launched in the european union-wide discussion on ageing. it takes a life-cycle approach and reflects the universal impact that ageing has on all generations and stages in life. it highlights the importance of striking the right balance between sustainable solutions for our welfare systems and strengthening solidarity between the generations. in fact, the full title of the paper is ‘green paper on ageing: fostering solidarity and responsibility between generations’. this highlights the importance we attach to inter-generational fairness. the paper’s public consultation closed in april. we gathered a total of 473 replies from 26 member states, as well as some from non-european countries. we are now analysing the input and considering possible policy responses. in this context the commission has recently published three reports related to ageing, namely the 2021 pension adequacy report, the 2021 long-term care report, and the 2021 ageing report. the reports address some of the main challenges of a continent growing older and identify areas that are key to ensuring intergenerational responsibility and solidarity. i look forward to hearing your views today as we strengthen efforts to address the challenges of ageing and grasp its opportunities.
on behalf of the ppe group. i would like to welcome the honourable president, mrs dubravka šuica, to the european parliament for the debate today. finally, demographics came on the agenda. europe is indeed ageing, and if we do not anticipate the consequences of this ageing today, we could be in big trouble tomorrow. i would like to draw your attention to the increase in the epidemic of neurodegenerative diseases such as alzheimer's. of course, we need additional funding for research and, above all, for investing in human resources to care for the sick and elderly in the future. there is a growing shortage of these today. demographic challenges do not affect all countries in the same way, but the trend is worrying everywhere. in recent years, there has also been an increase in anti-ageing behaviour, and this situation has worsened during the pandemic. i therefore expect the commission to set guidelines and policies to encourage intergenerational cooperation. older people are our wisdom, which we can use with the bold ideas of younger people to elevate to a collective success.

in writing. the european network and telecommunications facility plays an important role in the development of europe's energy infrastructure. at the beginning of the new budgetary cycle, it is timely to discuss the initiative in three dimensions: the results achieved, the new tasks and the impact on the central european region. in view of the results, the device has proved to be as expected. the projects have helped to create an integrated energy market. the european energy grid is thus better equipped to deal with shocks than before. in addition to completing the ongoing developments, the programme will also have to fulfil new tasks. the investments should also contribute to the promotion of decarbonisation and renewable energy sources, in line with the climate ambitions. finally, the instrument has also proved to be a driver of energy infrastructure development in central europe. the krk lng terminal has opened up global natural gas markets for the region. in the new cycle, the danube ingrid smart grid project will facilitate the effective integration of renewable energy sources in hungary and slovakia.
in writing. ensuring that the ten-t connections are completed quickly and efficiently is a matter of securing adequate funding and simplifying permitting rules. the rapid implementation of new regulations can reduce delays in the implementation of infrastructure projects and facilitate the participation of private investors. however, the new acts must not paralyse well-functioning national planning and permitting procedures and national institutions, including local and regional authorities. there is no need for eu intervention where countries themselves comprehensively regulate the stages of the procedure and activities and take additional account of specific regional conditions and requirements. the structure and organisation of the national authorities are a matter for the member states. the revised legal framework will give member states the flexibility to use existing procedures. the eu's approach to the ten-t has been to develop east-west links, which has delayed the development of connectivity and the socioeconomic development of regions in eastern europe. we must urgently make up for the losses and redirect funds to projects linking northern and southern europe, especially investments in the eastern flank countries. my regions warmia and mazury and podlasie still qualify as least accessible regions, despite the fact that two ten-t core corridors run through them. the s7 road is part of the baltic-adriatic transport corridor and the s61 road is part of the north sea-baltic corridor.
in writing. mr president, madam rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, thank you for this report and for your consultative work in this context. the safety objective of the traffic management system must remain a priority for the member states in the implementation of ertms. similarly, improving comfort and interoperability for passengers should drive further application of the directive, to make the train ever more attractive. finally, improving the speed and delivery times of rail freight will also support the credibility of the sector vis-à-vis road and air transport. to support deployment in the 21st century, i believe that the functionalities offered by satellites and particularly the galileo system must be given priority in the future. the development of satellite positioning for railway mobility, which is less expensive than a physical beacon, would further enhance its credibility at international level. research resources must be devoted to rapid implementation, on the main lines and throughout europe. new technologies can be used to meet the requirements of fluidity and security, but also to inform the public. we have enough to meet all our objectives.
madam president, the eu’s magnitsky act represents an important foreign policy instrument that allows us to defend the values and principles on which our union was founded and to punish those who are responsible for human rights abuses. however, i believe that it is absolutely essential that the sanctions regime also target economic and financial enablers of these human rights abuses. this is why the member states must urgently expand the scope of the eu magnitsky act by drawing on the us global magnitsky act and include acts of corruption within the instrument. all too often oligarchs or representatives of oppressive regimes launder money through our european cities, use the money stolen from their own people to repress them, as well as to corrupt and interfere in our democratic processes. using the magnitsky act to go after the money will not only help people who wish to live in free societies, but it will also make our own free societies more resilient.
president-in-office of the council, on behalf of the vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, well, i will not leave brussels today for ljubljana, i will stay with you for a couple of days so i will be able to discuss further. about those that should fall under this type of sanction regime: there are unfortunately many across the world who could be listed under this regime. the first listings were not meant to be exhaustive and the regime will continue to be used in the coming years. there was an intervention regarding incompleteness of the regime that the eu installed in the process. well, this sanction regime was designed to address serious human rights violations and abuses, and that is exactly what it is focusing on. it covers genocide, crimes against humanity, torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, and slavery, among others. at the same time, and at the time of the establishment of the regime, member states opted not to include corruption as one of the listing criteria. however, the framework of the regime also allows the economic enablers of human rights violations or persons or entities benefiting from such violations to be listed. with this sanctions regime, we make it clear that there are serious consequences to those violations and abuses. and the eu does not turn a blind eye. and it is for the council to decide by unanimity if any additional criterion should be added or not. there were also as well many interventions regarding unanimity. as you know, the modalities and scope of the new sanctions regime have to be agreed between member states by unanimity, including the provision on voting for the adoption of new listings. when the high representative submitted to the council the draft legal act for the establishment of the new sanctions regime, he systematically proposed to use qualified majority voting for the adoption of new listing. the eu global human rights sanctions regime was no exception. however, the member states decided that unanimity was required for any listings. under most of the eu sanctions regime, new listings are adopted by unanimity and not qualified majority voting. this standard practice is that member states reach consensus even in the few cases where qualified majority voting is possible. however, the high representative continues to believe that the greater use of qualified majority voting in the area of foreign and security policy could bring considerable benefit, to make decisions faster and more efficient. and the high representative will continue to discuss this issue with member states. as you know, the high representative made two proposals regarding the regime and both proposals were adopted in the council. that proves it works and that we succeeded to translate political commitment into actions. there was a question on how to cooperate with other third states, so like-minded countries such as the us, uk, canada have an ongoing interest to work together to combine with the eu to maximise the impact of respective human rights sanction regimes. this is an interest that we share and we maintain an open dialogue with them. and with other sanction regimes: 14 countries are systematically invited to align with our sanction decisions. this includes eu candidate countries and countries from the european free trade association and the european economic area. it is the regime that has been in force for the last six months, while the magnitsky act has been in power since 2012. about the scope: the regime covers serious human rights violations and abuses. more concretely, acts covered by the eu global human rights sanction regime are genocide, crimes against humanity, torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, slavery, extrajudicial summary or arbitrary execution and killings, enforced disappearance of persons, arbitrary arrests or detentions. it also covers other acts including, but not limited to, the following, insofar as these violations or abuses are widespread, systematic or are otherwise of serious concern as regards the objectives of the common foreign and security policy set out in article 21 of the treaty on european union, mainly trafficking in human beings as well as abuses of human rights by migrant smugglers, and referred to in article 1 of the council decision. and sexual and gender-based violence, then violation or abuses of freedom of peaceful assembly and of association, and violation or abuses of freedom of opinion and expression, violations or abuses of freedom of religion or belief. and how do we see the role of the european parliament? as i said already in the introductory remarks, we would like to acknowledge the important role that the european parliament has played in the establishment of the eu global human rights regime. your longstanding support across political groups significantly contributed to create the necessary momentum. and we are committed to continuing our exchange with the european parliament on this regime. in line with the existing treaty provisions, the council decides on the modalities and scope of any sanctions regime and on any listing proposal put forward by the member state or / and the high representative. of course, the high representative will surely consider the work of the european parliament in this field and especially its proposals. the eu global human rights sanctions regime was a missing link in our eu human rights toolbox. we now have a powerful tool at our disposal which we can use to complement our action at bilateral and multilateral level. we continue monitoring closely serious human rights violations and abuses around the world, and we will not shy away from taking further decisive action in the near future.

president, president michel, vice-president šefčovič, earlier this year the council decided to provide further support for the reception of refugees in turkey. the council also decided that efforts should be made in the wider region to help syrian refugees. this is certainly necessary for these refugees, but it is also a matter of self-interest. the budgetary procedure for this financing has not yet been decided. nevertheless, i should like to speak briefly about the way in which the funds are being spent. to date, there is a fairly broad consensus that this is done efficiently and effectively enough. however, it is clear that the continuation of this support and the structural anchoring of this effort will also change the nature of the operation, which may have an impact on the sound use of the funds. i therefore advocate a management structure with sufficient flexibility, in which envelopes are not fixed and in which basic support remains central. we should not therefore institutionalize a temporary measure - something we are unfortunately good at in the union - and then come to the conclusion that we could have done better otherwise.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, since 2009, achieving the european research area (era) has been an explicit treaty objective. nevertheless, progress is still needed today to accomplish many of the era objectives. for instance, on the level of research and innovation expenditure, we are still lagging very much behind other countries such as israel, south korea, japan or the us. research and innovation (r&i) are essential to enable europe’s recovery in a fair and sustainable way, bringing forward the green and digital transitions while at the same time enhancing our competitiveness and resilience, in particular taking into account the lessons learned from the covid—19 pandemic. the eu, its member states and regions have to redouble efforts and significantly increase r&i expenditure and to align these efforts with a more ambitious eu industrial policy that is consistent with the european climate law and the goal of climate neutrality. furthermore, member states should take full advantage of the next generation funds in order to boost their r&i efforts, making them a real driving force for advancing the twin transition. after all, a well—developed era is essential for promoting both fundamental research and the more applied research, enabling us to find faster and concrete solutions for the pressing challenges ahead. academic freedom is a fundamental pillar in the era and has to be promoted internationally, but also in the eu, especially in the light of some internal threats, as pointed out in the european court of justice (ecj) ruling on hungary and foreign higher education institutions. finally, yet importantly, we have to acknowledge the role of regional governments in promoting r&i policies and developing and connecting r&i ecosystems. there are some very successful experiences of that which significantly contribute to the excellence of european research.

president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, if we have learned anything from this covid-19 crisis, it is that patchwork measures in the various member states do not provide a solution. on the contrary, we need more europe, more coordination, faster agreements, better preparation and smoother cooperation in the field of public health. together, we can save more lives. what we have also learned is that the energy and time required to deal with such a health crisis has immense implications for the mainstream health care in the member states. at the most critical stage of this crisis, regular care was shut down. cancer screening, emergency treatment or surgery were postponed, with all the consequences that followed. we must avoid this in the future. the continuity of cancer care and screening must be guaranteed, also during the pandemic. by working together more closely, we can respond to pandemics faster and avoid having to delay routine care for too long. therefore, we call for special attention to be given to these vulnerable target groups and ensure that regular care is an integral part of any preparedness and response plan. because that will also save lives.

president roberta metsola, president ursula von der leyen, the speech by the president of the commission shows the difference that belonging to the european union makes. it was positive, it was optimistic, it was encouraging and it certainly has the support of the epp group and the portuguese epp members. it has made a strong defence of the rule of law here, especially with regard to funds and budgetary matters. but it is not enough to ensure that the funds are used in accordance with the values of the european union. we must also ensure that they are well spent, well applied, that they are not going to be spent on megalomaniacal works, on works that concentrate only on the most important region of the country or on works that serve only clienteles. it is therefore very important to monitor the use of funds to ensure that they are well spent. the plan is to revive the economy, to revive businesses, to give opportunities to young people, to have intergenerational solidarity. it is not to pay for the running costs of the budget, as is happening in some countries, including my own. it is not to be used as a weapon of election campaign, as the portuguese prime minister is now doing every day. the plan is not the plan of any party or any country, it is a european plan for europeans. it is important that the european commission is attentive to this, ensuring that funds are well used for the benefit of the digital transition, the ecological transition, the modernisation of economies and therefore prosperity and growth. i would like to draw your attention, madam president, to a fundamental point. at the moment, the price of energy is unbearable for businesses and households, in electricity and fuel. we want an ambitious green transition, an ambitious digital transition, but it needs to be socially sustainable. families and businesses expect this from europeans and expect this from our plan. i thank the commission for its strength and courage.

the commission has already made a number of proposals. europe's green deal is without a doubt the biggest climate change programme ever put forward by an eu commission, indeed, an entire continent. but it is precisely after this summer of climate crisis that we need more speed and ambition. the central problem with europe's climate policy, however, is not a lack of announcements from brussels, but the huge deficit of willingness to act by the member states. when i look at germany, i see a huge gulf between german ambition and european ambition in climate policy. the federal government wants to push back the end of the internal combustion engine. it has not even implemented the eu's more citizen energy directive on renewables. and with nord stream 2, it's going a detour to more fossil gas. germany is slowing down europe's ambitions in climate policy. germany is slowing europe down to become the world's climate leader. and that is why we now need a german federal government for european climate protection, which will become a german climate government.

in writing. the eu's motto is united in diversity! indigenous national minorities living in the union are part of a diverse society. more than a million of them called on the eu to create a legal framework to protect the rights of national and linguistic minorities, to avoid double standards when acting in the interests of a minority, while national minorities are protected only by council of europe directives. it is outrageous that the commission has nevertheless swept aside the minority safepack citizens' initiative for superficial, made-up reasons! the eu is not setting a good example in this area for its candidate or associated countries, and is imposing double standards on them. while strongly opposing abuses against the lgbtqi community, citing partnership agreements, they ignore violations against national minorities, e.g. in the case of ukraine, which continues to restrict the rights of national minorities. the education law of 2017, the language law of 2019, the law on the indigenous peoples of ukraine are trapping the country's national minorities in the trap of forced assimilation. the last hope of ukrainian minorities is the national communities act. however, she fears that this will only be a step backwards as ukrainian lawmakers try to circumvent international commitments to national minorities by creating new concepts that defy international norms. i ask the commission to put the protection of the rights of national minorities on its agenda, both inside and outside the eu.

for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, with the pegasus spyware scandal, revealed once again by journalists and ngos, we are entering a new era of surveillance. surveillance that escapes the control of public authorities and becomes the work of private companies whose profit-seeking makes them accomplices of governments, of unscrupulous regimes. journalists, political opponents, human rights defenders, lawyers, politicians, including a former mep, have been targeted for espionage and repression. any one of us could have been on that list. the israeli company that markets the software presents it as a decisive tool, exclusively intended for the fight against terrorism and organized crime. it was probably diverted from its original surveillance purposes, in violation of fundamental rights, but also outside any legal framework. this parliament is currently working on the establishment of a regulatory framework for technologies that are intended to combat crime. here is a frightening example of the risks inherent in these instruments. the european union is also working with the states involved in the scandal through police and judicial cooperation. are we really prepared to enter into cooperation agreements with states that spy on us? we demand a moratorium on the export, sale, transfer and use of these surveillance technologies. we demand protection and reparation for the victims. responsibility must be established and the european response must be commensurate with the gravity of the facts.
on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, we are finally discussing this case in this parliament now, the pegasus scandal. what would be the difference, when a spyware can activate the camera and microphone on your phone and make new recordings, if someone just entered your house and watched what you do and say during your private time? what would be the difference if someone entered your office and attended all your confidential meetings, listening, watching and even making recordings? worldwide, the smartphones of politicians, lawyers, journalists and human rights activists have been hacked with software, and even european leaders – we have spoken about president macron here today. this is very scary. it’s not only dangerous for our personal lives, it’s also dangerous for our democracies and for freedom. we need to share the values that we have in europe across the world and build a global framework for data protection, a global framework for respecting privacy. i also believe that the way in which some companies are treating data should make us think of how we need to deal with them.

address. i have always been very clear that the commission will not hesitate to take all the necessary measures to ensure the implementation of european law and respect for the rule of law, and as justice commissioner i will do my utmost to defend the independence of the judiciary. the commission acted decisively as guardian of the treaties last week, in view of the developments in poland over the summer, and i am pleased that the council has taken the necessary steps to ensure that the as indicated in the introduction, last week the commission took two important decisions to ensure the implementation of the two july rulings of the court of justice. as the president of the commission said this morning in this parliament in her state of the union speech, our values are guaranteed by our legal order and safeguarded by the judgments of the european court of justice. these judgments are binding. we make sure that they are respected and we do so in every member state of our union. as regards poland, let me briefly recall that the commission is of the opinion that poland failed to take the necessary measures to comply fully with the two judgments that the european court of justice issued in july. in this respect, we also see developments on the ground which are particularly worrying. the actions of the polish authorities continue to undermine the independence of the polish justice system. last week, on 7 september, the commission therefore took two separate decisions to ensure the implementation of the rulings that the court of justice issued in july. i also need to underline that the commission's concerns regarding the polish authorities' repeated questioning of the primacy of eu law are present, and it was described in our introduction today. this concerns the decision of the polish constitutional tribunal of 14 july, which found orders of the court of justice of interim measures in the area of justice to be ultra vires. i addressed this issue in the letter that i sent to the polish authorities in july. i had also written to the polish authorities on the motion of the polish prime minister to the constitutional tribunal, in which the principle of the primacy of eu law has been questioned. other motions of this kind are also pending before the constitutional tribunal. in view of these developments, i regret to conclude that the situation of the rule of law in poland continues to be a source of serious concern. i can only reiterate, as i have made clear several times before you, that eu law has primacy over national law and that all decisions of the european court of justice, including orders for interim measures, are binding on all member state authorities and national courts, including constitutional courts. in that regard, i also recall that the concerns regarding the legitimacy and independence of the polish constitutional tribunal, as set out in the reasoned proposal of the commission triggering the article 7 procedure, have still not been resolved. ladies and gentlemen, the commission remains committed to using all the instruments at its disposal to defend the rule of law and to respond swiftly and effectively to any breach of that rule of law. you can be assured of my personal commitment to this and you heard this morning the firm commitment of the president of the commission and the commission as a whole to this. for my part, i will of course go to poland to discuss with the polish parliament and civil society the situation of the rule of law in poland. some of you have raised the question of budgetary conditionality. i would like to point out that the president of the commission announced this morning that the first written communications will be sent in the coming weeks. some of you also touched on the recovery plans. in general, i would like to emphasize what the president of the commission said this morning, namely that each of these plans is evaluated on the basis of the country-specific recommendations of the european semester. these country-specific recommendations, where relevant in relation to the independence of the judiciary, are taken into account by the commission in its assessment. i would like to remind you again that the commission remains fully committed to interinstitutional dialogue on the rule of law and i remain of course at the disposal of your parliament to continue our regular exchanges on the subject. we have already exchanged views on the first report on the rule of law in the union. we are now discussing the second report, which the commission adopted on 20 july. and as you heard this morning, the annual report next year will take up one of your parliament's requests and will include country-specific recommendations in a very explicit way. i really believe that through all these actions we are taking all the steps necessary to ensure that the rule of law is respected, in poland as elsewhere in the european union. i would like to repeat that it is legitimate for a member state to disagree with the european commission. but once the court of justice has ruled, it is a binding decision which must be enforced by all the authorities in all the member states.

in writing. for the first time in this institution, the european left and liberals are attacking poland in a scandalous way. this time it is about the media law and the polish government's questions to the constitutional court on the supremacy of the constitution over eu law. these actions of the polish authorities are in full accordance with the democratic mandate won in the elections. therefore, the attack by the eu institutions on poland is a purely political attack and not a legal dispute. we cannot accept that the commission and parliament are applying double standards. in particular, the polish government has proposed new radio and television rules that would prohibit entities from outside the european economic area (eea) from holding more than 49% of the shares of polish media companies. similar decisions are used in other eu countries, such as denmark, which recently adopted similar legislation. the same can be said of the legal primacy of national constitutions over eu law. poland has the right to refer such a question to its constitutional court. other member states followed suit. in 2005 and 2020, the federal constitutional court of germany recognised the supremacy of the german constitution over eu law, but no one here took any special measures for this reason. the actions against poland are in breach of the treaties, including article 5 teu, and the european union is exceeding its competences.
the next item is the debate on the report by malin björk and diana riba i giner, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs and the committee on women's rights and gender equality, on the definition of gender-based violence as a new area of crime under article 83 (1) of the treaty on european union (2021/2035 (inl)) (a9-0249/2021).
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. colleagues on the ground and you who follow from afar! one in three women in the eu has been subject to physical or sexual violence, and an average of 50 women die every week as a result of gender-based violence. gender-based violence restricts our space, reduces our lives and takes lives. violence and threats of violence constrain life in homes, in public spaces, in schools, on streets, in squares, in social media. it is a structural problem, and it is one of the most serious human rights violations we have to face. this house, the european parliament, has been a driving force in every step the eu has taken to combat gender-based violence. so is this. this time, together with my co-rapporteur, diana riba i giner, who will speak after me, i have had the opportunity to hold the pen and negotiate another strong position and concrete demands on how to combat gender-based violence. we are demanding european legislation. it is not the first time this parliament has done so, but we will do it again. anything else is not sustainable. but we also say that a criminal law and justice system is only part of the equation. we affirm that the fight against gender-based violence cannot be seen separately from the fight for gender equality and the work for women's rights, in all sectors of society. gender-based violence is intimately linked to patriarchal structures, inequality between women and men, lack of economic independence for women and lack of justice. our report states that to combat gender-based violence we must have the right to decide about our bodies and our sexuality. we need access to free abortion and contraception. our report also states that we must ensure that all our actions are aimed at strengthening women's rights and taking control of our lives. we need to get the right support and service. we affirm that the women's journeys, which are a pillar in the fight against violence against women and girls, should be given permanent support. we say that the justice system needs to be so much better that the justice system is delivering convictions so that we slowly but surely, but everywhere and in all parts, actually put the blame where it belongs: on the perpetrators. we must therefore have legislation based on consent. we also state in our report that gender-based violence affects lgbtq people and that this must be included in the fight against gender-based violence. we are clearly saying that the threats and hatred and violence against lgbtq people also stem from patriarchal and sexist notions oppressive notions of how we must be as human beings and where those who violate heteronormative demands are threatened and subjected to violence. we say that this violence can and must be fought as part of the framework of gender-based violence. one would think that everyone is on board, that no one is against this, that everyone wants to take action and fight this violence. but there are those who are not on the train, who still want to control women's lives and bodies, who think it's okay to limit our lives in this way. sometimes they give other reasons, but basically they don't think it's important enough. but they won't defeat us. every step forward has been a struggle. feminists, activists, organizations, survivors themselves are the ones who have been pushing, along with parliamentarians, allies in parliament, to tighten laws. so today. i would like to thank all the tireless feminists who have worked with us, who have the eu is doing more and better, that the member states are doing more and better, because we are saying stop to gender-based violence.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members of the european parliament, thank you for your work on this report. in line with the president’s political guidelines, we committed ourselves to launching a strategy for gender equality in the eu that would include the fight against gender-based violence, as it is a widespread violation of fundamental rights and must be addressed on a common basis at eu level. the commission stands by its commitments. in the past two years, together with my fellow commissioners and under the lead of president von der leyen, we adopted several union of equality strategies, including the eu gender equality strategy. in 2016, the commission proposed the eu’s accession to the istanbul convention and signed the convention in june 2017. since then, however, the accession remains blocked in council. this is why, as the president stated this morning, we are presenting legislative measures to achieve the objectives of the istanbul convention within the limits of the eu competences. we will propose concrete measures by the end of this year. they will be composed of two initiatives. the first initiative is a broad directive on gender-based violence and domestic violence, with a focus on prevention, protection, support and access to justice. it will include the criminalisation of specific forms of gender-based violence and domestic violence on the basis of the existing euro-crimes. the second initiative aims to trigger a council decision to extend the list of euro-crimes to include hate crime and hate speech. of course, the council will then need to decide by unanimity. if this is achieved, the commission would be able to propose an eu-level criminalisation of hate crime on grounds of sexual orientation, amongst other grounds. with these two initiatives, that will be adopted as a package by the end of this year, and we will achieve to a large extent the objectives pursued by parliament. concretely, the directive on gender-based violence will cover targeted criminalisation of specific conduct such as, for instance, serious forms of online violence. it will therefore fill the most important gaps in member states’ legislation and ensure that the most serious forms of gender-based violence are criminalised in the eu. the proposal will include specific measures on prevention of violence, protection and support of victims, including, of course, the most vulnerable victims. it will address the intersection of sex with other grounds of anti-discrimination included in the treaties. in addition to that, the extension of the list of euro-crimes will provide a new legal basis for proposing additional criminalisation. once this new legal basis is established, we will be able to continue filling the gaps in the future in relation to criminalisation of misogynistic hate crime and hate speech. this package will deliver on your requests. it will allow for swift action that should be adopted within the current mandate period. let us not keep the victims waiting.
the european parliament has already expressed its concern about the situation in the country, and the commission has already taken action to combat it. one out of three. just because they're women. the annual cost of gender-related violence is estimated at 290 billion euros. behind all those numbers are stories and broken or stolen lives of women, mothers, daughters and friends. fellas, i want to be crystal clear. gender-based violence should be added to the list of criminal offences in article 83 tfeu. we therefore support the main objective of this report and consider the fight against gender-based violence as one of our top priorities. and yes, we support the directive which transposes the international standards of the istanbul convention. today, commission president von der leyen made it very clear that the commission will be proposing a law to combat violence against women by the end of the year. we shall vote in favour of the commission's proposal. however, this parliamentary report goes beyond national competence and undermines our common objective. commissioner, you yourself said that we must take account of powers in this matter. the fact is that we need unanimity in the council to add gender-based violence to article 83. it will not come if we simultaneously demand that the refusal of abortion be labeled as gender-based violence. that is why we have amended the text, despite our strong support for the political objective itself. so yes, we will respond positively to the commission's proposal and to the addition of gender-based violence to article 83 of the treaty. but this is our position.
mr president, first of all, i would like to say that i am really glad to see how much political support this subject got in this room. at the same time, i am very disappointed that despite this broad horizontal consensus, the commission continues to act behind closed doors and ignores all our calls for full transparency when it comes to vaccines. this is a legitimate call and, in this limited time, i will only focus on two points. first, the commission invested a huge amount of eu money to fund research, and the development and acquisition of these vaccines. eu money is taxpayers’ money. eu money is citizens’ money. we cannot accept that, at a critical and unprecedented time as during a pandemic, the european commission decides to deliberately ignore the right of citizens to have access to information. transparency is a key principle, and it is key because it reinforces public scrutiny and trust over what the institutions are doing. in this particular case, all this opacity, all this secrecy, not only undermines the trust of our citizens, but it also leads to uncertainty, disinformation and, even more seriously, to the risk of vaccine hesitancy. is the commission considering this aspect? mr president, the vaccine contracts are being kept a terrible secret. however, we have been able to lift a tip of the veil and understand that pfizer and moderna are raising their prices by 20 and 13 percent respectively. so we are paying more for the third dose than the first, while the cost of production of these companies is actually falling. the production capacity of the companies has been largely built up with public money, but we are still paying. how the hell did this get negotiated? the companies involved are already making hundreds of millions in profits, but we're paying more as their costs fall. this doesn't make any sense. you used public money to fund, buy and purchase the vaccines, among other things. you've transferred the investment risk to us, but you've asked nothing in return from the big pharmaceutical companies. you basically said: you set the price and the quantity of production and we'll pay. this is financial mismanagement and an attack on our social security. and many countries cannot afford to do so, so they are without vaccines. as a result, new variants are emerging and the pandemic continues to creep in. the british medical journal called it a crime against humanity. you're an accessory to that. stop this, share the vaccine technology and cancel the patents.
mr president, my colleague michèle rivasi's excellent report points out the inconsistencies in our european policies and their decisive impact on our partners in the south. our development policies, whether they are climate, agriculture or trade, must have one single objective: the achievement of the sustainable development goals. however, the european commission does not sufficiently integrate the impact of trade agreements on biodiversity when negotiating with countries such as indonesia, brazil and others. it is worth mentioning that the amazon is home to exceptional flora and fauna, that it now emits more co2 than it stores, and that it is home to endangered indigenous peoples. the consequences of the eu's trade policy - co2 emissions, increased agro-industrial practices, deforestation, land grabbing and pesticide use - are devastating for biodiversity and local communities. europe must therefore put its trade at the service of sustainable development and the well-being of its people, not the other way around.
the next item is the report by mrs elena kountoura, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, on the eu road safety policy framework for the period 2021-2030 recommendations for the next steps towards vision zero road traffic costs (2021/2014(ini)) (a9-0211/2021).
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, on behalf of commissioner vălean, who was unable to be here this evening, the commission very much welcomes this important report on road safety, and would like to thank ms kountoura and the committee on transport and tourism for their continued strong support for our work on road safety. while the number of road deaths fell by an unprecedented 17% in 2020, this was due largely to lower traffic volumes as a result of the pandemic. the figure of 19 000 fatalities across the eu remains unacceptably high. in the sustainable and smart mobility strategy, the commission reiterated its commitment to implementing the eu road safety policy framework and bringing down to close to zero the death toll for all modes of transport. however, i want to stress that the vision zero is a joint responsibility. success will depend on all of us playing our part from european, national and regional and local authorities, to police and health services, from all road users, employers to bike share operators, to car makers. we all have an interest in making our roads safer in partnership. i can assure you the commission is working very closely with the member states to ensure an effective implementation of the safe-system approach throughout europe. ms kountoura’s report includes a number of constructive proposals and recommendations to which the commission will give due consideration. i shall highlight a few of these issues. the commission fully agrees that road safety is an integral part of sustainable and smart mobility. there can be no sustainability without safety. as we build transport infrastructure, we need it to be both green and safe. when we improve the infrastructure for cyclists and pedestrians for instance, we both encourage reduction of our carbon footprint, healthy active mobility and we make it safer. indeed, as the eu becomes more urban, road safety is becoming increasingly an urban question, and vulnerable road users, pedestrians, cyclists and motorcyclists now account for 70% of traffic fatalities in our cities. much of our current focus is on preparing a new legislative package, including revisions of the driving licence directive and the directive on cross-border enforcement of traffic rules. we will also update the rules on roadworthiness where we will be looking both at emissions and safety elements, as well as odometer fraud. and we are also looking closely into factors such as speed and alcohol consumption and reflecting on possible eu recommendations to address them. more specifically on speed, we take note of the parliament’s position and the strong engagement of stakeholders at global and local level. we are looking very closely into this issue, in particular assessing the gains for road safety in those european cities which set a maximum speed of 30 km per hour in residential areas. we are also working closely with our partners to ensure that eu funding can be targeted towards road safety projects. of course, better infrastructure is a key factor in improving road safety. we have encouraged investment in road safety upgrades as part of the recovery and resilience facility. european investment bank loans, structural and cohesion funds and the connecting europe facility also provide additional avenues to incentivise actions in this field. the commission is also grateful to the european parliament for its support for the european road safety exchange, which is a twinning project bringing safety professionals from different eu member states together to help address specific road safety problems. the feedback today has been very positive. we are making good progress on establishing a first set of key road safety performance indicators based on a common methodology, and we expect the first set of results next year. we look forward to engaging with the member states and the parliament to help shape future work. we have also relaunched an important european road safety tool, the european road safety charter. this is a huge network of over 3 000 people, organisations, civil society and companies with an interest in road safety. it offers a perfect opportunity to share and reward good practice and to learn from each other. the 2021 eu road safety awards ceremony will take place on 18 november, and parliament is invited to help us make this event a real success. the commission fully shares the need for eu road safety policy to be adaptable to change, in particular with regard to the development of automated vehicles. an eu partnership under horizon europe on connected, cooperative and automated mobility has been set up to promote and facilitate large-scale testing in this field. our goal is to enable the safe introduction of automation on eu roads. the work of the partnership will go hand-in-hand with policy and legislative updates needed to take full advantage of these developments for safer, more sustainable and inclusive mobility. the commission takes note of your proposals for staging a european year of road safety and the creation of a new agency for road transport. we recognise the potential benefits of both, but also need to consider the costs, of course. we will continue to reflect on these options and report back to you on progress achieved. once again, i thank the rapporteur, ms kountoura, and the shadows for preparing this important report, and the commission looks forward to its adoption in plenary later this week.
