giants. when we vote on the digital markets act, the dma, later today, we will be putting our foot down in front of the biggest tech gatekeepers who have been able to dictate the rules of our digital single market for far too long. we in the european parliament want this to stop now. the rules must be set democratically. together with the digital services act, the dsa, we are setting the framework for a fairer digital sphere for the benefit of users, consumers and businesses alike. we are now ensuring that the business model of the biggest tech giants is more beneficial to users, and we are creating the framework for more free and fair competition so that new ones can also emerge. we are introducing interoperability requirements. i think this is an important tool in the fight against the monopoly of platforms. and we're sending a clear signal that the major platforms need to rethink their business model, which is based on harvesting and selling our personal data. with dma, targeted advertising to minors is now illegal for the biggest gatekeepers. it's an important step in the fight to ensure that we stop the extreme harvesting of our data by the big platforms. finally, i am also very pleased that a ban on so-called dark patterns has been included in the dma. now it's going to be forbidden for the biggest companies to influence our decisions by designing their interfaces in a way that unconsciously makes us act. we have to stop this. so, all in all, i think the dma is an important step towards us consumers getting back control of our personal data and ensuring a much better and more well-functioning internal market. let me end by saying a big thank you to my colleague evelyne gebhardt for her great work on this issue, but also for her 28 years of service to europe. i think that deserves a big thank you. thank you for that and thank you for the great effort with the dma, also to mr schwab.
madam president, the companies covered by this law are companies so big that they have annual profits bigger than the gdp of some of our member states. being so big is not in itself a negative. they are big because people like them and many of our citizens look forward to their next release. with this size, however, must come responsibility. these companies should act in such a way that they serve the needs of the greater society, instead of what is good for their shareholders alone. i regret, that despite their best efforts, these efforts have not been enough. so here we are today, doing by law what these companies should have done on their own. commissioner, i still think that any start—up can someday take down one of the gatekeepers. i still think that consumers will vote with their wallets and their eyes, and new alternatives will come, and one of the giants will fall. but we must give consumers and start—ups a chance and a choice. and today we do so.
madam president, we are well aware of the importance of regulating digital markets in an era of digital integration. we also know that we are always chasing after losses, and that is why this proposal is an important proposal, although not sufficient. it's important to fight the monopoly of the five platforms that control the digital markets. it's also important to combat the control these digital platforms have over our data. in fact, this data is from citizens and citizens and that's not how it's been happening. but more important than that would be to have eliminated advertising targeted to all people. this is a key element in market control and unfortunately in this proposal targeted advertising is only eliminated for minors. anyway, it's a first step, as i said. a first step that helps us in such important measures as interoperability. but we must do more and i hope that the ambition of this proposal will not be reduced further.
madam president, this digital markets act is very good news for europe and for the world. at long last, we’re making progress to bring the massive tech players that shape our lives under some democratic oversight. the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco) is proposing many good amendments, but let me stress one that other colleagues have already mentioned today, the one demanding interoperability. this relates to something that i have very much at my heart because those of us that are subject to political persecution and espionage must choose our providers carefully to protect our privacy, but this often means that we will be cut off from our friends and family. interoperability, if it becomes a reality, should put an end to that, and i hope it will make life easier for dissidents, democracy fighters and activists across the eu. it will certainly help me. speaking of activists, i cannot miss this chance to remember julian assange after the recent bad news. he has my full support and i call again for his immediate release. i urge all european democrats to do the same.
madam president, we are all becoming more aware of the importance of the digital world. that's why i'm glad we're here today to talk about the digital markets act. it sets the rules ex ante and therefore makes the necessary rules transparent. big companies have taken over the online space, and we often see this position being abused and market distortions. we need to protect fair competition, and what's illegal in the offline space has to be illegal online. if companies have access to user information, they must also be responsible for its content. advertising on the internet must be transparent, it must be clear who is the client and who is the promoter. the data collected must be better protected and everyone must have the right to request their complete erasure or to refuse to provide them. the digital markets act also modernises and clearly defines competition. it is essential that fair competition is maintained and that small firms in particular have access to information, but also the possibility of targeting their customers. today we are proposing a regulation that will not only create rules for the big players, but will also protect users of digital platforms and not burden small businesses. thank you very much, andreas, for such a proposal and for your great commitment. ladies and gentlemen, by adopting this proposal, we are establishing standards that have the potential to become global standards for the benefit of citizens.
madam president, first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteurs on the excellent work they have done on this great and important document. for too long, the internet has grown and changed without any control. in the more than 20 years since the last regulation, the internet has changed dramatically, occupying our daily lives, becoming both a necessity and a pastime. it has become both a source of great profit for some and a great threat to others. as much as 98.5% of facebook's revenue and 83.3% of google's revenue is generated by advertising. citizens are often unaware of how big platforms are profiting from our data, moods, health conditions, interests and so on. if we were being followed on the street, it would be illegal, but on the internet, anything goes. i welcome the ban on the tracking of minors online for advertising purposes, and the easier control of advertising for adults. the commission will also be able to use the existing data to provide a more accurate picture of the situation in the member states. in light of the recent judgment against google, which seems to be a drop in the bucket, it is very important to ensure market transparency. everyone must have the same initial incentive to succeed, small and large. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and to monitor the implementation of the new rules.
mr. chairman, facebook, google, amazon and apple and other tech companies have simply become too big and too powerful to be healthy. that is because they are monopolists for whom there is no competition. it allows them to accumulate unlimited capital, allowing them to concentrate unchecked power, allowing them to manipulate both the market and our democracy. they are companies that want to do business everywhere, but nowhere to pay fair taxes. they're companies that violate the privacy of our citizens. they're companies that want to dictate what our culture and identity should look like. they are companies that benefit from the hyper-flexible global economy, where there will always be someone forced to sell their labour for ever-less pay and ever-less social protection. those who disagree with this parasitic, globalist model, who censor and block them from their platforms, because they don't want to be contradicted, because they don't want their interests to be harmed. these soulless multinational companies are owned by an oligarchy of anonymous shareholders who know no culture, identity or nationality and are accountable to no one. they want to take over the authority of states and exchange cultures. they want to transform free and sovereign citizens into submissive, rightsless, shapeless and interchangeable consumers. the arrogance of these multinational tech companies who think they can get away with anything must come to an end. the rights of citizens, consumers and small businesses are guaranteed by the democratic rule of law and the sovereign will of the people, not by the shareholders and the user agreement. that is why i think the digital markets act is a good step in the right direction.
mr. chairman, i support the negotiating mandate for the digital markets act. the omnipotence of big tech is currently preventing fair competition in our internal market, depriving our smes of growth opportunities or even making them disappear altogether, and this is at the expense of our innovative strength. it is clear that the current competition rules are not sufficient to create a level playing field in our digital single market. that is why our regulation must indeed become more proactive, taking into account the digital speed. but we must not allow our regulations to become protectionist. the us is and will remain an important partner in jointly setting global digital standards in the face of china's advancing. we must not undermine the functioning of the trade and technology council.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, humanitarian action is faced with three major dangers. first, the constant threat of aid being privatised. we will not stop saying that humanitarian action cannot be a business opportunity, nor can the crisis become a pretext for deepening the doctrines of shock. if crises like the pandemic we are currently experiencing increase humanitarian needs, the solution is to free patents and not to put pharmaceutical multinationals at the global governance table. secondly, the continuing geopolitical exploitation of humanitarian crises in pursuit of interests that have nothing to do with humanitarian objectives. a good example of this is the impunity for humanitarian crises such as those in the occupied sahara or palestine, or hypocritical calls for increased humanitarian aid in the mediterranean while at the same time strengthening the mechanisms of fortress europe, which, in the end, generate new humanitarian crises such as those being seen in libya. the third threat has to do with the criminalization of humanitarian actors, something we see on a daily basis in the mediterranean. saving lives is not a crime. practicing racist policies and buying into the xenophobic agenda of the far right should be. i hope that this good report will help to combat these three traps of privatization, instrumentalization and criminalization of european humanitarian aid.
mr president it is gratifying to note that the european union and its member states together provide 36 percent of global humanitarian assistance. this leadership must be maintained and made even more effective in the future so that aid really reaches those in need. humanitarian emergencies are linked to migration, as the increased frequency and intensity of climate-related disasters fuels conflict and forces more and more people to leave their homes permanently. however, the rapporteur was right not to make the mistake of urging the creation of corridors for migrants for this reason. the report thus focuses on real problems and real needs. it is our firm and consistent principle that help should be brought to the place of need. the results support this approach. with our humanitarian support through the hungary helps programme, we have enabled half a million people to stay in their homeland or return to their homes.
mr. chairman, more people than ever are in need of humanitarian aid. ladies and gentlemen, according to the report, as i believe you have seen, there are two hundred and thirty-eight million people in need of assistance because of conflict, a number of factors such as natural disasters, limited resources. the fact that the european union is affected by a pandemic and that we are in a crisis of access to energy must not result in the humanitarian field being neglected. the european commission's communication 'humanitarian action by the european union: new challenges, the same principles' is key to defining new directions for the union's humanitarian action. i therefore support the measures it outlines, such as increased eu support for local aid providers or the extension of the funding mechanism with humanitarian partners. humanitarian aid is an integral component of european policy and on it we build our identity and the distinctiveness of our approach to countries in need without calculating or conditioning aid on economic dependence. what remains an ongoing challenge is how to ensure that humanitarian aid actually reaches those who need it most. this is particularly important in countries governed by authoritarian non-democratic regimes and countries facing internal unrest and conflict. we must also be prepared for the fact that humanitarian needs will increase further and that they will also pose a security challenge to which we will have to find a common response. ladies and gentlemen, humanity is the civilized achievement. her concept hasn't changed. we must therefore focus all our energies on the dignity of every human being and humanity has shown in the past year and a little bit that it is something that has no price and no alternative.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, everyone agrees that there are growing needs in the financing of humanitarian aid. as far as reducing the financing deficit is concerned, let us stop being hypocritical. the lack of financial resources has one or more names. name each member state that has pledged since the beginning of time to devote at least 0.7% of its gross national income to official development assistance. at the addis ababa conference six years ago, the commission was authorised to renew, on behalf of all the member states of the european union, the commitment to devote 0.7% of their gross national income to official development assistance. two member states had the audacity to announce a substantial reduction in their official development assistance shortly after the conference. is this policy coherence? i am addressing you - the commission - to urge you to confront each member state with its commitments. the opportunity to do so will present itself as early as 2022 at the first humanitarian aid forum. there is no point in lamenting the plight of the victims of humanitarian crises if we are not prepared to devote the necessary resources, which have been promised many times since then. i would not like to end without thanking our colleague norbert neuser. with his departure, the development committee of our parliament loses one of its best talents. we're losing a pillar of the community. i wish norbert neuser to have good memories of us, and he can count on our sincere reciprocity.

mr president, never in history have so many people in the third world been in dire need, namely a quarter of a billion. causes are wars, corrupt regimes, food and water shortages and, of course, the population explosion. every year the population of the underdeveloped countries increases by 80 million. that is an untenable situation. and what are we gonna do about it? we import their surplus population, with all the problems it causes our own population, and we dump billions into bottomless wells. the eu is already the largest donor in the world, but according to all the other groups, that is apparently not enough. of course, we must help people in times of sudden natural disasters and other calamities. but the causes of chronic problems must be addressed. donating billions more and more and bringing millions of refugees here is not the solution. in the end, our social security system is also in danger. to put it in a metaphor: our lifeboat will sink too if they take more and more and more people on board. it can and must be done differently: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the world's population is threatened to rise from 7.5 to 11 billion people this century. it is insane to think that we europeans can be responsible for the additional billions of hungry people in the world.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, this morning we have been speaking about the battle we are waging against covid—19. mr weber described it as a war, and indeed it’s a battle for human lives, it’s a battle for a return to life as we knew it before pandemia, and it’s a battle for minimising the consequences of covid—19 on jobs and the economy. mr mureşan and mr séjourné told us that the best response to these unprecedented challenges was european unity. i agree with them because, thanks to this european unity, we secured enough vaccines for europeans to already have managed to fully vaccinate 66% of our fellow citizens and we became, in a short period of time, the biggest manufacturer of vaccines. i would underline – which we never should forget – that we are also the biggest exporter of vaccines in the world. we have exported more than 1.4 billion doses so far, and this is more than anybody else. we know that other major economies have simply been closed for exporting to anyone with the exception of their own citizens. i think that we should be really proud of that because we supply to the world more than we supply to ourselves. the same goes for eu donations. we have donated so far 377 million doses through covax and 50 million additional doses bilaterally through the team europe effort, especially to countries of the western balkans and the eastern partnership. so i would say that it’s again the european union which delivers when others, very often with huge public relations noises, are just promising. i recognise that for many members of the european parliament, as was very well presented by ms iratxe garcía pérez, the issue of patents and the negotiations in the wto is very important. so here let me underline that it’s actually the commission which is engaging the most in these discussions. what we are trying to do is to find a solution which waives certain obligations in the trips agreement without the consent of the patent owners and that will have an effect on the ground. we advocate a solution that would clarify or waive certain obligations on compulsory licensing, to allow wto members to authorise a company to manufacture and export covid—19 pharmaceutical products in a fast and simplified manner. this would also ensure that countries with no, or insufficient, manufacturing capacity do not face any obstacles or legal uncertainty when they import these products. the details are part of the negotiating process, but the main principles are maximum simplification, speed and no red tape. in addition, a number of clarifications, including on supporting manufacturers that supply low and medium—income countries at affordable prices, would provide the needed legal certainty because the full waiving of patent and other rights under the trips agreement would not in itself help reach the objective of the widest and most timely distribution of covid—19 vaccines. on the contrary, we need measures that preserve the ongoing, unprecedented collaborations between pharmaceutical companies that yield record supplies of vaccines, as well as incentives to innovate and invest in research on covid—19 and its variants. all these elements must go hand in hand if we are to support the increase in production facilities in regions such as africa. production will not increase if there is no transfer of know—how and investment. for that to happen, we need the intellectual property framework which secures the rights of innovators. furthermore, we promote a comprehensive approach where intellectual property is just part of the solution. as the president has already outlined, we are also preparing, ahead of the eu—africa summit, the special support package to africa, not just doses of vaccines but also medical equipment and know—how. we also work, as team europe, with our partners in senegal, rwanda, south africa and the private sector to construct mrna vaccine production sites. many honourable members want us to help africa as much as we can, and we acknowledge this. we said from the beginning that we will not be safe until the whole world is properly vaccinated. therefore you will see that there will be lots of preparation invested in the eu—africa summit, which we want to have as a big success and a milestone in our mutual relations and collaboration. ms cavazzini referred to the excellent work of the scientists from south africa. i really want to officially appreciate their work, thank them for their transparency and thank them for their openness. you will agree with me that, at the moment when the news about this new variant first broke, we needed to act very quickly to assess the risk. but we are also acting very quickly now because what we are now proposing is that non—essential travel from this country could resume with vaccinations and a negative pcr certificate. so we want to make sure that this issue will be settled as quickly as possible. once again, huge appreciation to south african scientists for the work and openness. mr buşoi referred to the fact that vaccines are our main preventive tool to fight the pandemic but, even with vaccines and with this new variant, omicron, what we also need is good cooperation and progress in therapeutics. i think this is becoming even more important and urgent right now, because if people fall ill they need to be properly treated. we presented our strategy on covid therapeutics last may, but what is very important for you to know is that we should have six therapeutics authorised by the end of the year. four are authorised already and we expect that two will come through the approval process before the end of the year. what we are doing right now is supporting our member states to have access to these therapeutics, and we have already concluded two joint procurement contracts for the purchase of monoclonal antibodies. on 13 december, we launched the joint procurement for two more to be concluded early in 2022. if you allow, i will conclude with a couple of words on russia, ukraine, energy and the green deal because many honourable members referred to their concerns over the massive build—up of a military presence along the eastern ukrainian border. i would like to reaffirm once again our unwavering commitment to ukraine’s sovereignty and territorial integrity. on top of our close political cooperation, we have so far supported ukraine, as you know, with more than eur 40 billion since 2014. we are also working in other sectors, and i would say that we are collaborating very closely in all areas, from energy to critical raw materials. we are supporting ukraine with structural reforms, with modernising the administration, and we have also been faithful to the task given to us by the european council when they asked us to come forward with options if the situation were to become more dramatic and if there were to be a danger of further escalation or further aggression against ukraine. we have the options ready, we are prepared to act and we will present the options to the european council tomorrow. but we do of course, also this time, appeal for peaceful resolution, for de—escalation and for using diplomatic channels to work on a solution. if you allow me, i will close on the issue of energy and our green policies because this was very much present in the interventions by mr lamberts, mr eickhout, mr geier and ms spyraki. i think that we demonstrated very clearly throughout this year, with our green deal package, with fit for 55, with our clear leadership in cop 26 in glasgow, but also in our package which we adopted just yesterday, that we are very clearly the global leader when it comes to tackling climate change. we are the only major economy which translates our political aspirations into law and concrete measures. once we come and present to you in greater detail the package of further measures which we adopted yesterday and which tackles the issues of the hydrogen industry, energy security, and the modernisation of our transport, getting ten—t infrastructure ready for smart mobility, that will be further testimony in that regard. the issue of energy prices is, of course, very high on our agenda and we are working with our member states to make sure that they will deploy fully the toolbox which we offered to them to tackle energy poverty, help the most vulnerable and make sure that all these opportunities are used to the maximum. to conclude, i would like to thank my colleague, mr logar, for the excellent work of the slovenian presidency, and i think we can all symbolically welcome the new presidency of france here in the city of strasbourg.

the latest covid variant omicron has sent the stock prices of pfizer and moderna into a deep dive. pfizer saw its share close up 6.1 percent, about $17.5 billion in additional market value. competitor moderna even saw its shares rise by 21 percent. a virus variant as a business opportunity. that's very cynical. after all, it's precisely the patents of big pharma that make new variants more likely. patents shield vaccine technology from other countries and companies. this limits vaccine production and increases global shortages at the expense of global health. in poor countries, barely three percent of the population is fully vaccinated. these vaccine shortages facilitate viral circulation and provide breeding grounds for new virus variants. the omicron variant shows how urgently this needs to change. an open source approach can allow researchers around the world to work on adapting the vaccine to new variants. this would allow unused production capacity to be activated and new production units to be created within a few months. never has the need to abolish patents been more urgent. this parliament has already voted for it three times. what are you waiting for? are the profits of big pharma really that much more important to you than human lives? basta lockdowns. i'm not going to do it. stop the pandemic. share the technology. saving lives.

president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i am a christian democrat and, more than that, i am an ideological heir to the christian workers' movement, and i am proud of that. it is encyclicals such as rerum novarum that give me the courage to go against the tide on a regular basis, and sometimes even give my group leader a little gray hair. that is why i am asking to speak here, because it would be a historical mistake to leave the defence of christianity to the extreme right alone. i support the commission's intention to make this union more inclusive, so that everyone is valued and recognised, regardless of religion or belief. and the criticism of the internal guide was partly justified, it is good that it was withdrawn, but it also shows leadership that one dares to do this. the path to inclusion is not through the use of compulsory language, but through the use of language that binds, where there is room for differences, where everyone can be themselves, including christians. this is exactly what our founding fathers had in mind with our european project: connected in diversity. and we are for religious freedom. let us not be silent about the holidays of the faith communities, but let people respect their traditions and above all let us look ahead. i'm going to end, madam president, with what really makes our society more inclusive, because too many people are still excluded or structurally discriminated against. we'll work on this together. above all, let us spread the peace, tolerance and unity that christmas represents.
i would like to thank everyone for this initiative. i got a little story here: in 2013, as a young politician, i was interviewed for the first time for the flemish newspaper de standaard. there was a little picture of me on the front page. "assita, who did you blow for this?" a young man asked me at a party in brussels. i didn't answer, i was so dumb. the worst part, however, was not what he said but those who laughed along, namely, other women. years later, i got a call apologizing for what i did. i'm sorry about that. i was prejudiced, but you have proven who you are and i have a lot of respect for you now. do women have to prove themselves to be respected? i am glad that this text is on the table. however, this text regards members of parliament primarily as perpetrators and staff primarily as victims. but let me tell you something: this is not always true. everywhere you look, you find perpetrators, and everywhere you look, you find victims. members of parliament against members of parliament, staff against staff, influential staff against young members of parliament. this has to be for everyone; stereotypes exist at all levels. it is a question of power, but also of perceived power, as mrs regner also said to me here. so this should be an education for everyone. it doesn't hurt you to be informed, but it does hurt to experience sexism. nor should this parliament use or abuse it to replace the judiciary. i shall vote for the text. i am in favour of the principle, but i think that the implementation needs to be clarified. i also make a call to all women: whatever happens, keep your ambition, show your ambition! you will always be over-scrutinised, but don't give up.
on behalf of the group of the european right mr president, i remember very well the great problems with the different product standards in the different member states. there was always a screw, a bolt, a nut that collided with another standard from another member state, making it impossible or very difficult for products to cross the border. fortunately, the single market put an end to this. the single market is a boon to our upward social convergence, to internal free trade, to jobs and to prosperity. but today the internal borders are being closed again for a number of goods. more and more shops are selling only products manufactured in their own country, with large labels produit de or made in. i know of producers who were forced to move their production to another member state, even though they had sufficient production capacity in their own country. of course, the short chain is a good thing, but what i am denouncing is pure protectionism. this is destroying our single market. i have already repeatedly pointed out to the commission this de facto protectionism and i ask the commission again to draw up a report on these de facto obstacles and to put the deepening of the internal market at the top of its agenda.
